# OBSERVATION
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

...and others, in which it was possible to observe even the handles themselves. We collected these and, for the sake of antiquity, brought them back with us and preserved them. From this we conclude that these Urns were made of sand and small pebbles soaked in mud and fired. What we now assert has been confirmed by sight: these Urns were not formed on a wheel, like the pots we commonly use today, but were born under the hands of the potter. They say the farmers of that village had already carried away several vessels of various sizes before, and—as is believable—enticed by the hope of some gain, they did not stop digging, whether they hoped to happen upon gold or rings. (For the historian Golzius <note>Hubert Goltzius, a 16th-century numismatist and antiquarian</note> is witness that the ancients were accustomed to bury certain coins and rings—sometimes made of bone or clay, sometimes of bronze, gold, or silver—in the tombs along with the ashes.) Their efforts were in vain. For the outcome did not match their hopes, since nothing appeared besides ashes and bones.

I observe from these things (1) that it is not inconsistent with the truth that the cremation of corpses was a frequent practice among the Gauls, the Germans, and the peoples further north, as recorded by *Caesar in Book 6 of the Gallic Wars, Eustathius on the Iliad Book 1, Tacitus in 'Germania' section 27, Alexander ab Alexandro in 'Genial Days' Book 3, page 130, Schedius on the German Gods page 453, Philipp Clüver in 'Ancient Germany' Book 1, chapter 53,* and others.

And just as (2) the author *Dio Cassius* records (*Book 48, on Public Offices*) that in the times of *Augustus*, during the consulship of *Appius Claudius and Caius Norbanus* <note>The year 38 BCE</note> (that is, in the year 716 since the founding of Rome), an edict was issued stating that the dead should not be cremated within two thousand paces of the city on account of the danger of fire (as *Cicero explains in Book 2 of On the Laws*); so it is established that the ancient Germans observed this same principle of law. This is evident because the monument we have been discussing is located seven hundred sixty-four paces from the village. If you take the common pace to be a "step and a space"—that is, consisting of two feet (*following the commentator Philander on Vitruvius, Book 1, chapter 6*)—you will certainly calculate the distance to be over 2,000 Roman paces.

(3) Since, besides bones and ashes, nothing else was included in those vessels, the ancients called these small urns not without reason partly <term>Ossuaria</term> <note>From the Latin 'os', meaning bone; a vessel for skeletal remains</note> (a word mentioned by the jurist Ulpian in Book 2 of the Digest, regarding the violation of sepulchers) and partly also <term>Cineraria</term> <note>From the Latin 'cinis', meaning ash; a vessel for cremated remains</note>, as found in *Kormann's 'On the Miracles of the Dead,' chapter 29*.

(4) In the past, Urns were either cheap or precious according to each person's status and wealth. For *Homer* testifies in the *Iliad* that the bones of illustrious men were stored in golden vessels; *Eutropius*, writing of the *Emperor Trajan* in *Book 8*, mentions a silver Urn; *Ammianus Marcellinus in Book 19* a bronze one; *Virgil in Book 6 of the Aeneid* a marble one; *Xiphilinus in his life of Severus* a clay one; and *Propertius in Book 2, Elegy 11* also mentions clay. From which what else can be concluded than that those people whose clay Urns were recently opened were of very meager means?

He also relates the location of the finds to ancient Roman laws requiring cremations to take place a safe distance from settlements to prevent fires.</summary>

# OBSERVATION

...others, in which it was possible to note the handles themselves, we have both collected and, for the sake of antiquity, kept those brought back with us. From this we conclude that the Urns were made from sand and small stones soaked in clay and fired. What we now assert has been confirmed by sight: that these Urns were not formed on a wheel, like the jars we commonly use today, but were born under the hands of a potter. They say the farmers of that village had already carried off several vessels of various sizes before, and not being enticed—as is believable—by the hope of some profit, they did not cease digging, whether they might come across gold or rings. (For Goltzius <note>Hubert Goltzius, a 16th-century numismatist and antiquarian</note> is witness that the Ancients were accustomed to bury certain coins and rings—sometimes made of bone or clay, sometimes of bronze, as well as gold and silver—in tombs along with the ashes.) It was in vain. For the outcome did not correspond to their hopes, since nothing appeared besides ashes and bones.

I observe from these things (1) that it is not inconsistent with the truth that the cremation of corpses was frequently practiced by the Gauls, the Germans, and the peoples further North, on the authority of *Caesar, Book 6 of the Gallic War; Eustathius on the Iliad, Book 1; Tacitus on the Germans, section 27; Alexander ab Alexandro, Genial Days, Book 3, page 130; Schedius on the German Gods, page 453; Philipp Clüver, Ancient Germany, Book 1, chapter 53;* and others.

And as (2) *Dio Cassius* is the authority (*Book 48, on Public Offices*) that in the times of *Augustus*, during the consulship of *Appius Claudius and Caius Norbanus*—that is, in the year 716 from the founding of the city <note>original: "Edict. V. DC CXVI," referring to 716 A.U.C. or 38 BCE</note>—an edict was issued: that the dead should not be cremated within two thousand paces <note>approximately two miles</note> of the city, because of the danger of fire (as *Cicero* explains in *Book 2 of On the Laws*). Thus, that the ancient *Germans* observed this law, whatever it may be, is established by the fact that this monument—of which we have spoken thus far—is distant seven hundred sixty-four paces from the village; if you take a "pace" according to the common track and space—that is, for two feet (*following Philander on Vitruvius, Book 1, chapter 6*)—you will certainly have more than 2,000 Roman paces.

(3) Since nothing else was included in those vessels besides bones and ashes, the Ancients called these small urns not without reason partly <term>Ossuaries</term> <note>original: "Ossuaria," literally "bone-holders"</note> (a word *Ulpian* mentions in *Book 2 of the Digest on the violation of tombs*) and partly <term>Cineraria</term> <note>literally "ash-holders"</note> also, according to *Kormann in Miracles of the Dead, chapter 29.*

(4) Formerly, Urns were either cheap or precious according to each person's status and means. For *Homer* testifies in the *Iliad* that the bones of illustrious men were usually stored in golden vessels; *Eutropius* writes of the *Emperor Trajan* (*Book 8*) regarding a silver urn; *Ammianus Marcellinus* (*Book 19*) mentions a bronze one; *Virgil* (*Aeneid, Book 6*) a marble one; *Xiphilinus* mentions one in his life of *Severus*; and *Propertius* (*Elegies, Book 2, Elegy 11*) mentions a clay one. From which what else can be concluded, except that those whose clay Urns were recently opened were in very humble circumstances?

# OBSERVATION

...others, in which we could even note the handles, we collected and kept with us for the sake of antiquity. From this we conclude that the Urns were made of sand and small stones mixed with clay and fired. We now assert, based on visual evidence, that these Urns were not formed on a wheel like the pots we commonly use today, but were born under the hands of the potter. It is said that the local farmers of that village had already carried away several vessels of various sizes; and it is likely they did not stop digging because they were enticed by the hope of some profit, whether they might stumble upon gold or rings. (For *Golzius* <note>Hubert Goltzius (1526–1583) was a Flemish painter and numismatist whose work on ancient coins was a standard reference for early archaeologists.</note> is a witness that the Ancients used to bury certain coins and rings—sometimes made of bone or clay, sometimes of bronze, gold, or silver—in tombs with the ashes.) Their efforts were in vain. For the outcome did not match their hopes; nothing appeared except for ashes and bones.

I observe from these things (1) that it is not at all unlikely that the cremation of corpses was a frequent practice among the Gauls, the Germans, and peoples further to the North, according to the authorities *Caesar in Book 6 of the Gallic Wars, Eustathius on the Iliad book 1, Tacitus on the Germans § 27, Alexander ab Alexandro in Genial Days book 3 page 130, Schedius on the German Gods page 453, Philipp Clüver in Ancient Germany book 1 chapter 53, and others.*

And just as (2) the author *Cassius Dio* states (*Book 48, on Public Offices*) that in the time of Augustus, under the consulship of *Appius Claudius and Caius Norbanus* (the year 716 from the founding of Rome), an edict was issued: that the dead should not be cremated within two thousand paces of the city, because of the danger of fire (as *Cicero* explains in *Book 2 of the Laws*); so it is clear that the Ancient Germans observed this specific Law. For the monument we have been discussing is located seven hundred and sixty-four paces from the village. If you take a pace to be the common measure of a step and its space, that is, two feet (following *Philander* <note>Guillaume Philandrier (1505–1565), a French architect and commentator on Vitruvius who attempted to standardize ancient measurements.</note> in his notes on *Vitruvius, Book 1, Chapter 6*), you will certainly have more than 2,000 Roman paces.

(3) Since, besides bones and ashes, nothing else was enclosed in those vessels, it was not without reason that the Ancients called these small urns partly **Ossuaries** <note>original: *Ossuaria*, derived from the Latin 'os' (bone).</note> (a word mentioned by the jurist *Ulpian* in *Book 2 of the Digest on violating sepulchers*) and partly **Cineraria** <note>original: *Cineraria*, derived from the Latin 'cinis' (ash).</note> as seen in *Kormann's On the Miracles of the Dead, chapter 29*.

(4) In former times, Urns were either cheap or precious according to each person's status and means. For *Homer* testifies in the *Iliad* that the bones of illustrious men were stored in golden vessels; *Eutropius* writes of the Emperor Trajan's silver Urn (*Book 8*); *Marcellinus* (*Book 19*) of a bronze one; *Virgil* (*Aeneid, Book 6*) of marble; *Xiphilinus* of the Emperor Severus; and *Propertius* (*Book 2, Elegy 11*) of clay. From these examples, what else can be concluded but that those whose clay Urns were recently opened were of very modest means?

### CONCERNING THE PAGAN GERMAN URNS DISCOVERED

And from this (5) I conclude: these were not communal graves, but were actually family ones <note>original: "familiaria" — referring to private family burial plots as opposed to public cemeteries.</note> (which are mentioned in Book 4 of the chapter *On Religion*), because there were two distinct pits only 27 paces apart, and each one contained several small urns.

Since (6) the authors of the annals testify that **Charlemagne** <note>original: "Carolus Magnus"</note>, the first Emperor of Germany, began to wage war in the year 772 AD against the Saxons while they were still pagans <note>original: "Ethnici"</note>—as they were refusing the Christian religion or, if some had accepted it, were rejecting it again and again. This war had already raged for more than thirty years (*Sethus Calvisius's Chronology*, page 708) before **Widukind** <note>The leader of the Saxons who eventually surrendered and converted to Christianity in 785 AD.</note>, the forefather of the Electors of Saxony, professed the saving faith along with all his lands. (Although some people on this side of the **Elbe River** <note>original: "cis Albim"</note>, resisting longer, continued to worship idols until nearly the year 1000.) From this, we estimate that this monument and the urns we have mentioned several times remained intact for approximately 700 or 800 years.

<meta>This page continues the dedication by Christian Adolph Balduin (nicknamed Hermes), shifting from his discussion of archaeological urns to the philosophical and alchemical foundations of his work, specifically the "Gold of the Air."</meta>

# DEDICATION.

There are indeed those who cast doubt on its origin, wishing to claim it for *Bernardo Canesio* <note>original: "Bernardo Canesio" — an early figure often cited in alchemical bibliographies, though his historical identity is obscure.</note>, whom they call the first of the Alchemists. This is the opinion that *Father Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit polymath who studied Egyptian hieroglyphs and Hermeticism.</note> himself followed in his *Oedipus Aegyptiacus, class 10, chapter 7*. Yet, it does not stray even a hair’s breadth <note>original: "ne latum... unguem" — a Latin idiom meaning "not by a fingernail's width."</note> from that inscription carved in Coptic characters on a stone near Memphis (as *Schatta the Coptic Memphite* writes): *Heaven above, heaven below; Stars above, stars below; whatever is above, all that is below. Take these things and you will be blessed.* And then, as Kircher himself does not deny, this is the *Tablet of the Theory of the entire treasury of Nature* <note>The author is quoting the famous "Emerald Tablet" of Hermes Trismegistus, the foundational text of Hermetic philosophy.</note>. Therefore, because the Egyptians themselves call *Hermetic Chemistry* <term>Alchemy: The ancient art of transmuting matter and seeking the "philosopher's stone" or universal medicine.</term> "Wisdom," it seems at the very least possible to conclude this: that *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom the Academy gave Balduin's nickname.</note> is the same person whom they place as their Prince and consider the fountainhead from which that knowledge later flowed to others.

And (to speak candidly), as for me: I shall never be so ungrateful as to disparage the fame of that *Tablet* or of the men who have illustrated it with their most learned commentaries. However, I have simultaneously held the same opinion: that it does not deal so much with metallic gold, which it would refine, as with **medicine**; a medicine which embraces and encloses the powers of all bodies, both superior <note>meaning the "celestial" or gaseous forces</note> and inferior <note>meaning earthly or solid matter</note>.

It has now been a full year since I decided to commit some thoughts on the **GOLD OF THE AIR** <note>original: "AURO AURÆ" — Balduin’s term for his discovery of a phosphorescent substance (calcium nitrate), which he believed captured the "spirit" or "gold" of the atmosphere.</note> to the public light. I call them "first fruits" because I had not previously produced that kind of writing. I would have spontaneously hidden my name, like a brick <note>The author likely uses "laterem" (brick) as a pun on the verb "latere" (to be hidden).</note>, were it not for the fact that it contained an **Anagram** which I wished to be read on the title page:

Thus (below, above) *a double Sun abounds in the Breezes*. <note>The original Latin "Sol duplus abundat in Auris" is an anagram for Christianus Adolphus Balduinus.</note> But that favor—GREATER THAN USUAL, ILLUSTRIOUS ACADEMY!—and your kindness, which I would have dared more easily to wish for than to ask, did not allow it. For that such a small labor of mine should be held in such high regard by YOU! One that would bring such honor and a name to its author that you would entice and even draw him into the fellowship of such a great Society with the most kind words! And you desired that this work, having been expanded by me, be entered into your *Ephemerides* <note>The "Miscellanea Curiosa," the first medical and scientific journal in the world, published by the Academy.</note>, which are worthy of being preserved in Cedar <note>original: "Cedro dignis" — an ancient idiom meaning "worthy of immortality," as cedar oil was used to preserve precious scrolls from rot.</note>; using the hand and pen of **SIR SIRIUS**, your interpreter <note>A reference to Henry Vollgnad, whose academic pseudonym within the society was "Sirius."</note>. Allow, most excellent man **HENRY VOLLGNAD**, your immortal name to be, as it were, profaned by me; and let these light pages promise themselves some fame and authority from it.

Nor will you be indignant to be read here, you who will always live through your eternal merits toward this most noble Order,

->Magnificent PRESIDENT and ARGONAUT,<-
->**JOHANNES MICHAEL FEHR**!<-

<sig>Thus</sig>

<meta>This page continues Balduin’s dedication, moving from the historical origins of alchemy to the personal honors bestowed upon him by the Academy and an explanation of the alchemical symbols used in his work.</meta>

# DEDICATION.

For such indeed is your merit toward me. Through YOU I have obtained these honors and distinctions! YOU, by the authority uniquely granted to You, added me as a member to this great *ACADEMY*! <note>The Academia Naturae Curiosorum (now the Leopoldina), which granted its members names of ancient sages.</note> You proclaimed it! YOU—something I would never have dared to hope for—increased my status with the enviable surname of *HERMES*! Finally, you seem to have acted in such a way that, perhaps believing this man would compete with you in performing kindnesses, you have instead won over my spirit. You have left me with the memory of so great a benefit that it can never be taken away, and however grateful I remain as long as I live, I shall never be able to fully repay it.

Just as I have struggled thus far to prove myself at least an imitator and disciple of *HERMES*, since I despaired of ever being called his "Son": so, I decided that the hours left to me should be spent in such a way that—having taken back into my hands the work on the *Gold of the Upper Air* <note>original: "Auro Aurae superioris" — Balduin’s term for his "phosphorus," which he believed was a universal spirit captured from the atmosphere.</note> attracted by the force of the universal Magnet—I might add *Gold* to this writing of the *Lower Air*, that is, our most universal Matter. To me, both of these are truly Hermetic.

But now it seems no less necessary for me to give an account of the *EMBLEM* <note>Referring to the frontispiece or a symbolic illustration in the book.</note>. For there you see the winged hieroglyphic circle, and the Chest, and the Sun, and the Moon, and whatever else appears. Surely the circle denotes the dual Spirit of the World: one Liquid, or the *Little Bee of Hermes* <note>original: "Apiculam" — "little bee." Alchemical texts often used bees or birds to represent volatile spirits.</note>, as they call it; the other coagulated. That circle includes, hidden within itself, the *upper Gold*; and flying about in the air, just like a little bird, it is attracted by the Philosophical Magnet <term>Philosophical Magnet: A substance believed by alchemists to have the power to "draw down" the vital spirit of the stars or the atmosphere.</term> lying in the chest. Through this Magnetism, I wish to suggest our fertile [matter] of the *lower Gold*. The Sun, with the fiery Triangle <note>The symbol △ represents the element of Fire.</note>, and the watery *Moon* <note>The symbol ▽ represents the element of Water.</note>, are the parents of the Spirit of the World and of both kinds of the *Gold of the Air*, and they designate them.

Nor did I wish this emblem to be "mute" <note>An alchemical "mute book" (mutus liber) is one with images but no text; Balduin is explaining why he added captions.</note>; hence, as I inscribed *ABOVE* at the top, so I took care to subscribe *BELOW* at the bottom. By this very act, I signify the Hermetic *Table* <note>The Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>: *That which is above is like that which is below, etc.* and truly that Philosophical saying of the Egyptians: *Heaven above, heaven below, stars above, stars below.* Receive these things either *BACKWARDS*, by the force of the universal Magnet from the *upper Air*, or *TOWARDS THIS SIDE AND APART* from the lower air. For he shall be blessed who knows how to include the *Triangles* △ and ▽ contained there in a double fashion.

Come then; since the name of *Hermes* has been decreed for me, I command this Treatise to be sacred to the honor of our Philosophical Mercury, as if it were a *Herma* <note>A "Herma" was a squared stone pillar topped with a head of the god Hermes, used as a boundary marker or sacred monument.</note>.

I establish this *Herma*, with the favor of the Most Serene *Elector of Saxony*, <insert>my</insert> most Merciful Lord: For whom, even for him, as a Hermetic Symbol: *ABOVE, BELO-*

<sig>P 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: LOW (Latin: SUM).</meta>

<summary>Balduin expresses gratitude for his academic title "Hermes" and explains the alchemical symbolism of his "Gold of the Air," linking it to the Emerald Tablet's principle of "as above, so below."</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dedication of Balduin's work to the Academy. He transitions from a personal thank-you for his academic title "Hermes" to an invitation for his colleagues to critique and contribute to his findings, framing the book itself as a "Herm" or boundary marker in the landscape of natural philosophy.</meta>

# DEDICATION.

In summary: However piously one acts, let it be understood even more in a Christian spirit. I establish this *Herm* <note>A "Herm" was a squared stone pillar topped with a carved head, traditionally used as a boundary marker or signpost. Balduin is using it here as a metaphor for his book.</note> at the prompting of your Renowned Academy:

->O LIGHTS and PILLARS<-
->of Ancient Hermetic Wisdom!<-

There were statues called *Herms*, bearing the image of *Mercury*, which of old were sacred to him; they were placed along public roads, and their heads could be interchanged. The paths upon which this *Hermes* of mine is placed and dedicated are likewise ambiguous. Let those who wish to determine what the more suitable "head"—that is, the principle and foundation of our Hermetic *Mercury*—might be, feel free to do so; for my part, I leave it open.

Near these *Herms*, piles of stones <note>original: "hermacas"</note> were formed by the spontaneous throwing of stones by those passing by; these heaps were also sacred to *Mercury*.

GREAT MEN, Incomparable COLLEAGUES: Build your own *Herm-heaps* at my *Herm*. Let each man contribute his stone—that is, let him change, remove, or add to it—but let him do so in a friendly, candid, and philosophical manner, as our 12th Academic Law commands.

If, however, contrary to expectations, there are those who would spitefully detract from it or soil their hands upon it, you have your sanctuaries: there are the *Ephemerides* <note>Referring to the *Miscellanea Curiosa*, the first medical and scientific journal in the world, published by this Academy.</note>. Once brought and inserted here, this work will not fear the hands of those who wish to disturb or cast it down; it shall remain safe and tranquil within that bosom of Wisdom, as it were. To this sanctuary I commit and consecrate it with the spirit of duty I owe you.

"Man is a God to man." <note>A common Latin proverb: *Homo homini deus*.</note> And you are *Gods*, for you are *Aesculapiuses* and *Apollos* <note>Aesculapius and Apollo were the Greek gods of medicine and light/knowledge; Balduin is complimenting the medical expertise of the Academy's members.</note>. Permit me, in the future, to be your Messenger, or, if you prefer, your *Hermes* and *Mercury*. Written at Hayn <note>Grossenhain, Saxony.</note> from my Study, on my birthday, the 29th of June—a day which (according to the account of Caelius Secundus Curio in *The Maltese War*, p. 430) was once held by the people of Malta as a month sacred to *Hermes*. In the year 1674.

->*To the Renowned Academy of Nature Curiosities of the Holy Roman Empire*<-
->A Devotee and Colleague most bound to any service,<-
->Christian Adolph Balduin,<-
->called *Hermes*.<-

# On the book *The Gold of the Air* and its appendix on *Hermetic Phosphorus*.

### [By] C. A., Baron of F. <note>While the final signature is Melchior Friebe, these initials likely refer to a patron or the author’s formal title; "Lib. Bar." is an abbreviation for *Liber Baro*, or Baron.</note>

The Muse had just witnessed the labors of our *Hermes* <note>This refers to Balduin himself, whose academic pseudonym within the Academy was "Hermes."</note>
and, marveling, she spoke these words:
"Now, learned old man, let your jewel-encrusted tablet be set aside;
this small book teaches more noble things.
It displays Gold floating through the thresholds of immense Olympus,
and flowers, and a day without night."

->In<-

### The Gold of the Air.

When HERMES beheld this book, he carried it to the Gods,
and drew it toward the various regions of Helicon <note>A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses, representing poetic and scientific inspiration.</note>.
The matter lacked a leader and requested a Name from Apollo.
He, after various meditations, finally said:
"O little Book, you shall be called my DIVINE WAND <note>original: *VIRGULA DIA*. The "divining rod" or "wand of Mercury" was a common symbol for the power to find hidden treasures.</note>;
The AIR <note>original: *AURA*</note> shall give the GOLD that the Earth cannot, with you as the guide."
Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and spring.</note> was present, and remembering her own glory, added these honors:
"Unless I am mistaken, an eternal Spring is now upon me."
So says the joyful Goddess. To whom the rest of the crowd of Gods asked:
"Is death then absent even from the Flowers?"
"Indeed, we intend the same for Mankind," Hebe <note>The goddess of eternal youth and cupbearer to the gods.</note> added.
"Tell me: shall the 'sacred hunger for gold' <note>original: *AURI sacra fames*. A play on a famous line from Virgil's *Aeneid*. While Virgil meant "accursed greed," the poet here suggests a holy or divinely-inspired quest for alchemical gold.</note> now be mine?"

->De<-

### On the HERMETIC PHOSPHORUS.

Cease to wonder at the use of a perpetual light;
This Book shall be like a perpetual Light itself.

*The Gold of the Air and the Hermetic Phosphorus:*
*as a truly Hermetic Labor and a study*
*of pure Curiosity, this must be commended by*
**Melchior Friebe**, Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine, 
Physician of the Hayna District, and Fellow of the 
Academy of the Curious in Nature of the 
Holy Roman Empire <note>Original: *S. R. I.* (Sacri Romani Imperii). The Academy mentioned is the Leopoldina, founded to study the natural world.</note>.

# INDEX OF CHAPTERS

Ch. I. On the Air <note>original: *Aura*. In this context, it refers to the atmosphere or a subtle, life-giving breath of the world.</note> in general.
II. On the Air, filled with the effluvia <note>Term: *effluvia* refers to invisible particles or vapors flowing out from physical bodies.</note> of various things.
III. In the Air also flutters the Hermetic Matter, or the Spirit of the World <note>original: *spiritus Mundi*. A vitalizing essence believed by alchemists to permeate the atmosphere.</note>.
IV. On Gold in the Air.
V. On the universal magnet, attracting Gold from the Air.
VI. On the method of attracting Gold from the Air.
VII. On the time for attracting Gold from the Air.
VIII. On the colors of the Gold of the Air.
IX. On the potable Gold <note>original: *Auro potabili*. "Drinkable gold" was a legendary medicinal elixir believed to grant longevity and health.</note> of the Air.
X. On the virtues of the Gold of the Air in the Animal kingdom.
XI. On the virtues of the Gold of the Air in the Mineral kingdom.
XII. On the virtues of the Gold of the Air in the Vegetable kingdom.

# INTRODUCTION.

The ancient Philosophers recognized, and we also freely recognize, that the highest things are in the innermost, and the lowest things are in the highest: in Heaven, indeed, are earthly things, according to their cause and in a heavenly manner; and on earth truly are Heavenly things, in an earthly manner; see <note>original: *vid.*, abbreviation for *vide*.</note> *Proclus on Sacrifice and Magic* <note>Proclus was a 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher; his works were fundamental to later Renaissance magic and alchemy.</note>. From this, one may gaze upon the Sun, the Moon, and the other stars on the earth, but according to their earthly quality; while in heaven one may see plants, stones, metals, and minerals, according to their heavenly nature. Therefore, by the light of Hermetic Philosophy, all superior natural things are known through the inferior, and the inferior through the superior. This Harmony is that golden chain <note>original: *catena aurea*. A classical concept representing the interconnectedness of all levels of creation, from God down to inanimate matter.</note> by which lower things are connected to higher ones, governed by them, and indeed mutually attracted to them. Do you not see how wonderful is the play of the Elements? How even contrary things, by the mediation of another, embrace one another? How fire is joined to water, water to fire, earth to air, and water to earth?

<summary>This page lists the twelve chapters of the treatise, focusing on the extraction of "Gold" from the atmosphere, followed by an introduction explaining the Hermetic principle of "as above, so below." It establishes the philosophical foundation that all things in the universe are interconnected through a "golden chain" of nature.</summary>

<header>INTRODUCTION. 113</header>

...[how it is] preserved? and how is the World preserved by this "discordant concord" <note>original: *hâc discordi rerum velut concordiâ*. This is a reference to the philosophical concept of *discordia concors*, where the universe remains stable through the balance of opposing forces.</note> of things? Moved by these reasons in the previous century, *Antoine Mizauld of Montluçon, a Frenchman* <note>Antoine Mizauld (1510–1578) was a famous French physician and astrologer known for his works on natural secrets.</note>, published in *Paris* the *Harmony of the Superior Nature of the World and the Inferior*, along with the wonderful bond and sympathy of both realms. All searchers of Nature are moved alongside me to embrace this Chain <note>The 'Great Chain of Being' or 'Golden Chain of Homer', symbolizing the interconnection of all things from the divine to the material.</note> connecting the higher things to the lower. For he who knows this shall penetrate the greatest of Mysteries, according to the testimony of *Agaziel the Arab*.

Since the famous Emerald Tablet <note>original: *Tabula Smaragdina*. One of the most famous and foundational texts in the history of alchemy, attributed to the legendary Hermes Trismegistus.</note> of our *Father Hermes* teaches this most perfectly, and since I intended it to be the foundation for my work on the *Gold of the Air*, I believed it would be worth the effort to insert here the version that *Kriegsmann* <note>Wilhelm Christoph Kriegsmann (1633–1679) was a 17th-century scholar who proposed that the Emerald Tablet was originally written in Phoenician.</note> reconstructed from the Phoenician context, which reads as follows:

> 1. *I speak truly, without fiction, certainly and most truly.*
> 2. *These inferior things join forces with those superior things, and those in turn with these, to produce one thing, the most wonderful of all.*
> 3. *And just as all things were brought forth from one by the word of the one GOD: so all things are perpetually regenerated from this one disposition of Nature.*
> 4. *Its father is the Sun, its mother the Moon: it is carried in the womb, as it were, by the air, and nourished by the earth.*
> 5. *This is the cause of all perfection of things throughout this universe.*
> 6. *It arrives at the highest perfection of forces if it should return into the soil.*
> 7. *Divide the soil into parts, having suffered fire, thinning its density, with the sweetest thing of all.*
> 8. *Ascend with the highest sagacity of wit from earth to heaven, and from there descend again to earth, and gather the forces of superiors and inferiors into one. Thus you will possess the glory of the whole world, and so you shall no longer be a man of abject lot.*
> 9. *Now this thing itself exists stronger than strength: for by penetrating it will subdue bodies both thin and solid.*
> 10. *And thus indeed were created all things that the world contains.*
> 11. *From here proceed admirable works, which are established in the same manner.*
> 12. *To me indeed was the name Hermes Trismegistus given for this reason, that I was found to be a Doctor of the three parts of the Wisdom of the world.*
> 13. *These are the things which I thought were to be recorded concerning the most excellent work of the Chymical art <note>term: *Chymicæ artis*. In the 17th century, the terms "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of matter and its transformations.</note>.*

By the superior Air <note>original: *Auram*. While "aura" can mean a breeze, in this alchemical context, it refers to a vital, spiritualized air or vapor.</note> I mean simple air, as an Element: by the inferior Air I mean coagulated air, as a compound of elements. From both, Philosophical Gold <term>Philosophical Gold: Not common bullion, but the perfected "seed" or essence sought by alchemists to transform matter.</term> can be extracted. Each is Gold: the former superior and remote, the latter inferior and nearby. Yet our Hermetic Gold is one!

# CHAPTER I.

## *On the Air in general.*

Just as the Air or "Aura" is that instrument of the World and the universal messenger of our Spirit: Whence *Trismegistus* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says that the Wind—that is, the Air—carried it in its belly: <note>original: *Ventum, id est, Aërem, inquit, portasse illum in ventre suo*. This is a famous line from the *Emerald Tablet* describing the birth of the "One Thing."</note> so in this 1st Chapter, by the name of the Aura or superior Air, we understand that primordial, transparent Element, devoid of both lightness and weight, unchangeable and perpetual, endowed with natural cold, unless hindered by the strength of locations or mixtures: Filled everywhere with pores, and thus far tolerating its own extension or compression: *Jan Baptist van Helmont* <note>A pioneering Flemish chemist (1580–1644) who first recognized "gas" as a state of matter.</note> in *The Tomb of the Plague* <note>original: *in Tum. Pest.* refers to Helmont’s work *Tumulus Pestis*.</note> states it exists below the heavens and above the water and earth: as *Scaliger* <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated polymath and physician.</note> says in *Exoterical Exercises on Subtlety, Exercise 27*: notwithstanding that *Isaac Vossius* <note>Isaac Vossius (1618–1689) was a Dutch scholar whose work on physics and geography was influential in the 17th century.</note> in *On the Motion of the Sea and Winds, ch. 21, p. 49*, calls the withdrawal of the four Elements in the Macrocosm <note>The universe as a whole, reflecting the "microcosm" of the human body.</note> a fable: [claiming] the Air is truly nothing else but water, or moisture dilated, extending itself in every direction according to the law of equilibrium: while the seas especially—

<header>CHAPTER I. ON THE AIR IN GENERAL.</header>

the principal source of the air and is thus subject to the sun; and that the air is moved according to the various aforementioned motions of the sea. <note>The author is likely referring to a theory that the tides and the atmosphere are moved by the same solar or celestial forces.</note>

Yet this Elementary air is not enclosed within the celestial spheres; <margin>Tycho Brahe, *Astronomical Epistles*, Book 1, page 100.</margin> but rather moves within its three regions—namely the highest, the middle, and the lowest—and it runs from the surface of the earth and water even to the heights of the clouds. And although those words of *Scaliger* <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated scholar and natural philosopher.</note> are most true: *Our air is mixed with water: for if this were not so, it would surely perish:* as the great *Albertus Magnus* <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and influential philosopher-scientist.</note> quite aptly concludes from this: nevertheless, it does not deserve to be called a species of water: even though *Lambert Danaeus* <note>A French Calvinist theologian and author of *Physica Christiana* (Christian Physics).</note> <margin>*Lambert Danaeus, Christian Physics, Treatise 1, chapter 9, page 34.*</margin> denies that air is an element distinct from water. For this claim, he is rightfully rebuked by *Andreas Libavius* <note>A German physician and chemist, often credited with writing the first systematic chemistry textbook.</note> in his *On the Universe and the Origin of Created Things, Book 1.*

Air lives in fire, and fire in Air; Water in Earth, and Earth in Water; and finally, Water in Air. Fire purifies the Air, Air purifies the Water, and Water purifies the Earth; each one, by the benefit of fire, communicates its own clarity to the others. And since anyone who searches for "pure" Elements in the World is likely to "waste both oil and labor," <note>original: *oleum perditurus juxtà & operam sit*. A Latin idiom for wasting time and resources on a fruitless task.</note> what is there to prevent us from believing this: that what we call air is "thin water," and fire is likewise "thin aura," and similarly water is "thin earth," while earth is "thick fire"? And on the contrary: that earth is "frozen water," water is "frozen air," and air is "thickened Fire"? (See: *King Alphonso’s Key of Wisdom* in the *Theatrum Chemicum*, Vol. 5, page 861). <note>Alphonso X of Castile, to whom several alchemical works were attributed.</note> Hence air becomes lymph <note>In this context, "lymph" refers to clear water or a pure liquid state.</note>, from lymph becomes earth, and from earth comes water. *Drebbel* proves each of these with examples in his *Treatise on the Nature of the Elements*. <note>Cornelis Drebbel (1572–1633), a Dutch engineer and alchemist famous for building an early submarine.</note> Therefore, since neither the aura nor the ether differ in any way from the air—except, as *Emanuel Magnanus* states in *Natural Philosophy, chapter 16, proposition 2*, just as the clearest water differs from the impure—and consequently the aura and the superior ether are nothing but the most subtle air; I believe we shall commit no error here if we take these terms to mean one and the same thing everywhere.

2. By the "lower aura," or "coagulated air," as a compound of elements, we understand our "universal subject" <term>Universal Subject: The primary material or "materia prima" that alchemists believe contains the potential for all things.</term> (which we discuss specifically in *Chapter 3*). Concerning this, *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and pioneer of chemistry.</note> says most correctly in his *Epilogue*: *Air is the matter of the ancient Philosophers, which has within itself an airy, that is, a white color.* In this "coagulated aura" of ours, the dry and cold part is called **salt** or "body"; that which is hot and fiery is called **sulphur** or "soul"; and the cold and moist part is called **Mercury** or "Spirit." <note>These are the *Tria Prima* (Three Primes) of Paracelsus, the basic components of all matter in alchemical theory.</note> Indeed, through continuous and perennial "cooking," <term>Cooking: Also called decoction; the application of steady, controlled heat to transform substances.</term> those three are converted into one in this manner: the cold and moist is converted into the hot and moist, and finally into the hot and dry. Thus, those things which are found in our matter...

those confused and distinct elements are converted and become a single fiery and dry Element. Regarding this subject, we shall add several remarks in all the other Chapters in their proper order.

# CHAPTER II.

->*On the Aura, filled with the Effluvia of various things.*<-

It must be observed, then, (1) that the Aura—meaning the upper air—can be considered in two ways: either as a simple Body and a pure, unadulterated Element (which we discussed in the preceding Chapter); or insofar as it contains various vapors and exhalations within itself. *Dr. Daniel Sennert, On Fevers, book 4, chapter 2, page 382.* <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637) was a prolific German physician who attempted to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with atomism and alchemy.</note> This latter view is the discourse we shall now establish. The pores, or porosities, with which we said the air is filled, are either completely open and devoid of matter, remaining in their own integrity, or they are filled with vapors and foreign exhalations. These pores are like "wombs," in which the outflows <term>aporrhoeae; a Greek term for the invisible flow of particles from an object</term> of the "fruits of water" <note>The author likely refers to substances born from moisture</note> are resolved again into the ultimate simplicity of water from which they came; they are stripped of whatever "signatures" <note>The 'Signature' was a common alchemical belief that every natural object bore a physical sign of its inner medicinal or spiritual properties</note> of their first seeds they once held. *See Helmontius in The Tomb of the Plague, section: Form.* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician who coined the word "gas."</note> For from sublunary things <note>Objects in the terrestrial world "beneath the moon"</note> a double spirit or breath is extracted by the power of the heat of the sun or stars, or even by subterranean fires, as well as by the heat innate to the things themselves, and is lifted up into the heights. *Father Caspar Schott in Curious Physics, book 9, chapter 1, section 1.* <note>Caspar Schott (1608–1666) was a Jesuit scientist and mathematician known for documenting wondrous natural phenomena.</note>

Moreover, such foreign Effluvia <note>original: "Effluvia"; refers to invisible particles or "out-flows"</note> in the aura are varied by circumstance, according to the nature of the solid body from which they exhaled. Because of this, "Virgin" Earth <note>Soil that has been purified or has not yet produced growth</note> produces herbs of its own accord if you expose it in a suitable vessel to the open air by night and day, especially at a favorable time. This is a sign that the earth was impregnated by the aura alone, which contained either the seeds or the spirits of such herbs. The Earth exists at least as a permanent matrix <note>A "matrix" in this context is a womb-like environment that hosts growth</note>, not as the Mother of Bodies; this must be carefully remembered. In this way, the Spirits of Saltpeter <term>Nitrum</term> and Vitriol <term>Vitriolum; a term for sulfate minerals</term> fly through the air. For if, after you have extracted the spirit from Vitriol or Saltpeter, you place its residue <term>Colcotar; the dry, metallic remains after distillation</term> under the open sky and leave it there for a few days, and soon return it to a Retort <note>A glass vessel used for distillation</note> and apply degrees of fire, you will draw out a new and even more powerful spirit. *Magnanus* <note>Emmanuel Maignan (1601–1676), a French physicist and philosopher</note> provides these experiments *in Natural Philosophy, chapter 16,* and from this he concludes the reason why in certain places the air is healthy, while in others it is not at all so, and why it produces pleasant odors in some places and foul ones in others: namely, because that spirit contains either healthy and pleasant things, or unhealthy and unpleasant ones.

Clearly, the winds smell of the bodies they have passed by. While...

<header>FILLED WITH VARIOUS THINGS.</header>

When roses are in bloom in the districts of *Fontenay* <note>original: "Fontenau"; likely Fontenay-aux-Roses</note> and *Vaugirard*, which are near the city of *Paris*, the air is brought to the nostrils steeped in a wonderful sweetness of scent, as reported by *Sir Kenelm Digby in The Theater of Sympathy, page 48.* <note>Sir Kenelm Digby (1603–1665) was an English courtier and philosopher famous for his "powder of sympathy," a form of "magic" based on the idea that particles move through the air to heal wounds.</note>

In contrast, in *Madrid in Spain*, the air is so thin and rare that, unless it were thickened from time to time by the emissions <term>effluvia; the invisible particles or vapors flowing out of bodies</term> of other things, it would be less suitable for the breathing of men and animals.

In *London in England*, because the inhabitants in that whole vast city use fires made from that type of coal which they are accustomed to call "fossils" <note>Sea-coal or mined coal, which was notorious for its heavy smoke in 17th-century London</note>, the entire air is filled as if with salt and soot; because of this, beds, tapestries, and any other utensils, no matter how clean and shining they were before, are darkened in a very short time.

In *Trapani in Sicily*, a maritime city famous for its salt-works, the air is so corrosive that it eats away at iron. *Father Caspar Schott in Curious Arts, book 4, chapter 1, preliminary 1, section 44.* <note>Caspar Schott (1608–1666) was a Jesuit scientist who documented various natural wonders and mechanical inventions.</note>

Therefore, the constitution of the air is varied for various reasons, insofar as it depends on the site of locations, the nature of the earth, the proximity of the sea and lakes, metallic mines, and the seasons of the year, as is described at length by *Dr. Daniel Sennert in Institutions of Medicine, book 4, part 1, chapter 2.*

Therefore, just as various emissions of various things are continuously mixed into the air—whether these are "spirits" of every kind, such as earthy, watery, and fiery ones, released from the earth, water, fire, and also from minerals, plants, animals, and even the bodies of men in great abundance by the force of heat (whether external or internal) and carried on high because of their acquired lightness—so too are these same things "embodied" <note>turned from vapor back into solid matter</note> when they are attracted by homogeneous bodies through a magnetic force.

I use the "Mineral of Matthias of Solms" <note>original: "Minera Matti-Solari"; likely a reference to a specific yellowish pyrite or marcasite mineral named after a local chemist or source</note>; (which they bring from *Hesse*, and value at no more than three pennies <note>original: "diobolis"; an ancient coin used here to denote a very small sum</note> per pound in *Leipzig*). It is yellow inside, like a golden marcasite, but otherwise plainly tasteless. For if you boil it in water for some time after it has been reduced to powder, it will not even change color, nor will you have any flavor other than that of the water. Twelve ounces of this I recently exposed to the cold air for fourteen full days. When this was done, it immediately attracted a certain moist and sweet salt, like that of vitriol <note>Sulfates, often appearing as green or blue crystals</note>. What happened then? It had actually increased in weight by two ounces. This extracted salt, purified with rainwater and appearing in a green form, is marvelous for how much it resists those diseases which arise from an abundance of *Tartar*— <note>In the medical theory of Paracelsus, "Tartar" referred to stony deposits or "gravel" formed in the body (like kidney stones or gouty deposits) resulting from metabolic "smoke" or waste.</note>

# CHAPTER II.

## *On the Air, Filled with the Effluvia of Various Things.*

<margin>Dr. Daniel Sennert, *On Fevers*, book 4, chapter 2, page 382.</margin>
<margin>Father Caspar Schott, in *Curious Physics*, book 9, chapter 1, section 1.</margin>
It must therefore be observed, first: that the Air <note>original: "Aura"; often used in this period to refer to the atmosphere or the vital breath of the world</note>—meaning the upper air—can be considered in two ways: either as a simple body and a pure, unadulterated element (which was discussed in the preceding chapter); or, insofar as it contains various vapors and exhalations within itself, which is the subject of our discourse here. The pores, or porosities, with which we have said the air is filled, either stand completely open and devoid of any body, remaining in their own integrity, or they are filled with vapors and foreign exhalations. They are like "wombs," in which the emanations <note>original: "aporrhœæ"; a Greek term for the invisible flowings or particles emitted by objects</note> of the "fruits of water" are resolved again into the ultimate simplicity of water from which they originated, and are stripped of whatever "signatures" or imprints of their first seeds they possessed (see *Helmont in The Plague-Tumor, § Form*). <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician who believed everything originated from water and "seeds" or spiritual blueprints.</note> For from sublunary things, a twofold spirit or breath is extracted and raised on high by the heat of the sun or stars, or even by subterranean fires, as well as by the heat innate to the things themselves.

Moreover, these foreign <term>Effluvia</term> <note>Invisible particles or vapors flowing out of solid bodies</note> in the air are varied by accident, according to the nature of the solid from which they exhaled. Hence the Earth, as a "Virgin," produces herbs of her own accord if you expose soil "seasoned" in a suitable vessel to the open air night and day, especially at a favorable time. This is a sign that the earth was impregnated by the air alone, which contained either the seeds or the spirits of such herbs. The Earth at least exists as a permanent matrix <note>A "matrix" in 17th-century science was a place where things are formed or held, like a womb or a mold</note>, not as the Mother of Bodies; this must be properly understood. In the same way, the spirits of <term>Nitre</term> <note>Saltpeter</note> and <term>Vitriol</term> <note>Sulfuric acid or the metal sulfates from which it was derived</note> fly through the air. For if, after you have extracted the spirit from vitriol or nitre, you place its <term>Colcothar</term> <note>The dry, reddish-brown residue left after distilling vitriol</note> (as they call it) under the open sky and leave it there for a few days, and then soon place it back in a <term>Retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling chemicals</note> and apply degrees of fire, you will draw out a new and even more effective spirit. The philosopher *Magnanus* <note>Emanuel Maignan (1601–1676), a French physicist and theologian</note> brings forward these experiments in *Natural Philosophy, chapter 16*, and from them he gathers the reason why the air is healthy in certain places but not at all in others; and likewise why it produces pleasant odors in some places and foul ones in others: namely, because that spirit contains either healthy and pleasant things, or unhealthy and unpleasant ones.

To be sure, the winds smell of those bodies which they have passed by. While...

# FILLING THE AIR WITH VARIOUS THINGS.

When roses bloom in the regions of Fontenau and Vaurigard, which are near the city of **Paris**, the air is brought to the nostrils imbued with a wonderful sweetness of scent, as reported by *Sir Kenelm Digby in his Sympathetic Theater, page 48.* <note>Sir Kenelm Digby (1603–1665) was an English courtier and natural philosopher famous for his "powder of sympathy," which he claimed could heal wounds from a distance.</note>

On the other hand, in **Madrid in Spain**, the air is so thin <note>original: "tenuis"; meaning rare or low-density</note> that, unless it were repeatedly thickened by the effluvia <note>Invisible particles or vapors flowing out of substances.</note> of other things, it would be less suitable for the respiration of humans and animals.

In **London in England**, because the inhabitants throughout that vast city use fires of that type of coal which they are accustomed to call "fossils" <note>This refers to "sea coal" or mineral coal, which was then a relatively new and notoriously smoky alternative to wood charcoal.</note>, the entire air is filled, as it were, with salt and soot: whence beds, wall-hangings, and whatever other utensils, however neat and shining before, are darkened in a very short time.

In **Trapani in Sicily**, a maritime city famous for its salt mines, the air is so corrosive that it eats away at iron. *Father Caspar Schott in Curious Techniques, book 4, chapter 1, prelude 1, section 44.* <note>Caspar Schott (1608–1666) was a Jesuit physicist and mathematician known for documenting mechanical and natural wonders.</note>

Therefore, the constitution of the air varies for various reasons, insofar as it depends on the situation of places, the nature of the earth, the proximity of the sea and lakes, metallic mines, and the seasons of the year, as is extensively described by *Dr. Daniel Sennert in Medical Institutions, book 4, part 1, chapter 2.* <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637) was an influential German physician who attempted to reconcile Galenic medicine with atomism and alchemy.</note>

Just as various effluvia of different things are continuously mixed in the air—or spirits of every kind, namely earthy, watery, and fiery—released in great abundance from the earth, water, fire, minerals, plants, animals, and even the bodies of men by the power of heat (whether external or internal) and carried aloft due to their acquired lightness: so they are "corporified" <note>original: "corporificantur"; the process of a spiritual or vaporous substance taking on a solid, physical body.</note> when attracted by homogeneous <note>of the same kind</note> bodies through magnetic force.

I use the <term>Iron-Solar Mineral|Minera Marti-Solari: A term from alchemy/mineralogy. "Mars" represents iron, and "Solar" refers to the sun or gold, likely describing a golden-colored iron pyrite or marcasite.</term> (which they bring from **Hesse**, and value at **Leipzig** for no more than three diobols <note>An ancient coin; the author is noting the mineral is inexpensive.</note> per pound); it is yellow inside, like a golden marcasite, but otherwise completely tasteless. For if you boil it in water after reducing it to a powder for some time, it will not even change color, nor will you have any flavor other than that of water. I recently exposed twelve ounces of this to cold air for a full fourteen days. Having done so, it continuously attracted a peculiar, moist, and sweet salt—of the kind that vitriol <note>A sulfate mineral, often appearing as green or blue crystals.</note> is—and what more? It even increased in weight by two ounces itself. This salt, extracted with rainwater, appearing in a green form and then purified, is a wonder how much it resists those diseases that arise from an abundance of Tartar <note>In the medical theory of Paracelsus, "Tartar" refers to stony deposits in the body, such as kidney stones, gallstones, or the "stone" of gout.</note>...

If you consider its power, it is endowed with an attractive quality equal to that of **Bismuth ore**; it truly draws from the air a very green **Vitriol** <note>Vitriol refers to various sulfate minerals; green vitriol is iron(II) sulfate.</note>. Most Chymists believe it to be the immature **Electrum of Paracelsus**, **Black Saturn** <note>original: "niger Saturnus"; in alchemy, Saturn is the symbol for lead.</note>, and the **White Antimony of the Philosophers**. Out of what I believe was a touch of envy, *Joachim Polemann, in his Treatise on Philosophical Sulphur, page 114,* preferred to keep its true name silent; however, I have had my reasons for stating it here distinctly.

An experiment which has **Sir Kenelm Digby** as its author is bordering on the miraculous. For he found that **Arcueil earth** <note>original: "Arcevilenſi terra"; Arcueil is a commune near Paris famous for its quarries.</note>, when exposed to the open air and moistened every day with rain water, yielded **vitriol** after the first month; **sulfur** in the second; **lead** in the third; **tin** in the fourth; **iron** in the fifth; and in the sixth month, he found thin filings of **silver**. This same experiment was attempted most successfully by **Mr. de l’Oborie** and **Mr. Lotques**, the Spagyrist <note>An alchemist, specifically one who practices the "spagyric art" of separating and recombining plant or mineral components.</note> to the King of France. Anyone wishing to know more about this may consult the very lengthy discussion by **Olaus Borrichius** in his *Dissertation on the Origin and Progress of Chemistry, page 149.* In this same way, **Athanasius Kircher** teaches in *The Subterranean World, book 4, chapter 8, page 215,* that exhausted mines of gold, silver, iron, and stone grow back anew.

**Sir Marc-Antonio de Castagna**, Supreme Prefect of the Mines of the Venetian Republic and founder of the Panctetic Society, will attest before anyone that it is possible through art, in any house or laboratory, to construct a **perpetual mine** in various ways. From this, at appointed times, true metal can be extracted just as if from a mine, according to **Johann Joachim Becher** in *New Chemical Experiments, page 62.*

You see, I believe, what the air provides and what the influence of the Heavens achieves, so that it is not so necessary to descend into ingeniously crafted "rinds" <note>Metaphorical or superficial explanations.</note>. # CHAPTER III.

## The Hermetic Matter, or Spirit of the World, also floats in the Air.

Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650) was a French philosopher and scientist whose mechanical philosophy sought to explain the world through the movement of matter.</note> (*Principles of Philosophy*, Part IV, no. 48) and Lipstorfius <note>Daniel Lipstorp (1631–1684) was a German astronomer and mathematician who promoted Cartesian ideas.</note> (*Specimen of Cartesian Philosophy*, page 3, chapter 2) seem to assert that air is nothing other than a collection of terrestrial particles, so thin and so far apart from each other that they obey any movements of the celestial globules <note>original: "globulorum cœlestium"; in Cartesian physics, these were tiny spheres of "second matter" that filled the universe and transmitted light and force.</note>. Although Mr. Robert Boyle <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691) was an Anglo-Irish natural philosopher, often considered the first modern chemist, famous for his laws on the pressure of gases.</note>, in his *Experiments on Air* (Experiment 1), interprets Descartes differently. Nevertheless, one must believe that nature—which has always taken peculiar care that the tireless exchange between heaven and earth should endure as the marvelous harmony of the whole machine of the world—has, from the very beginning of the world, disseminated a certain celestial substance or matter throughout the entire globe.

And primarily 1. in the Breath [Aura] or upper air, as the most worthy Element, in which the seed and vital spirit, or the home of the soul of every creature, lies hidden. (Michael Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a Polish alchemist and physician who believed a life-giving substance ("food of life") existed in the air.</note>, *Treatise on Sulfur*, page 152). For this reason, the Doctors of the Hebrews remove Air from the number of the Elements, regarding it as a kind of medium and glue, joining diverse things together at once, and like a resounding spirit of the world’s instrument. (See *The Temple of Nature* by Heinrich Kornmann, page 72, and Dr. Marcellus Vrankheim’s *Calculation* to the *All-Woven-Shield-Battle*, page 36). <note>original: "ἐπιλογίσιν" (epilogisin) and "πανυπερυπλόμαχ." (panyperyplomachos). These are scholarly Greek terms for "summary calculation" and a "battle involving an all-encompassing shield," likely referencing complex philosophical or astronomical disputes.</note>

Daily experience teaches this: that by this Element not only minerals, animals, and vegetables live, but even the other Elements. (Johann Sperling <note>Johann Sperling (1603–1658) was a German physician and professor of physics.</note>, *Institutions of Physics*, book 4, chapter 2, at precept 1). Inasmuch as a certain astral spirit, flowing into these lower realms, adapts itself to metals, stones, and plants; indeed, it secretly works itself into their very substance. (Rudolph Goclenius <note>Rudolf Goclenius the Younger (1572–1621) was a German physician who studied "magnetic" medicine and the sympathetic cures of wounds.</note>, *On Magnetic Union*, page 102). It flourishes everywhere, and in all generations it is the most immediate author and movement of everything. (Marsilio Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499) was an Italian scholar and priest who helped revive Neoplatonism during the Renaissance.</note>, *On Life*, book 3, chapter 3).

This ethereal substance Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a Flemish chemist and physician who recognized that "gas" was a distinct state of matter.</note> calls the **Great Magnale** (*Treatise on the Magnetic Cure of Wounds*, section 151). There are some who attribute the same to the super-celestial waters,

# CHAPTER III.

## The Hermetic Matter, or Spirit of the World, also floats in the Air.

...celestial <note>original: "lestibus," completing "coelestibus" from the previous page.</note> waters, as Athanasius Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a Jesuit polymath and one of the most famous scholars of the 17th century, known for his studies on everything from volcanology to Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> describes in his *Ecstatic Journey* [*Itinerarium Exstaticum*]. He imagines these waters to be like dew by their very nature, containing their own light, transparent, and flowing down into this sublunary <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—everything below the orbit of the Moon—which in ancient science was subject to change and decay.</note> world. He believes they serve as a kind of nourishment to sustain whatever bodies exist anywhere in the world. Heinrich Nollius <note>Heinrich Nolle (c. 1583–1626) was a German physician and "Paracelsian" philosopher who sought to combine chemistry with mystical insights.</note> supports this opinion, drawing from the Kabbalah and secret philosophy in his *Physics* (Book 2, Chapter 2). He says there is a water of this kind, by whose perpetual and continuous influx all lower things are animated and prepared to receive "exaltation" <note>In alchemy, "exaltation" is the process of refining a substance to its highest, most potent state.</note>. It first communicates itself to the visible stars, then through the stars to the air, and through the air to the water and earth, and so on.

But I do not make this opinion my own. Instead, I cast my favorable vote <note>original: "album adjicio sententiae calculum," literally "I add a white stone to the judgment," a Roman way of expressing approval.</note> for the judgment of Pierre Paulmier <note>Pierre Paulmier (1568–1610) was a French doctor whose alchemical interests put him at odds with the conservative medical establishment in Paris.</note>, Doctor of Paris, in his *Dogma on the Philosopher’s Stone* (page 64). (This is despite the fact that, for weighty reasons, the Paris Medical College condemned his writings in 1609, marking them for destruction <note>original: "obelisco ut jugularetur." The "obelisk" or dagger symbol (†) was used by editors to mark text as spurious or condemned.</note>). The Sun is the heart of the world, not merely its eye. It spreads a life-giving nectar through the air into all things below. Therefore, this is a permanent, celestial, and divine substance, entirely foreign to the physical mixture of the four Elements. Just as it arrives at the birth of each individual thing, why should it not be drawn out again when they dissolve? It is rooted in a host <note>original: "subjecto," referring to the physical matter that holds the spiritual essence.</note> that is more or less tenacious depending on the condition of the thing. It stays with the object, departs, dissipates, returns, recedes, is introduced, and is extracted. For it does not exist in a pure state, stripped of every host. Just as it is not born naked, so it is not extracted naked.

Furthermore, this celestial matter of which we are now speaking is called the **Spirit and Soul of the World** (by Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, known as Avicenna (c. 980–1037), was a Persian philosopher whose medical and philosophical texts were foundational in Europe.</note> and the Platonists); it is the **dew of night**, **rarefied water of the day**, **invisible frozen spirit**, **seed of all things**, **living spirit of all creatures**, **celestial life**, and the **universal spirit** enclosed in all things and thickened by the air. Friedrich Hoffmann <note>Friedrich Hoffmann (1660–1742) was a prominent German physician and chemist who tried to explain the "mechanics" of the human body.</note>, in his *Method of Medicine* (Book 1, Chapter 19), calls it the **Universal Seed of Nature**, the **Saline-Sulphuro-mercurial Spirit** <note>Alchemists believed all matter was composed of three principles: Salt (stability), Sulphur (combustibility), and Mercury (volatility).</note>, a single substance distinguished by a triple power, and the immediate principle of all things. Athanasius Kircher, in *The Subterranean World* (Book 12, Chapter 5, page 332), calls it **Light**, **Balsam of Life**, **Vital Mummy** <note>In this context, "Mummy" (Mumia) refers to a spiritual grease or vital balm that prevents decay and sustains life.</note>; **Natural Heat**, **Congenital Moisture**, the force and vigor of all nature, the principle of motion, **Entelechy** <note>A term from Aristotle meaning the full realization or "actualization" of a potential.</note>, **Sperm of the World**, **Water of the Great Ocean**, the **purest Water of Life**, **Universal Fire**, and the **Chaos and Storehouse of Nature**, from which vital heat and spirit come forth just as they do from seed. It is the **Quintessence** <note>The "Fifth Essence," a substance superior to the four physical elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note> and the **Mercury of Life**, among a thousand other names. So say Pierre-Jean Fabre <note>Pierre-Jean Fabre (1588–1658) was a French alchemist who believed the secrets of nature could be unlocked through "Chymistry."</note> in his *Compendium of Secret Chemistry* (Book 1, Chapter 3); Damianus de Campis in his *Enucleation of the New Chemical Moon*; and Michael Sendivogius <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a world-famous Polish alchemist and chemist, known for his theories on the "food of life" found in the air.</note>, who says: "we can call it a Spirituous-"

# DE MATERIA HERMET.
### ON THE HERMETIC MATTER.

...is called the Balsam of Life <note>original: "sum vitæ Balsamum nennen"</note>; and in the *Explanation of the Dream* <note>original: "Enarrat. Somni"</note>, if not a Pan-sophic <note>"Pansophy" was a 17th-century movement seeking to organize all human knowledge into a single, divine system.</note> one, then at least a Philo-Polymathic <note>A lover of universal learning.</note> one, §. 34.

It is also called the **Green Lion** <note>In alchemy, the "Green Lion" usually represents a potent, acidic mineral solvent or the raw vital energy found in nature.</note> because of the strength by which it can move and penetrate all things: which **Trismegistus** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founding figure of Hermeticism.</note> taught, calling it in the ***Emerald Tablet*** <note>The *Tabula Smaragdina*, a foundation text of alchemy containing the famous phrase "as above, so below."</note> the "strong strength of all strength" <note>original: "totius fortitudinis fortitudinem fortem"</note>. It is called further the **Bird** or **Little Bird of Hermes**, and indeed the most **Universal Mercury**, which—famous under the name of "dry water"—flies about in the common graveyard of the upper and lower worlds to perform the miracles of the whole world; it is called the "hidden food of our life" by the **Cosmopolite** <note>The "Cosmopolite" was the pseudonym of Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist.</note>. For this substance, when captured duly by a light digestion, turns red, and is the father of magnificent refreshments—if its power be turned into earth—according to the testimony of the aforementioned Hermes. Dr. **Johann Tack**, in a letter to Dr. **Johann Daniel Major** on *Infusion Surgery* <note>original: "Chir. infus." referring to the early medical practice of injecting substances into the veins.</note>, page 3.

I could name many here who have mentioned this **Radical Moisture of the World** <note>The "Radical Moisture" (Humidum radicale) was thought to be the fundamental fluid that sustained the life and flexibility of all living things.</note> throughout their writings; among whose number, certainly not the least, is **Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa von Nettesheim** in *Occult Philosophy* (Book 1, Chapter 14). Also **Bernhardus G. Penotus**, then **Gerhard Dorn**, **Papinius** in his dissertation on the *Sympathetic Powder* <note>The "Powder of Sympathy" was a 17th-century remedy believed to heal a wound by being applied to the weapon that caused it.</note>, **Oswald Croll** in *Chymical Basilica*, **Michael Sendivogius** in the *Twelve Treatises*, **Dr. Pierre-Jean Fabre** in *Spagyric Palace*, *Spagyric Hydrography*, in the *Spagyric Storehouse*, the *Pious Chemical Hercules*, and the *Compendium of Chemical Secrets*. Also **Cornelius Drebbel** on the *Nature of the Elements*, **Robert Fludd** (Chapter 5, Book 1) on *Uromantia* <note>The study of urine for medical diagnosis.</note>, **Cesare Riviera** in his treatise *On the Magical World* written in Italian (Book 1), **Johann Poppius** in the *Chymical Guide*, **Dr. Olaus Borrichius** on the *Origin and Progress of Chemistry*, **Johann Veremund Rhumel** in *Chymical Basilica*, **Johann Rudolph Glauber** in *Spagyric Pharmacopoeia*, **Johann Ludwig Gottfried** in *Cosmic Archontology*, and others. Furthermore, **Johann Rothmann** gave an entire commentary on this subject, which he titled *On the Soul of the World and the Spirit of the Universe*.

There is also the book by **Lord Nuyssement** written on the *Universal Spirit of the World* and the *True Secret Salt of the Philosophers*. Likewise, **Dr. Andreas Nitner** on the same *Spirit of the World* drawn from the air, and also the treatise on the *Terrestrial Heaven* by **Wenceslaus Lavinius of Moravia**. Therefore, we refer students of this matter to those volumes.

**Lord Pierre Borel**, in the 1st Century of his *Observations*, Number 6, under the title *Curing Malignant Fever with Dissolved Gold*, teaches the method of preparing a **Magnet** by which this essence can be attracted from the stars and distilled, so that gold may be dissolved with it.

And, to say this much about myself: it was about seven years ago that, by the aid of the **Philosophical Magnet** prepared from **fixed nitre** <note>Fixed Nitre (saltpeter) was believed to be a "magnet" capable of drawing vital spirits out of the atmosphere.</note>, I attracted a certain spirit or Mercurial water from the air,

### CHAPTER III.

Once this substance has been rectified <note>meaning: purified or distilled to its most potent state</note>—as they like to say—the remaining **phlegm** <term>phlegma: the watery, tasteless residue left after distillation</term>, although entirely tasteless like common water, still extracted a red tincture from **corals** <term>corallis: red coral, often used in alchemical preparations for its supposed medicinal and "solar" properties</term>, changing their color to white. We should not rashly assign the cause of this effect to anything other than the volatile Salt of the air.

Therefore, it is placed far beyond any risk of doubt that the sun is the primary body through which celestial matter is rendered palpable and visible. For it is the **Salt of the Earth** <term>Sal Terrae: a philosophical salt, not common table salt, representing the fixed principle in nature</term>, a Virgin who has as of yet produced nothing, into which the Spirit of the World is first converted through **vitrification** <term>vitrificationem: the process of turning a substance into a glass-like or transparent state through heat</term>—that is, the refining or thinning out of moisture. It was undoubtedly in this sense that *Dr. Joel Langelott* (*in his Letter to the Most Excellent Seekers of Nature, page 191*) searched for the unique "Catholic" or universal solvent in the Salt of the air. In my mind, he was quite correct.

Furthermore, I agree with *Nollius*: so that this divine treasure would not seem to descend and humble itself in vain, it impressed a perfect image of itself upon a certain mineral that is cast aside as worthless before the world. This mineral is called the **Magnesia of the wise** <term>Magnesia sapientum: a mysterious "first matter" often described by alchemists as a common, overlooked substance containing the seed of the Philosopher's Stone</term>, and it has been so disposed that, by the singular blessing of GOD and the industry of human wit, the treasure can be drawn out from it with ease.

To speak more plainly, this is: 2. The coagulated lower air, or that magnesia, which is our **nitre** <term>nitrum: saltpeter (potassium nitrate), which alchemists believed contained a "vital spirit" from the atmosphere</term>, or **saltpeter** <term>sal petrae: literally "salt of the rock"</term>, in which that ethereal substance is most abundantly scattered. For there is no Salt at all that is more "catholic" <note>meaning: universal or widespread</note> than this. Indeed, nitre is so diffused through the entire nature of things—and so active in the solidifying and the breath of sublunary mixtures—that anyone who turns their mind and most attentive thought to investigating its character will find it a worthwhile task. *Robert Boyle, Noble Englishman, in his Experiments on Physico-Chemical subjects, page 1.* There is no salt among the number of salts that can be called "living," with the sole exception of our **Virgin Nitre**. And yet even this substance, previously lacking life, received it from the air. This is the most universal Subject of the Philosophers; in it, not only Mercury, but even the golden Sulfur and the Salt of the World are most abundantly present.

But oh, how many voices of contradiction there are! I hear them all here, asserting the opposite against me: they say these ideas reek of that new and—at first glance—flattering opinion of *Johann Rudolph Glauber*, who established that Nitre is that Mercury of the Philosophers and the most universal Menstruum <note>original: "Menstruum" refers to a powerful solvent used in alchemy to break down substances.</note>. However, after Glauber had asserted this for some time in his *Miracle of the World* <note>original: "Mundi Miraculo"</note>, he later exploded <note>meaning: rejected or disproved</note> the idea in his other writings, and began to move toward entirely different views. In the end, **Antimony** <term>Antimonium: a metallic element used in alchemical processes; often personified as a "wolf" that devours other metals</term> pleased him best. Regarding this, he says in his *Secret Fire* (*page 20*) in express words: Antimony is the Spirit of the World, leading the souls of the dead out of the underworld; it is likewise the **Alkahest** <term>Alkahest: the hypothetical "universal solvent" capable of dissolving every other substance into its primary matter</term>, from which that of *Pontanus* and *Artephius*—
