...so that a secret fire can be prepared. Compare the same author’s *Glauber's Proserpina* <note>Johann Rudolph Glauber (1604–1670) was a famous apothecary and chemist; *Proserpina* refers to his work on the "thief" or solvent of metals</note>, page 29.

To these opinions I respond that there is nothing in them which should offend anyone. Especially since they do not contradict one another. For as far as the first is concerned—that a metallic Spirit of the World lies hidden in **antimony** <term>antimonio: a brittle, silvery-white metalloid that alchemists believed contained a "protean" spirit capable of purifying gold</term>—it does not escape me that from this, a universal metallic **Menstruum** <term>menstruum: a solvent or liquid used to extract the essence of a substance</term> could be drawn. Regarding the other opinion: it is indeed well-known from page 91 of *The Wonders of the World* <note>original: "Mirac. Mund." or *Miraculum Mundi*, a key text by Glauber</note> that Glauber was ignorant of the method for preparing the **Philosophers' Mercury** <term>Mercurius Philosophorum: the essential, "living" spirit of matter sought by alchemists, distinct from common liquid mercury</term> from **nitre** <term>nitro: saltpeter, which alchemists believed captured the "spirit of the world" from the air</term>. Nevertheless, it does not for that reason cease to be that matter from which a most universal solvent can be extracted, by whose benefit a universal metallic solvent may afterwards be prepared. 

Beyond these things, we still constantly assert that common **quicksilver** <term>Argentum vivum: literally "living silver," the common liquid metal mercury</term> and Antimony are to be excluded from our work, and not without reason: the latter because of its impotence, and the former because it has already been coagulated into a metallic body by its own internal sulfur. For it is well known that bodies do not act upon bodies. <note>An alchemical axiom meaning solid, "fixed" substances cannot react with one another; they must first be "volatilized" or dissolved into a spiritual state.</note> I know that few will believe my words. But if they were taught by experience just as the Philosophers were, they would know how this kind of **Salt of Stone** <term>Sal petræ: saltpeter or nitre, literally "salt of the rock"</term> is found in all things, and can be skillfully extracted from those same things. Then they would undoubtedly trust their own eyes as witnesses, and would not so lightly cast away such a precious Salt—which is only despised by the ignorant (and therefore called "vile" by the Philosophers)—along with filth, rubbish, and dung, but would rather hold it most sacredly, having collected it from every corner of the world. 

Although I would also wish this to be observed according to *Raimund Lull’s On the Fifth Essence, Distinction 3, page 95* <note>Lull was a 13th-century polymath; his works were cornerstone texts for later alchemists</note>: although the Mercury I spoke of is found in every elemental thing, yet in some it is so far removed that a man's life would expire before the Artist of this art could possess it, abstracted from itself as is required. Indeed, it is said to be in all things by reason of the "first mover" in natural things, which is called the **vegetable spirit** <term>spiritus vegetabilis: the vital life-force or growth principle that animates the natural world</term>, in which our matter abounds above all others. (As noted by *Bernard Penotus à Portu in the Preface to his Apology in the Tract of Arislaeus, page 92; Sendivogius in the Tract on Sulfur, page 185; and Dr. Johannes Tackius in the Chrysogonia on the Fifth Essence of Wine, page 32.*)

Meanwhile, we cannot help but marvel at that power and virtue—altogether stupendous—with which the divine goodness has endowed the Spirit of the World and that salt clinging to the air. For despite the thousands of subjects existing in the world to which it is joined or attracted in a magnetic fashion, it does not change them or make them something other than what they were; rather, it uniquely acts so that it becomes one and the same with them. This truly is a thing of such great value, that he who

### CHAPTER III.

...by which, as they like to say, [the spirit] being rectified, the remaining **phlegm** <term>phlegma: a watery, tasteless distillate produced during chemical operations</term>, even if completely tasteless like common water, extracted a red tincture from corals, changing their color to white; the cause of which we should, not without reason, assign to nothing other than the **volatile Salt of the air** <term>sal aëris: a vital mineral spirit believed to be suspended in the atmosphere</term>.

Therefore, it is placed far beyond any risk of doubt that the sun is the primary body through which celestial matter is rendered palpable and visible. For Salt is the Earth, the Virgin, who has produced nothing yet, into which the **Spirit of the World** <term>Spiritus Mundi: the universal life-force that alchemists sought to "capture" in their substances</term> is first converted through vitrification—that is, the thinning out of moisture. In this sense, *Dr. Joel Langelottus* (in his *Letter to the Most Excellent Seekers of Nature* <note>original: "Epistola ad Præcellentissimos Naturæ Curiosos"</note>, page 191) was undoubtedly correct when he identified the Salt of the air as the unique **Universal Solvent** <term>solvens Catholicum: a legendary "all-dissolver" capable of reducing any solid to its liquid essence</term>. Most rightly so, in my opinion.

Furthermore, I believe along with *Nollius* <note>Heinrich Nollius (c. 1580–1626), a German physician and hermetic philosopher</note>: that this divine treasure, lest it should seem to descend and humble itself in vain, has impressed a perfect image of itself upon a certain mineral that is cast aside by the world, which is called the **Magnesia of the Wise** <term>Magnesia sapientum: a symbolic term for the "magnetic" starting material of the alchemical work, often identified with nitre or crude ores</term>, and has so disposed it that, with God’s singular blessing and the industry of human wit, it can be drawn out from there with little difficulty.

To speak more plainly, it is (2) the coagulated lower air, or that magnesia, our **nitre** <note>original: "nitrum"</note>, or saltpeter, in which that ethereal substance is most abundantly disseminated. For there is absolutely no Salt more universal than it. Indeed, nitre is so diffused through the entire nature of things, and so active in the formations and states of earthly mixtures, that anyone who turns his mind and most attentive thought to investigating its nature will find it worth the effort. *Robert Boyle* <note>1627–1691, the famous Irish natural philosopher often called the "Father of Chemistry"</note>, *English Nobleman, in his Physico-Chemical Essays, page 1.* Among the number of Salts, there is none that can be called "living," except for our **Virgin Nitre**. Yet even this, previously lacking life, received it from the air. This is the most universal Subject of the Philosophers; in which not only **Mercury**, but also the **Golden Sulfur** and the **Salt of the World** are most abundantly present.

But oh, I ask, how many objectors there are! I hear all those asserting against me here: that these ideas reek of that new and at first glance flattering opinion of *Johann Rudolph Glauber*: he who established that Nitre is that Mercury of the Philosophers and the most universal solvent. This opinion, which he asserted for some time in *The Wonder of the World* <note>original: "Mundi Miraculo"</note>, he later rejected in his other writings, and began instead to move toward completely different things; in the end, **Antimony** <term>antimonium: a metallic substance that Glauber eventually championed as the true "Spirit of the World"</term> pleased him. Regarding this, in his *Secret Fire* <note>original: "Igne Secreto"</note>, page 20, he says in explicit words: Antimony is the Spirit of the World, leading the souls of the dead out of the underworld; it is likewise the **Alkahest** <term>Alkahest: the hypothetical universal solvent sought by Paracelsian alchemists</term>, from which [the works] of *Pontanus* and *Artephius*—

A fundamental alchemical principle stating that substances must be reduced to a spiritual or liquid state before they can react or transform.</note>. Few, I know, will trust my words. <meta>This page continues the argument for identifying Nitre (saltpeter) as the primary universal matter of the alchemists. ### CHAPTER III.

...should penetrate [these secrets], I have not yet seen, nor perhaps will it ever happen that those who do penetrate will see them. Therefore, following Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundation of alchemy is attributed.</note>, we conclude that there is one Universal matter: *So, all things were born from this one thing.* <note>original: "Sic, ab hac una re natas omnes fieri." This is a core tenet of the *Emerald Tablet*, the foundational text of Hermeticism.</note>

And although it is not the purpose of this place or project to accumulate the authorities of the ancient Philosophers to bolster what we have said—for anyone is free to believe what they wish—I cannot, however, refrain from calling upon *Basilius Valentine* <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemical author, now believed to be a pseudonym used by the publisher Johann Thölde. His works bridge medieval alchemy and early modern chemistry.</note>, and him alone. Thus he speaks in the final chapter of his *Book on Natural Things* <note>original: "libr. de Reb. Natur. c. ult."</note>, abundantly laying out the matter of the inner root before our eyes:

> *This matter,* he says, *is manifest before the eyes of all men. Yet, because its virtue, power, and force are buried very deep within, and are thus unknown to most, it happens that this matter is considered worthless and unsuitable through ignorance. Its name is called **Hermes**, who carries the flying serpent as his emblem, and has for a wife her who is called **Aphrodite**, who knows the hearts of all mortals; and yet all is one, and a single thing, uniquely, and a unique essence, which is common in all lands and known in all places. Anyone can grasp it with their hands and use it for lowly tasks. The ignorant person considers the precious to be vile and rejects the precious. In short, he says it is nothing else than **water and fire**, from which the earth was born with the addition of air, and by which it is still preserved.*

The same is echoed in his *Treatise on the Great Stone of the Philosophers*, where toward the end, he explains what this "all in all" truly is.

But you might ask: "Who will teach me that Valentine intended to imply **nitre** <term>Nitre: also known as saltpeter (potassium nitrate), essential for making gunpowder and historically viewed as a 'miracle salt' containing celestial energy</term> by the name of *Hermes*?" Truly, nothing could be easier for me; indeed, *Schröder* <note>Johann Schröder (1600–1664), a German physician whose *Pharmacopoeia* was a standard medical reference for over a century.</note> agrees with me. Grant me a moment, and—lest such a small effort should trouble you, Reader—come with me to the same *Schröder's Medico-Chemical Pharmacopoeia, book 3, chapter 23*. There, among those names with which the Ancients honored nitre, I will show you the name of **Hermes**. This matter is that **Virgin**, to whom the name **Uranogaea** <term>Uranogaea: From the Greek 'Ouranos' (Heaven) and 'Gaia' (Earth), meaning a substance that is both terrestrial and celestial in origin</term> is given, or that of the "terrified heavens," because although it exists as earth (that is, a Salt), it nevertheless contains the virtues of the heavens and holds them connected to itself by an indissoluble bond, whose miraculous operations it displays for all to see.

But if the genealogy of this substance still makes you anxious, there is someone who can again satisfy your desire: *P. Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who wrote extensively on everything from volcanoes to Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> in *The Subterranean World, volume 1, book 6, section 2, chapter 3*. To him you may add *Quercetanus* <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a French physician and chemist who championed the use of mineral-based medicines.</note> in his *Spagyric Pharmacopoeia, pages 578 and following*, as well as the *History of the Royal Society of London, part 2, section 18, page 319*, and *William Clark* in his *Natural History of Nitre, London, 1670*. Also *Johann Rudolph Glauber*...

He provides a list of scholarly references, including Athanasius Kircher and the Royal Society, to support the chemical importance of nitre.</summary>

<header>ON THE MATTER OF HERMES.</header>

<margin>Rudolf Glauber in *Miracles of the World* concerning the Universal Solvent.</margin> Regarding experiments concerning the various and manifold parts of nitre, read the *Physical-Chemical Essay of Master Robert Boyle, Noble Englishman, page 18.* <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691) was a foundational figure in modern chemistry; the author here cites his experiments to lend scientific weight to alchemical theories.</note>

Indeed, why do I light a torch for so evident a matter? Since the things found in *Paracelsus*—that he found **Alchemy in Nitre**—are certainly more illustrious than the rays of the Sun. Nor are those of *Geber* <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the "father of chemistry," though many Latin works under this name are by "Pseudo-Geber," a 13th-century European author.</note> otherwise: *The Stone which is not a stone, which is found in dung heaps, that is the Stone of the Philosophers.* To which *Dominicus Gnosius the Belgian* harmonizes in *Hermes Trismegistus, chapter 3, page 167*: *Our Stone is saltpeter, by which that which runs is transmuted into the best and most perfect metal, and flint into the hardest Diamond.* And the *Work of Peter de Silento* says: *The best Salt of Nitre is our Mercury, see Theatrum Chemicum, Volume 4, page 1117.* <note>The *Theatrum Chemicum* was a massive 17th-century compendium of alchemical texts.</note>

Indeed, I know how much some attribute to the Salt of **May Dew**, to which they believe the birth of Philosophical Nitre is owed (which is considered to be "coagulated air" and is expressly called the **"Nothing of the Philosophers"** <note>original: "Nihilum Philosophorum"</note> by the brother *Umanus*). This kind of Salt, prepared by a singular artifice by the English Knight, *Henshaw* <note>Thomas Henshaw (1618–1700), a founding member of the Royal Society.</note>, when viewed with a Microscope, equaled the angles of Nitre in the number and figure of its angles; as the French reports relate from the *English Journals, Volume 11, page 23.* Indeed, *Jan Baptista van Helmont* <note>A pioneer of chemistry and medicine who first recognized "gas" as a state of matter.</note> was taught that dew is rich in sweet sugar in *On the Image of Ferment, page 73*; and from the Salt of May Dew (indeed, from rainwater) *Master Johannes Dienheimius* elaborated his universal medicine; and with the Spirit of the same, *Borellus* radically dissolved Gold. We join to these *Detardingius*, whose constant assertion in his *Treatise on Potable Gold* is: that by means of the volatile Salt of May dew, he dissolved Gold, just as ice is dissolved in warm water.

But, to confess the truth of the matter, you would obtain this in vain in Germany from that Dew, which is evidently less fertile in the "Salt of the World" than in other regions, especially Egypt. This is testified by *Pierre Belon in Singular and Memorable Observations, book 2, chapter 77, as well as the author De la Chambre in his dissertation on the cause of the Nile's inundation*: that in the Dew, which the Egyptians call the **"Drop"** <note>original: "Guttam"</note>, there lies hidden a fermenting force, which, when the mass is exposed to the air, ferments the Nile itself, or rather the nitre that is in the Nile. So too is Brazilian Dew, according to the geographer *Varenius*; it is then fatter than the European kind and much impregnated with Nitre, more fecund, and much more penetrating and tenacious, especially in summer. Likewise, in the Faroe Islands, there is much fecundity from the snow, which carries Nitre with it, as reported by *Master Thomas Bartholin in Medical and Philosophical Acts of Copenhagen, years 1671 and 1672, page 87.* Interposing my own opinion, I freely profess: our nitre, found everywhere, however impure it may be, is nevertheless most full of celestial matter, and is the same true, universal, and miraculous subject.

<header>CHAPTER IV.</header>

...is Salt; from which not only a liquid and universal philosophical solvent—or **Spirit of Mercury** <term>Spiritus Mercurii: A volatile, acidic essence believed by alchemists to be the key to breaking down metals into their primary components.</term>—but also, more directly, the **Medicine of the ancient sages** can be prepared and fashioned. It should be noted in the meantime: although its Spirit is drawn out from that water by a strong fire, mixing and dwelling within that same water, that thick and bodily (or earthly) spirit is not yet that most secret and life-giving spirit of heaven (whose Father is the fiery Sun and whose Mother is the moist Moon, according to our *Hermes* <note>original: "Hermetem nostrum"; a reference to the *Emerald Tablet* of Hermes Trismegistus, which describes the dual solar and lunar origins of the "One Thing."</note>). Instead, this elemental Spirit is the dwelling place of that celestial spirit which is called the soul and life of all creatures, and here most closely bears the image of GOD. Therefore, just as GOD dwells in a fiery essence and is a consuming fire, so too this Spirit—which, as the soul giving life to all things, dwells hidden in the fiery and most burning Spirit—is also a fire consuming all earthly things, yet purifying celestial things to the highest degree. And thus the water distilled from Nitre has three parts: the **Body**, which is the water; that **thick, elemental fiery Spirit**; and finally the **hidden, life-giving soul**, which is incorporated into the proper subject.

Those ignorant of this method may be content to know the celestial matter attracted from the air. Nevertheless, according to the testimony of *Master Nitner and Hoffmann* <note>Johannes Nitner and Friedrich Hoffmann were prominent 17th-century physicians who wrote on the medicinal uses of salts.</note>, although the same substance, once circulated and distilled into a Spirit, may greatly comfort the heart and cure all kinds of fevers and diseases arising from foul dregs, it is still impossible for that most secret Spirit—whether attracted from the air or dug out from its own **Magnesia** <term>Magnesia: In this context, the "primordial matter" or "mother" substance from which the alchemist extracts the spirit, rather than the modern mineral.</term>—to perform those wonders in curing diseases that might strike anyone with admiration, unless it is joined and fixed either with its own body (that is, the purest Salt), or with the **Gold of the Air**, or with **metallic Gold** itself. Regarding this matter, we shall now treat in what follows.

### CHAPTER IV.

->*Concerning GOLD in the AIR.*<-

It is clear, however, from the philosophy attained and the treasury of Nature, and it is agreed among all who have recorded memories of Gold, that there are altogether three distinct kinds of it: first the **astral**, then the **elemental**, and finally the **metallic**. Consult on this matter *Master Johann Tackius's Tripartite Bundle of Medicinal Books concerning Gold, page 11.* <note>Johann Tackius (1617–1675) was a court physician and professor who wrote extensively on iatrochemistry and alchemical medicine.</note> Setting those others aside, we speak now only of the **Astral**. I know you will wonder, and not without reason, why I seek it first in the **Aura** (or upper air), and from where it was found and the method of attract...

-># ON GOLD IN THE AIR <-

...to be grasped, about which not even a single word has been explained by any of the authors, so far as I know, nor has it hitherto become known to me; but there are no long delays. Take heed, I will teach you in a few words.

(I.) I considered that the highest Creator placed the virtue of manifold power especially in the Sun, which is like the Heart of the heavens. This power is an invisible fire and life-giving heat, permeating all created things, in which all things both celestial and terrestrial are quickened and preserved in their being. Indeed, the Sun is the Father of the <term>Spirit of the world</term> <note>original: "Spiritus mundi"; the universal life force or "breath" believed by alchemists to flow from the stars and sustain all life on Earth.</note>, or of celestial matter, and even of the <term>Universal Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphuris universalis"; in alchemical theory, "Sulfur" is the active, masculine principle of combustion and growth, not merely the chemical element.</note>. For the sun acts upon water through its rays, which it thins and, by thinning, turns into air. Nor does it stop in this work, but continues and turns the aerial vapors into fire, by continuously fostering and cooking them, and then converting them into the most subtle nature. Thus, if afterwards <term>Native Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphur nativum"; the internal, natural heat of a substance.</note> (understand this as heat) is ignited in water by the **Sulfur** or heat of the Sun, fires of various kinds emerge from the <term>cohesion</term> <note>original: "coagmentatione"; the joining or binding together of different elements into a solid mass.</note> of that matter. Thus **common Sulfur** was first water, whose nature was cold, which was afterwards turned into air through circulation by the heat of the Sun—the nature of which is hot and dry—with which the earth later tempers itself; and thus **Sulfur** is produced: *Egidius de Vadis in the Dialogue between Nature and the Son of Philosophy, chapter 5.*

(II.) I knew that <term>Natural Gold</term> <note>original: "Aurum naturale"; a celestial or "astral" form of gold that exists as an essence before it solidifies into the metal we find in the earth.</note> is the Son of the Sun, since it is of the same nature as the ethereal matter, or rather it is nothing other than celestial matter which has taken on the nature of a body in some pure place before it was reduced to a perfect <term>fixation</term> <note>original: "fixionem"; the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated.</note>. Regarding this, *Raymond Lull*, in his *Treatise on the Intentions of Workers*, very elegantly wove together its genealogy. See now *Sir Johann Christoph Steeb’s Elixir of the Sun and of Life, section 7*, concerning which nothing is taught more learnedly regarding the generation of the Sun.

(III.) I had come across a passage in *Sir Kenelm Digby's Demonstration of the Immortality of the Rational Soul, chapter 7, page 62*: that by the help of glass vessels, made in a certain peculiar way and artfully arranged, collected sun rays were precipitated into a powder of a dusky color, or a purple tending toward red. To which he afterwards adds: **"No fraud could underlie this operation; for nothing was contained in the vessel before they were arranged; it must also be carried out in the warmer season of the year so that this effect follows. Furthermore, by this operation, nearly two ounces could be collected in a few days,"** etc. From all these things, I began to conjecture; although it is different from what we read produced in the same *Digby’s Theater of Sympathy, page 27*: that <term>Material Light</term> <note>original: "Lucem materialem"; the controversial theory that light is not just a quality, but a physical, corporeal substance that can be captured.</note> is a substance and corporeal...

# CHAPTER IV.

...to this view Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering chemist and physician who bridged the gap between alchemy and modern science.</note> approaches not unwillingly, and proves it by the example of the shining fern. Because it shines, it surely receives this from the sun; and finally he adds: that light is truly an entity existing outside of light <note>original: "ens extra lucem"</note>. Even if these ideas, I say, were further from the truth, it is nonetheless impossible but that the most excellent **sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and soul-like principle of a substance, not just the chemical element.</note> is present in the heat of the Sun, carried down to us through the air. For Tackius, in his *Treatise on the Genealogy of Gold* <note>original: "de Genealogiâ Auri, Tractatu"; Johann Tackius (1617–1675) was a German physician and alchemist.</note>, establishes: that the Spirit which we breathe in and drink down daily along with the air for our greatest benefit, is most full of that red **Tincture**. For there is in the Sun a **Balsam** <note>A "Balsam" in this context refers to a life-preserving, medicinal essence or "marrow" of a substance.</note> most excellent of all, free from all corruption; if you know how to extract this from the Sun and learn how to apply it to the human balsam, you will acquire for yourself the noblest medicine of all: *Henricus Nollius in his Introduction to Hermetic Physics, Chapter 6, page 37* <note>original: "Henr. Noll. in Prod. Phys. Herm. 6. Cap. p. 37."; Nollius was a 17th-century follower of Paracelsus.</note>. With him I finally began to conclude: the Heat of the Sun, or its Balsam, is imparted to the lower bodies by the benefit of its rays. If you are able to grasp that heat with a created **Magnet** <note>The "Magnet" refers to a prepared chemical substance (often a salt like Nitre) intended to "attract" the spiritual essence from the atmosphere.</note> and draw it out from there, you will prepare for yourself a treasure a thousand times more precious than gold and silver, to whose strength all diseases are forced to yield. In this same opinion are Oswald Croll and Croll himself in the *Admonitory Preface* prefixed to his *Royal Chemistry* <note>original: "Ofuvaldus & ipse Crollius in Præfat. Admonit. suæ Basil. Chym. præfix."; Oswald Croll was a major proponent of iatrochemistry (chemical medicine).</note>:

> To those wishing, he says, to bring the life of man to the height of health, it will be necessary to seek not the Elemental Sun, but that celestial heat of the Sun and Moon, dwelling in a more incorruptible substance to be found beneath the sphere of the Moon: to make this heat similar to our own heat or spirit, which happens when it has been prepared into a medicine and a most sweet food.

When I had learned these things and previously worked through them in my mind, I began further to sink into this thought: Whether 

(IV.) The Magnetism I mentioned—which, when placed in the air, especially at the time of the equinoxes (when the air overflows, as it were, with ethereal and balsamic atoms), attracts that celestial matter in a moist form—might also likewise attract the **Golden Sulfur** in a dry form through the breeze from the solar rays?

Therefore, having placed the fire <note>Likely referring to a gentle heat or a specific chemical "fire."</note> beneath that Magnet which we have already mentioned several times, and doing so under a clear sky in the most open air, it immediately began to act and work according to its nature. This "Gold of the Breeze" stood revealed to the investigator through so many and such great experiments and labors.

This, therefore, I call the **Solar Balsam**, or the **Life-giving Sulfur**, attracted from the air by the power of the universal magnet; it preserves health above all else, wonderfully delivering and protecting both the parts and the humors of our body from corruption and rot.

For, because this sulfur undoubtedly flows out and influences us from the celestial Sun (that golden source of Light and vital vigor), I have dared to call it Gold—

## CHAPTER V.

->Regarding the universal Magnet, attracting Gold<-

Many have devised many ways by which they might acquire for themselves the Spirit, or (as we mentioned in Chapter 3), the Salt of the World <note>original: "Sal Mundi"</note>, from the upper air. Some diligent men indeed acted superstitiously, so far as I can judge; others, whom it is safer to follow, proceeded by a middle path from the opposite direction.

I have in my possession a certain old German manuscript <note>original: "liber MSC. Germanicus"</note>, formerly shared with me by Ludwig Gottfried Otthmar. Whoever that author is, he believes, among other things, that if the weather be clear, that celestial matter can be collected by some boy of most commendable beauty, twelve or at most sixteen years of age, in flat places filled with a sweetness of odors and facing the rising Sun, by drawing in the air with the mouth and then breathing it out into some very large glass vessel, as much as is sufficient of that soul or Spirit; a method which **Basil Valentine** best suggests in his *Fifth Key* <note>Referring to the "Twelve Keys of Basil Valentine," a famous series of alchemical allegories.</note>.

# Chapter V.

...Philosophical <note>The text begins with the suffix of "Philosophica," continuing from the previous page's discussion of Philosophical Gold/Mercury.</note>, (as prescribed by J. R. H., Johann Rist <note>Johann Rist (1607–1667) was a German poet and dramatist who also wrote extensively on alchemy and "the secret of the salts."</note>, from a communication by Dr. Joachim Morsius <note>Joachim Morsius was a famed scholar and traveler known for his vast collection of alchemical and Rosicrucian manuscripts.</note> of the Library), will undoubtedly attract the spirit by magnetic force, if you first properly macerate steel filings with flints reduced to powder, and the juice extracted from river crayfish.

But there are also those ideas of another Anonymous author, which must now likewise be brought forward. He commands that crystalline hailstones, which sometimes fall in summer, be enclosed in glass mixed with Sal Ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride; used in alchemy to "open" metals and assist in the sublimation of spirits.</note>. For it will happen that this matter, being magnetic, will draw out salt from the air, which will adhere to the outside of the glass. Let this be cast onto molten silver, and from that same impregnated metal, let a concave globe with a stopper be made. If you then draw out the air enclosed within its mouth, he says you will have a suitable instrument by which you may lure forth the Spirit of the World <note>original: "Spiritum Mundi"; the vital, life-giving essence that alchemists believed permeated the atmosphere.</note> in the form of water.

*Orthelius* <note>Andreas Orthelius, a commentator on alchemical works in the mid-1600s.</note> must now also be called to our side; who, in his commentary on *Michael Sendivogius' Chemical Light, p. m. 43*, says he has seen hydraulic globes of various sizes fashioned from gold, silver, bronze, and brass. With these, even in a heated room, it was possible without delay to attract water of an extremely cold nature and very rare virtue and character, and to return it immediately on a slope.

Next is *Barnerus* <note>Jacob Barner (1641–1686), a chemist and physician who sought to bridge the gap between traditional alchemy and early modern chemistry.</note>, whose method is this: A pear-shaped glass, closed at the tip but with a small open hole at the base, is filled with finely crushed ice or snow. Expose this to the more perceptible solar rays, especially during the dog days <note>The "dog days" (canicula) are the hottest days of summer, associated with the rising of the star Sirius, which alchemists believed amplified the Sun's power.</note> when the sun's heat is more intense; thus the nitrous salt adheres to the sides through <term>antiperistasis <gloss>a philosophical concept where one quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, such as internal heat increasing when surrounded by external cold</gloss></term>. As *Dr. J. David Porez* reports in his *Chemical Anatomy of Rhenish Wine, p. m. 5*, that famous "Bird of Hermes" <note>A symbolic term in alchemy usually referring to the volatile spirit or the process of sublimation; "capturing the bird" means fixing a volatile substance into a stable form.</note> is itself captured and tamed when a parabolic mirror is exposed to the solar rays. By some, the rim of a concave mirror is likewise smeared with some magnetic material. When this is further committed to the open, dry air through the night, the Salt of Nature could be scraped off with a feather in the morning without any trouble.

There is also a peculiar method of making a bronze vessel in the shape of a flame. Especially if you place a sponge with marble inside it, *Staricius* <note>Johannes Staricius, an alchemist active in the early 17th century, known for his "Thesaurus" of secrets.</note> believes that even the air itself can be drawn in. These details are more long-winded than should be repeated here. Therefore, the author himself must be approached and consulted in his so-called *Treasury of Heroes*.

*Burrus* <note>Giuseppe Francesco Borri (1627–1695), a flamboyant Italian alchemist, adventurer, and knight who traveled through the courts of Europe.</note>, a man of the knightly order and very well known throughout Germany, is said to have fished for water from the air in the following way: He took a glass vessel, wide at the top

### ON THE UNIVERSAL MAGNET.

...shaped like a bowl, but gradually tapering and closed below. Into this glass he placed a great quantity of Mercury <note>original: "Argenti vivi," literally "living silver"</note> or ice (refer to Dr. Friedrich Hoffmann in his work *On the Method of Healing*, Book 1, Chapter 19). Placing another glass below the bottom point as a receiver, he situated it in a deep cavern of the earth during the height of summer; having done this, by the coldness of the Mercury (or ice), the warm air was resolved into water and began to flow down the outside of the glass walls into the vial placed beneath.

What further? Expose black calcined flints to the air while they are still hot, and they will most abundantly attract humidity once their barriers are loosened. Distill this through a retort and expose the residue <note>original: "caput" (caput mortuum), the dregs or remains left after distillation</note> once more to the air to attract more moisture. If you dissolve Saltpetre <note>original: "Sal Nitrum"</note> in this moisture and coagulate it, the resulting substance will acquire such strength that it will explode with double or triple the force than before its impregnation—though this process must be repeated several times. (Dr. Friedrich Hoffmann in his *Dissertation on the Method of Healing*, page 267.) We have heard of two men in London in the year 1658, including a Mr. Kuffler <note>Johannes Sibertus Kuffler (1595–1677), an inventor and chemist associated with the famous Drebbel family</note>, who calcined flints in this manner using the rays of the sun by means of a burning mirror <note>original: "speculi caustici," a concave mirror used to focus sunlight for intense heat</note>. When they exposed these flints, contained in a funnel, to the open air on a high place like a mountain or a tower, they too attracted a vast amount of water from the air through magnetic power; this water was red before sunrise and white once the sun had risen. Johann Morian of Arnhem communicated this experiment in a letter to Dr. Friedrich Clodius <note>Friedrich Clodius was a chemist and son-in-law to Samuel Hartlib, a central figure in 17th-century scientific communication</note> dated April 20, 1658, of which a copy of the principal excerpts has been provided to us.

Fabricius Bartoletti <note>An Italian physician and chemist (1576–1630) who was one of the first to describe milk sugar</note> invented the "Spirit-Catcher" <term>Pneumatolabium: an apparatus designed to capture and condense the vital spirits or humidity from the air or breath</term> (of which I also have an illustration to show). The lower glass tube of this instrument, hanging from the middle of the glass, is to be buried in a heap of snow. Afterward, the breath from the mouth of a healthy and well-nourished man is to be introduced through one of the nozzles, as if through a glass artery—not by forceful exhaling or blowing, but by the simple contraction of the chest. Thus, the breath pushed through and circulated through the glass coils is distilled into water by the mastery of the cold <note>The snow acts as a cooling agent to condense the breath</note>. We place this liquid in an open glass urinal <note>A common wide-necked laboratory vessel</note> under the open sky; as it slowly evaporates, it thickens into whitish crystals spread out like hairs. See the aforementioned book by Bartoletti, *On Shortness of Breath*.

Regarding the "Fiery Chamber of Mars" <note>likely a specialized iron vessel or furnace used for alchemical attraction</note> for attraction, read the aforementioned Johann Christoph Steeb in his *Elixir of the Sun and of Life*, section 42.

### CHAPTER V.

**Raphael Eglinus** <note>Raphael Eglinus (1559–1622) was a Swiss theologian and alchemist known for his works on the "Table of Hermes."</note> proceeded very differently. Placing a huge glass retort <note>original: "Retortam," a glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distillation</note> in glowing sand and constantly blowing upon it with bellows, he finally achieved a result where it appeared as if covered all over with little droplets. Having sealed its mouth very tightly, and placing it in a Water Bath <note>original: "Balneo Mariæ," a method of heating a container by placing it in boiling water</note> day and night for an entire week, it yielded a thin mercury.

It is also said within our memory that there was someone—citing *Wilhelm Christoph Kriegsmann* in *Taaut*, page 73 <note>Wilhelm Christoph Kriegsmann (1633–1679) was a scholar who attempted to link ancient Phoenician religion to alchemy; Taaut refers to the god Thoth.</note>—who obtained Mercury for himself by means of an "animated machine."

You will not be indignant, Reader, if I mention here in a few words the sulfur globe <note>original: "globô sulphureô"</note> made by me last year according to the instructions of **Mr. Otto von Guericke** <note>Otto von Guericke (1602–1686), the Mayor of Magdeburg, was a famous physicist who invented the vacuum pump and this sulfur globe, which was the first electrostatic generator.</note>, Noble and Councilor of Magdeburg.

This globe is endowed with more than just one power. For besides being strong in impulsive, conservative, and expulsive forces, as well as in sounding, heating, and finally glowing, it also has this peculiar quality: that in cold weather, when the moist air is drawn in, if it is grasped with both hands, it gives out a very great cracking sound, as if it were about to burst on the spot.

Therefore, from these methods—of which we have now cited more than one—I would wish the fair Reader to judge which is to be preferred over the other. For these individual methods are not so easily compared that they would prove themselves and commend themselves equally to everyone. For my part, if I may now freely express what I feel and confess it without prejudice to anyone, as far as the true universal Magnet is concerned, I would not object if someone gave the first prize to the Polish **Noble, Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and diplomat.</note>, or more truly to **Seton** <note>Alexander Seton (died c. 1604), known as "The Cosmopolite," whose alchemical secrets Sendivogius was said to have inherited.</note>, to whom the *New Chemical Light* <note>original: "Novum Chymicum Lumen," one of the most influential alchemical texts of the 17th century</note> must be entirely attributed. I myself do not deny credit to him when he writes in the Riddle which he annexed: 

> *Air is the matter of the ancient Philosophers. This is the water of our dew, from which is drawn the saltpeter of the Philosophers, by which all things grow and are nourished. And it is our Magnet, which in the preceding I called Steel* <note>original: "Chalybem," often used alchemically to refer to a "magnetic" or attractive principle in minerals</note>: *N.B. Air generates the Magnet. But the Magnet generates or makes our air appear.*

For we know by chemical doctrine <note>original: "Chymica doctrinâ"</note> that Salt is produced in all parts of the world by the natural heat of the world itself; which, while it acts upon the moisture itself by consuming it so that it may be nourished by it, dries out that same moisture. However, that which is dried out within the natural moisture itself is natural salt, and the natural essence of every thing, since there is nothing that cannot be resolved into it. By this reasoning and method, I have experienced that in Salt—which is like a universally solidified spirit—there exists a certain incombustible or secret element of fire, which performs the same actions as the primitive fire.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Universal Magnet," moving from the physical phenomena of Otto von Guericke’s sulfur globe (mentioned at the end of the previous page) to the chemical theory of how substances can be reduced to a "magnetic" salt. It bridges the gap between the vegetable and mineral kingdoms through the concept of a shared "fermentative principle."</meta>

# ON THE UNIVERSAL MAGNET.

Hence, the transmutation of plants into minerals must certainly be established as not impossible; nor should the production of plants and minerals from one and the same fermentative principle <note>The "fermentative principle" refers to a vital spark or catalyst that alchemists believed directed the growth and form of all matter, whether organic or inorganic.</note>, distinguished only by a different disposition, be doubted. *This is something Mr. Filippo Talducci della Casa, General Overseer of military architecture for the Kingdom of Bohemia, demonstrates in his "Physico-Metallurgical Observation" and confirms with necessary experiments, as reported by Dr. Philipp Jakob Sachs von Löwenheim in the notes to Observation 113 of the Miscellanea Curiosa, Year 2.* For plants do not differ from minerals, except that the former are volatile, while the latter are more fixed—and some, like Gold and Silver <note>original: "Sol & Luna," literally Sun and Moon, the standard alchemical symbols for Gold and Silver</note>, are the most fixed of all. *Georg Horn in "The Ark of Moses," page 29.*

From all of this, I have now concluded: if the external form of any body whatsoever is mortified <note>In alchemy, "mortification" is the process of destroying the outward appearance or "life" of a substance (often through heat or decay) to release its inner essence</note>, and then reduced to the form of universality, which is saline, it will become a Magnet. This is nothing other than a fixed Salt, or <term>Alkali</term> <note>Alkali: A substance, often derived from plant ashes, that reacts with acids; here viewed as the "fixed" or stable foundation of matter</note>, in its highest state of purification.

Just as all salts can be converted into "burning saltpeter" <note>original: "Nitrum ardens," likely referring to refined potassium nitrate, which was central to theories of a "life-force" in the air</note> through fire and the atmosphere—indeed, burning saltpeter itself perpetually regains its "life" from the air after being consumed by fire—so too, when it becomes the royal offspring of the Sun, or even the "Egyptian Bird of the Sun" <note>likely a reference to the Phoenix, symbolizing the process of distillation and rebirth</note>, it is the finest of all things. If the choice were mine, I would judge that substance to be the best subject from which, by the power of charcoal dust or on its own, saltpeter can be fixed in fire and that universal Magnet most easily prepared. However, even if I do not rank them above it, I certainly do not consider <term>potash</term> <note>original: "cineres clavellatos," literally "clubbed ashes," a common source of potassium carbonate used in glass and soap making</note> to be second to it; their power is manifest, and by everyone's testimony, they are already fixed by fire. For these, after melting and purification, can be converted into the purest and most shining Salt. Indeed, as I can testify from my own experience more than once, they can be changed back into natural saltpeter in a short time by the benefit of the air. For all Alkalis seem to represent a type of the "Philosophers' Chaos" <note>The "Chaos" refers to the *Prima Materia*, the raw, unformed matter that contains the potential for all things</note>, in which, they say, their Mercury <note>The "Mercury of the Philosophers" is the spiritual essence or "living" part of a metal or salt</note> is brought back to life; this is what Tachenius holds regarding the Alkali of Tartar in *Hippocrates Chimicus*, page 60.

These and similar Magnetic Salts, once reduced to the form of universality, do not only attract water of a most pleasant taste from the air—within which is the Spirit and Salt of the world—but they also extract a most pure <term>Tincture</term> <note>Tincture: A concentrated essence or "soul" of a substance, often capable of transforming other materials</note> from that mineral which I mentioned above as "Hessian." This includes Piedmontese Magnesia, Antimony, common Sulfur, and both ordinary and bituminous coals. This Tincture is not at all foreign to our intended goal, whether it be the Gold or the Sulfur that is brought down to us daily by the benefit of the solar rays.

### CHAPTER V.

But neither is the (2) lower Air—that is, Nitre—merely a "lower Gold"; it is also our most universal Magnet. It draws the hidden Hermetic Gold from both the (1) upper and (2) lower air (that is, from itself). For this reason, this same "Golden Magnet" is spoken of by *Basil* <note>Referring to Basil Valentine, a legendary alchemical figure traditionally associated with the discovery of antimony and various acids.</note>; because (3) to it alone, and to no other, must the dissolution, coagulation, and fixation of all metals be attributed. Indeed, the writings of most authors we know on this subject are covered in layers and are hardly accessible. We shall strive for clarity as much as possible, and we will not delay the worthy Reader with anything regarding ourselves; we are little concerned with the unworthy, whom, as God is the judge, we are fully confident will either not understand what they read, or if they should wish to apply what they have understood, will meet with little success.

Therefore, (*first*) there is within Nitre <note>Nitrum: historically refers to saltpeter (potassium nitrate), which alchemists viewed as a "celestial salt" containing the life-force of the atmosphere.</note> in general a certain distinguished and notable power which brings things to pass. Because of this, it is called not only Magnesia (in his 4th *Treatise*) by *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist who proposed that a "food of life" existed in the air.</note>, but now "Magnet," and now "Steel," with no distinction made between them (*Treatise 9 and in the Epilogue*). For it arises in the beginning from a certain stony or mined Salt, which is the "Magnet" or attractor to him; and it is also born from an aerial and fiery salt: which by comparison is the "iron" or "steel" and the thing attracted to it. The witnesses are not only the mines from which Nitre is dug, but also certain stony places where it is generated. I too can offer my own testimony, if needed; quite recently, I extracted a certain "burning Nitre" in great abundance from a certain "martial" <note>Martial: related to Mars, the alchemical symbol for iron.</note> stone dug up at *Hayna*. When dissolved and coagulated, it flowed in the air, not unlike a yellow oil. *Encelius* notes (*Book 2 on Metallic Matters, Chapter 15*) that this kind, blooming spontaneously on certain cliffs and stones, is called <term>Aphronitrum</term> <note>original: "Aphronitrum," or "froth of nitre," refers to the efflorescent salt crusts found on damp walls or rocks.</note>, nor was *Andrea Cesalpino* silent on the matter (*Book 1 on Metals, Chapter 20*).

I also discovered a volatile Magnet made from the "Spirit of Nitre" <note>Spirit of Nitre: historically, this refers to nitric acid, though here it refers to a salt-form derived from it.</note> in the form of a non-corrosive salt, for the making of which a specific earth was employed. There is no more immediate proof of drawing the "Spirit of the World" from the air, nor was it possible to observe this phenomenon elsewhere as clearly as this exhibited it. This occurred on September 15, 1673, which was followed at 11:00 at night by a full moon. My Magnet, which I mentioned, was poured out in the afternoon; it was committed to the air, and when exactly three hours had passed, it showed itself in a shape perfectly matching a Circle, notable for its white orbicular rim. It was a very hard body, yellow in color, resembling a star, and was distinguished by rays within. Shortly after, three others accompanied it, whose nature, shape, and color were all the same, except they were smaller. Two, which were also of [great] ma-

But it is neither a secondary Breeze <note>original: "Aura"</note>, that is, Nitre <note>Nitre, or saltpeter, was considered by alchemists to be a "magnet" capable of drawing vital celestial forces from the air.</note>, nor merely a secondary Gold; but it is also our most universal Magnet, when from the air (1)

...by attracting the Spirit of the World <note>original: "Spiritum Mundi"</note> from the air—a more striking proof of which has not been permitted to be seen in any other phenomenon. ...unequal in size to these, after about so many hours had passed, were seen. Finally, a very small one followed, most similar to the previous ones if you look at its form. The difference between these objects was that it was both soft and yellowish. At night, these seven bodies of stars, coming together into one huge mass, had hardened; however, in such a way that one could clearly note distinct figures with their rays in these four larger ones, even if they lacked a circle. After this, the Magnet began to attract the Spirit of the World <note>original: *Spiritum Mundi*, the vital life-force believed to permeate the atmosphere</note>, and for nearly two days, it was entirely turned into a yellowish Oil. The phenomenon was as you see here. There it exists with a double circle—both worthy of being inscribed with the same right as what *Basilius Valentinus* <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemical figure</note> saw fit to insert into his diagram of the Great Stone, prefixed to his Treatise: THE WONDER OF NATURE! <note>original: *MIRABILITAS NATURÆ!*</note> There exists, I say, in this double Circle, a Signature of Gold <note>original: *☉ri*; the symbol ☉ represents the Sun or Gold</note>, which anciently was marked only by a single circle.

But now let us also see the other thing we proposed: namely, that here this most universal Magnet attracts even out of itself the Hermetic Gold hiding within it, and is made with it into a permanent red sulfur. For the three Philosophers' principles—Salt <note>symbol: 🜔</note>, Fire <note>symbol: Δ; here representing Sulfur</note>, and Mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note>—are joined in it by an inseparable bond, which are fixed and coagulated by perpetual Cooking <note>original: *Coctione*</note> alone. Indeed, Salt is the central principle, attracting Fire and Mercury so that it may be perfected.

Thirdly: This most universal Magnet also answers to the name of the matter from which that *Alkahest of Paracelsus and Helmont* <note>The Alkahest was a hypothetical "universal solvent" capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid form</note> can be prepared; which is the pure, unadulterated fire of Nature—not elemental, but entirely celestial and central and incorruptible, reducing all things, without any decrease in its own action, into the first matter of things, and with truly no dross <note>original: *fæcibus*</note> left behind; truly dissolving. Those who, about to prepare the *Alkahest*, seek help from the mineral as well as the animal and vegetable kingdoms, follow a different path from us; joining the fixed and volatile salts of these (for example, Vitriol <note>symbol: ♀</note>, Sal ammoniac <note>symbol: *</note>, etc.) with our mineral Acid of Gold <note>symbol: ☉</note> and distilling them into a liquid. 

But why is there a need for infinite detours to go where you can go straight? And why weary yourself with labors that you can do without? You have, if you desire, the mineral, vegetable, and animal [kingdoms]; indeed, you have our "universal subject" ruling widely in all those kingdoms—although, if we wish to speak the truth, it is not such, but more truly the Matter of Meteors and Minerals, and a kind of nourishment for all Vegetables and Animals. This is the reason why, when we are about to prepare our Solvent <note>original: *Menstruum*</note>, we embrace that same "shroud of everything" <note>original Greek: *πᾶν ἀμφίον* (pan amphion)</note>, as it is called. This is the one thing, or that Monad of *Hermes* <note>Hermes Trismegistus</note>; the Spirit of Wine of *Arnold* <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova</note>; the Spirit of Lunaria of *Lull* <note>Ramon Llull</note>; the immortal Liquid, the Universal solvent, the Secret of fire, the Fire...

### CHAPTER V.

The Water of *Van Helmont*; the Mercurial Water of Fire of *Rovillasci*; the Dragon’s Blood of *Albertus Magnus*; the Saturnine Water and Mercurial Spirit of *Basilius Valentinus*; the sharpest Vinegar, Virgin’s Milk, Pontic Water, the dry water of *Sendivogius*; the Water of Paradise of *Isaac Hollandus*; the Alkahest <term>Alkahest: A hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving every other substance, much sought after by alchemists.</term> of *Martin Ruland, Michael Toxites, and Paracelsus*; the Ethereal fluid (that is, a spirit-fiery water) <note>original: *ein geiſtfeuriges Waſſer*</note> of *Heinrich Khunrath*; the Water of life—not of the vine <note>original: *Aqua vitæ non vitis*</note>—of *Lord de Nuysement*; the First Essence <term>Ens primum: The primary or original essence of a substance, believed to hold its highest medicinal power.</term> of *Flamel* derived from *Abraham the Jew*; and without doubt the Mercurial Oil of Salt, putrefied and distilled in the Hermetically sealed Tomb of Semiramis <note>A famous alchemical allegory; the legend claims an inscription on the tomb of the Queen of Babylon promised great wealth to anyone who opened it, but the seeker found only a warning against greed or a hidden wisdom.</note>. The Author of this Book—whoever he may have been—preferred to remain hidden until this day. Yet, wishing to offer this sacred thing to the renowned Academy of Nature Curiosorum <note>The *Academia Naturae Curiosorum*, one of the oldest scientific societies in the world, now known as the Leopoldina.</note>, he has bound all my colleagues, most celebrated men, and even myself to him in wonder. For which reason, I offer him the greatest thanks publicly; and I shall feel I have achieved the height of my wishes if I finally happen to learn his name (which I suspect to be J. H. C. D.).

Indeed, that author (in chapter 1) rejects intermediate minerals and Nitre <term>Nitrum: Saltpeter or potassium nitrate, which alchemists believed could attract the "vital spirit" of the air.</term> especially; yet it is possible that, being more favorable to it, he understood the crude and common kind. Otherwise, since he himself admits (chapter 9) that our Water was potentially a Mineral, it can be nothing else but the First Essence of salts, namely the Salt of Nature, from which mineral Nitre—being a universal Salt—has its origin. To me, this is the "Virgin Nitre" mentioned above, and not without the agreement of *Van Helmont*; for he himself established the Alkahest as the first Essence of Salts (*Treatise on Medicines, p. 322*), and likewise the highest and most fortunate of Salts. Furthermore, the Anonymous Author calls his liquid the "Philosophical Fire" (chapter 6), which in chapter 7 he calls an "Igneous Menstruum" <term>Menstruum: A solvent used in alchemy to dissolve or extract materials.</term>. In my mind, this is quite correct, as *Van Helmont* himself (on page 481) designates it the "Fire of Gehenna" <note>original: *Gehennæ ignem*; a term for a "hellish" or indestructible fire that dissolves substances without destroying their essence.</note>. Moreover, according to *Alphidius*, Salt is nothing but fire, and fire nothing but sulfur, and sulfur nothing but mercury. And specifically in our Nitre there is the highest heat, that is, an Infernal Spirit, than which nothing hotter is given in the whole nature of things, as *Basilius* testifies. But if, beyond my expectation, the most noble Author (whom we have praised so many times) intends something else, and perhaps what is called "Mercurial Salt" by many, I shall not change anything hereafter regarding this subject I have undertaken to defend. Furthermore (if I may say so with the leave of such a great Man), I long to know: did he himself labor to produce the Alkahest from that material of his, and by its aid perform what he records concerning its virtues? (For he preferred to pass over all those things in silence). If so, I rightly congratulate such great daring, counting it as a part of my happiness that it was my lot to have a Teacher here whom I might follow. If not, I shall seem to have achieved no small praise for myself, if from our efforts some fruit might redound to him in this matter.

He defends the use of Nitre as the "Salt of Nature" and the primary essence of all salts, despite potential disagreements with other anonymous alchemical authors.</summary>

# ON THE UNIVERSAL MAGNET.

...that someone, I have understood, has been redundant. However, I marvel most at that same man, that he was entirely silent regarding the manner in which that most noble juice <note>The "juice" refers to the concentrated universal solvent or "Nitre" discussed on the previous page.</note> ought to be prepared. In which matter it will seem to some that he did not even follow the footsteps of the Ancients, to whom (as he constantly commanded his disciples) to "make it obscure" <note>original Greek: τὸ σκότισον (*to skotison*) — a common alchemical injunction to hide secrets from the uninitiated</note> was certainly a concern and a care of the heart, but not excessively so. From among their number, there occurs at present *Basilius Valentinus* <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, purportedly a Benedictine monk, though his works likely date to the 16th century.</note>; whom alone I cannot, nor indeed should I, deny has pursued these matters most clearly. For thus he whom I mentioned says in his *Fifth Key*: "The first matter at the beginning of our work is best and most perfectly to be purified, then without any addition or admixture dissolved, destroyed, and reduced into ash and powder, from which afterwards must be expelled the volatile Spirit <term>Volatile Spirit: A substance that easily evaporates; in alchemy, it represents the active, 'spiritual' part of a matter that can be extracted by heat.</term>, white as snow, and yet another red volatile Spirit, like blood, which two contain a third within themselves, and are only a single spirit, which grants strength and life, etc." To him I shall add as a companion *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist known for his theories on the "food of life" found in the air.</note>, who himself in his *Treatise on Sulphur* says: "The Alchemist saw a fountain full of water, around which the Sun <note>represented by the symbol ☉</note> and Salt <note>represented by the symbol 🜍</note> walked, arguing among themselves, until at last they began to fight; and the Salt inflicted an incurable wound upon the Sulphur, from which wound, instead of blood, there flowed out water like the whitest milk, etc." There is no reason why I should be ashamed of their example. I shall, therefore, annex several things regarding that matter to the following chapter.

### CHAPTER VI.

->On the Method of attracting Gold from the Air.<-

**I**t has pleased the Philosophers to call Gold the King of Metals, or the Sun, because the rays of the Sun, dispersed in a visible and infinite manner, were invisibly united in the single body of Gold. Sacred Scripture seems to call this effluvium <term>Effluvium: An invisible emanation or outward flow of particles, here referring to the solar energy trapped in matter.</term> of the Sun "the precious fruits brought forth by the sun" <note>original Hebrew: מגד תבואת שמש (*meged tevuat shamash*)</note>, or the richness and dripping flower of the Sun, in *Deuteronomy 33:14*.

Indeed, there exist various writings of authors concerning the Sulphur of terrestrial Gold; but, I do not remember anyone who has spoken even a little word concerning the method of attracting it from the first source—namely, the Air <note>original: *aura*</note>, which is filled with the rays of the Sun—whether that was done perhaps through ignorance or, as one might also suspect, through envy. And although we do not go so far as to deny that others at different times have taught about acquiring it from the air by the work of Magnets—which some have devised as their own peculiar secrets—nevertheless, we have not yet seen anyone who

# CHAPTER V.

Helmont’s Water; the Mercurial Fire-Water of Rovillasco; the Dragon’s Blood of Albert <note>Likely referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and bishop often credited with alchemical discoveries.</note>; the Saturnine Water and Mercurial Spirit of *Basilius*; the Sharpest Vinegar, *Virgin’s Milk, Pontic Water, the dry water of Sendivogius*; the Water of Paradise of *Isaac Hollandus*; the *Alkahest <term>Alkahest: A hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving every other substance, including gold, sought by Paracelsus and his followers.</term> of Martin Ruland, Michael Toxites, and Paracelsus*; the *Ethereal Fluid* (that is, *a spirit-fiery water* <note>original German: ein geistfeuriges Wasser</note>) of *Conrad Khunrath*; the Water of Life, though not from the vine <note>original Latin: Aqua vitæ non vitis. This distinguishes the alchemical "water of life" from ordinary brandy or wine-alcohol.</note>, of the *Lord de Nuysement, the First Being of Flamel from Abraham the Jew*; and without doubt the *Mercurial Oil of Salt*, putrefied and distilled in the *Hermetically sealed Tomb of Semiramis* <note>A legendary alchemical allegory; the "Tomb of Semiramis" refers to a hidden vessel or secret process that, once opened (solved), reveals the riches of the art.</note>. Whoever the author of this book may be, he has preferred to remain hidden to this day. Nevertheless, wishing this sacred work to belong to the renowned *Academy of Nature Curiosors*, he has bound both all my colleagues—most celebrated men—and myself to him in a wonderful way. On which account, I publicly offer him the greatest thanks, and I shall feel I have achieved my highest wish if (as I suspect him to be J. H. C. D.) I might eventually come to know his name.

Indeed, he rejects (in Chapter 1) the *intermediate minerals* and especially *Nitre*; yet it is possible that, being more favorable to the former, he understood it as the crude and common sort. Furthermore, since he himself admits (in Chapter 9) that our Water was potentially a *Mineral*, it can be nothing else but the first *Being of salts, namely the Salt of Nature*, from which *mineral Nitre*, as the universal salt, has its cradle. To me, this is the *Nitre* previously called the *Virgin*, and not without the agreement of *Helmont*; for he himself established the *Alkahest* as the primary *Being of Salts* (*Treatise on Medicines*, page 322), likewise the highest and most successful of Salts: *The Power of Salt*. Further on, the *Anonymous Author* calls his liquor *Philosophical fire* in Chapter 6, which in Chapter 7 he calls an *igneous Solvent* <term>Menstruum: A technical alchemical term for a solvent, derived from the Latin for "monthly," relating to the timing of chemical cycles.</term>; and in my mind, he is quite right, since it is *Helmont* himself who (on page 481) labels it the fire of *Gehenna* <note>original: Gehennæ. Here used to describe a sharp, corrosive "hellfire" that breaks substances down.</note>. Now, according to *Alphidius*, Salt is nothing but fire, and fire is nothing but sulfur, and sulfur is nothing but *Mercury*. And specifically in our *Nitre* is the highest heat, that is, the *infernal Spirit*, than which nothing hotter is given in the whole nature of things, as *Basilius* testifies. But if, in truth, the most noble Author whom we have praised so many times intends, contrary to expectation, some other meaning—perhaps that which is called *Mercurial Salt* by many—I shall not change anything in the future regarding this subject which I have undertaken to defend. However (if I may say so, by the leave of such a great man), I long to know: whether he himself elaborated the *Alkahest* from that material of his, and by its power performed the things he records concerning its virtues (for he preferred to pass over all those things in silence). If it be so, I rightly congratulate such great daring, counting it as a part of my happiness that I have found a *Teacher* here whom I may follow; if not, I shall seem to have attained no small praise for myself if, from our own efforts, I might produce some fruit for him in this matter...

# ON THE UNIVERSAL MAGNET.

...achieved something, I will understand it to have been of benefit to someone. However, I am greatly amazed that he [the anonymous author] remained entirely silent on the manner in which that most noble juice <note>This "noble juice" refers to the universal solvent or the *Alkahest* mentioned on the previous page.</note> ought to be prepared. In this matter, it will seem to some that he did not even follow the footsteps of the Ancients, for whom—as he constantly commanded his disciples—"the darkening" <note>original Greek: τὸ σκότισον (to skotison). This refers to the alchemical practice of *Hermeticism*, or deliberately keeping instructions obscure to prevent the "unworthy" from discovering the secret.</note> was a matter of heart and care, but perhaps not too much so. From among their number, *Basilius Valentinus* <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist, supposedly a Benedictine monk, though his writings likely date to the 1600s.</note> comes to mind at present; I certainly cannot, nor should I, deny that he alone pursued these matters most openly. For in his *5th Key*, he says: "The first matter, at the beginning of our work, is to be purified in the best and most perfect way, then dissolved without any addition or mixture, destroyed, and reduced into ash and powder; from which, afterwards, the volatile Spirit is to be expelled, white as snow, and yet another volatile Spirit, red like blood. These two contain a third within themselves, and are but a single spirit, which bestows strength and life, etc." To him I shall add *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist known for his theories on the "food of life" found in the air.</note> as a companion, who himself says in his *Tract on Sulfur*: "The Alchemist saw a fountain full of water, around which ☉ [Sun/Gold] and ♁ [Antimony/Saturn] <note>The symbol ☉ represents Gold or the Sun. The symbol ♁ usually represents Antimony in this context, or sometimes the Earth; here they represent the masculine and feminine or the metallic and mineral principles.</note> were walking, arguing among themselves until at last they began to fight; and the Salt inflicted an incurable wound upon the Sulfur, from which wound, instead of blood, there flowed out a water like the whitest milk, etc." There is no reason why I should be ashamed of their example. Therefore, I shall annex several things regarding this matter to the following chapter.

## CHAPTER VI.

->On the Method of Attracting Gold from the Air.<-

It has pleased the Philosophers to call Gold, the King of Metals, the "Sun," for the reason that the rays of the Sun, dispersed in a visible and infinite way, were invisibly united in the single body of Gold. Holy Scripture seems to call this effluence of the Sun "the precious things of the fruits of the sun" <note>original Hebrew: מגד תנואת שמש (meged tenu'ot shemesh). From Deuteronomy 33:14, often interpreted by alchemists as a reference to the celestial influence on minerals.</note> *Deut. xxxiii, 14.*
Indeed, there exist various writings of authors concerning the Sulfur of the terrestrial Sun; but I do not remember anyone who has spoken even a small word about the method of attracting it from its first source—namely, the air [aura]—filled with the rays of the Sun. This was done perhaps through ignorance, or, as one might suspect, through envy. And although we do not deny that some have taught, at various times, about acquiring it from the air by the work of Magnets <note>In this context, "Magnets" are not necessarily lodestones, but substances or devices designed to "attract" the vital spirit or "nitre" from the atmosphere.</note> which they devised for their own use, nevertheless we have not yet seen anyone who...

# CHAPTER VI.

Concerning the universal Sulfur, and the reasons why any commentary has been made thus far regarding its attainment, or shared with the learned world. Except for one Mr. Faber of Montpellier <note>Pierre-Jean Fabre (1588–1658), a famous French physician and alchemist who sought to reconcile Paracelsian chemistry with traditional medicine.</note>, whose words from his *Spagyric Hydrography, chapter 14*, we wish to be read here; in which he clearly puts forth his opinion, saying: "In the fountain of the Chemists, there is a fixed part, red and white in a twofold difference: the red being the Gold of the Philosophers, and the white being their Silver." <note>original: "In fonte... Chymicorum esse partem fixam rubeam & albam... rubea quæ sit, Aurum Philosophorum, & quæ alba sit Argentum eorundem." Alchemists often used "Gold" and "Silver" to refer to the perfected masculine and feminine principles of matter, rather than the common metals.</note> Otherwise, he seems here to want to draw out his "fountain" from two substances having the nature of Salt, yet arising from one and the same root (see chapter 13); for which reason we cannot agree with him, and we deservedly reject the opinion he fostered. We find nothing lacking in the *Nollian* <note>Referring to Heinrich Nolle (active 1610s), a Paracelsian physician who wrote on "Hermetic Philosophy."</note> view that the color and the Balsam of the Sun <note>The "Balsam of the Sun" refers to the vital, life-giving essence or "sulfur" extracted from sunlight or the atmosphere.</note> can be contained within a created Magnet (as seen in his *Introduction to Hermetic Physics*). For what could have been said more clearly than those words, far from all obscurity? But either he [Faber] was entirely ignorant of the magnet and the method of attracting, or, as those who are most secretive about their mysteries are usually wont to be, he did not wish to publish it to the common people.

I myself could have done the same—keeping this discovery of mine within my chests and so suppressing it entirely; but that is not my custom, nor is it my solemn duty to serve others. Furthermore, a certain ardor and a truly relentless effort to excite others to this same study have allowed this secret to be drawn from me; for I have frequently turned over in my mind that quite clever saying of Gellius <note>Aulius Gellius, a 2nd-century Roman author known for his "Attic Nights."</note>:

> He who wishes to speak what no one understands performs a great feat if he remains silent. <note>original: "Qui loqui vult, quod nemo intelligat, magnam rem præstat, si taceat."</note>

Come then: 1. To attract the Hermetic Gold of the Air from the higher atmosphere, prepare for yourself a magnet—that same white and most pure one; concerning which the preceding chapter will speak to you if you wish. Then, let there be four baked bricks, which should be restrained within a crucible in which that magnet is contained, with a fire placed beneath it under a clear sky, so that it does not collapse. This done, you will see that magnet I mentioned, in a very short time, as if reviving and moving; and in the space of about a hundred minutes, you will see it attract the golden Sulfur—which I call the Gold of the Air <note>original: *Aurum Auræ*</note>—from the Atmosphere. The magnet assumes a grass-green color, and that of the highest degree, both inside and out; but after it has cooled, it assumes a blue color, such as copper-rust <note>original: *erani*, likely referring to *aerugo* or verdigris, the blue-green patina on copper.</note> is wont to be. This is usually done to the great amazement of onlookers.

Otherwise, for those wishing to experiment, it will not be useless to use certain means, both for arranging the magnet especially and for governing the fire particularly, which are surely more laboriously described than shown. But if you put your hand to the work yourself, following as if a guide, those things which...

# ->ON THE MODE OF ATTRACTION.<-
t 2
<meta>catchword: Pot-</meta>

...we have instructed you sufficiently so far; we have no doubt that you, having learned the means, will reach that place by a much easier path than that by which we penetrated into the very citadel of the method itself.

2. Furthermore, regarding the attraction from the lower Air—specifically from our **Nitre** <note>Saltpeter, which alchemists believed contained a celestial life-force or "spirit" from the air.</note>, the Hermetic Gold of the lower regions: we have mentioned above that this subject is not only a Magnet in itself, but also contains the Hermetic Gold within itself. Since this is the case, the Magnet (that is, the purest body of salt) does not lack its own power for attracting and retaining its spirit, in which the Hermetic Gold lies hidden. Thus, it becomes one volatile substance with these, and finally, through repeated conjunction and attraction, the true Liquor **Alkahest** <term>Alkahest: A hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any substance into its primary liquid form without changing its own nature.</term> can be distilled. Indeed, **Trismegistus** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage and purported father of alchemy.</note> teaches this very thing about the subject in these words:

> *By this, it ascends from the earth into heaven, and from heaven, it descends again to the earth, etc., so that it is able to penetrate and overcome all subtle and thick things.* <note>original: "In hoc à terra ascendit in cœlum, hoc à terra à cœlo rursùs in terrâ descendit... quod omnia subtilia crassa penetrare & subigere potest." This is a famous passage from the Emerald Tablet describing the circulation of the universal life-force.</note>

And here we must again call upon **Basilius** <note>Basilius Valentinus, a famous, likely pseudonymous alchemist known for his "Twelve Keys."</note> to join us. For what else is that volatile white spirit, like snow, in his *5th Key*, if not a certain sublimated Salt? Or what is the red volatile Spirit, if not the very spirit of Nitre? (Which, while being distilled, passes over looking like a crimson vapor.)

Nor does **Lullius** <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan polymath. Many alchemical texts were attributed to him in later centuries.</note> contradict this in his *Experiments*, where, under the veil of the "volatile Salt of Tartar," he very fully handed down this labor. In a word:

> *In our mastery, we first make the subtle from the gross—that is, the Spirit from the body—from the moist, the dry, and from the water, the earth; thus we convert the natures and make the higher lower: which happens when the Spirit is made into a body, and conversely, the body is made into a Spirit.* <note>original: "In nostro magisterio primò facimus de grosso gracilè... quùm Spiritus efficitur corpus & è converso corpus fit Spiritus."</note>

I hold this to be true, and I have persuaded my mind, that the Liquor **Alkahest** is most properly prepared if **Nitre** (or that matter of ours, purified with the greatest zeal), regenerated in a particular way, is united in a certain quantity with its own spirit (which has been rectified again and again), putrefied in a water-bath, distilled, cohobated <term>Cohobation: The process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its solid remains and re-distilling it to concentrate its medicinal or chemical power.</term>, and sublimated. Then, let it be cohobated with new Spirit; and after subsequent repeated cohobations, until it becomes a milky water that can be congealed by cold like butter, let it finally be distilled and rectified.

These things, however, are not easy to do; they require a distinguished artist and a notable Philosopher. Regarding this matter, these words by **Mr. Jean Baptiste van Helmont** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering Flemish chemist and physician who famously wrote about the Alkahest.</note> in his *Treatise on the Rise of Medicine, page 52*, are especially worth remembering:

> *Since the Liquor Alkahest is of a most tedious preparation, no one, even if they were skilled in the art, shall arrive at its attainment, unless the Most High, by a special gift, has led them to it.* <note>original: "Cùm Liquor Alkahest sit tædiosissimæ præparationis... quem Altissimus speciali dono non eò deduxerit."</note>
