# LORD JOHANN MICHAEL
# FEHR, <note>Johann Michael Fehr (1610–1688) was a physician and a co-founder of the Academy of Nature-Curiosities.</note>

->Surnamed<-

## *ARGONAUT*, <note>original: *Argonauta*; members of this Academy were traditionally given an "academic name" from antiquity. Fehr’s was "Argonauta," referring to the Greek heroes who sought the Golden Fleece, symbolizing the search for medical and natural knowledge.</note>

->To my most Honored Patron, Friend, and Kinsman,<-

->Sends Heartiest Greetings. <note>original: *S. P. D.*, an abbreviation for *Salutem Plurimam Dicit*.</note><-

# CHRISTIAN MENTZEL, Doctor.

->Counselor and Chief Physician to the Most Serene Elector of Brandenburg, Fellow of the<-
->Academy of Nature-Curiosities of the Holy Roman Empire.<-

---

**A**s the initiates <note>original: *Mystæ*; literally "priests" or "initiates," here referring to scientists or natural philosophers.</note> of nature's secrets <note>original: *arcana rerum naturæ*</note> in this century of ours curiously investigate them, they seem to emerge entirely divine into the sight of mortals. And these things, indeed, come to light and are made known not without the nod of the Most High, so that His glory and eternal honor should rightly shine forth from them and be spread among us. For this is the ultimate end of all secrets and of all things; yet in this utterly perverse age, Satan—the most bitter enemy of both divine and human affairs—seems once again to attack it with war and the overthrow of kingdoms, soon to transform our most learned century into mere barbarism (unless God forbid). But since nothing happens without the divine will, we must acquiesce in it, and equally... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as it transitions to the next page.</note>

<meta>catchword: and equally [original: riterq; (pariterque)]</meta>

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Christian Mentzel's work to Johann Michael Fehr, the President of the Academy of Nature-Curiosities. Mentzel frames the scientific study of "nature's secrets" as a divine pursuit intended to glorify God, while expressing fear that contemporary wars might plunge their learned age back into barbarism.</summary>

<keywords>Christian Mentzel, Johann Michael Fehr, Argonauta, Leopoldina Academy, dedication, divine glory, secrets of nature, 17th-century science, Thirty Years' War aftermath</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Christian Mentzel to Johann Michael Fehr. Mentzel transitions from general praise of the Academy to a specific discussion of a new scientific discovery: Balduin’s "Hermetic Phosphorus." He compares this new substance to the famous Bologna stone, which he studied during his university days in Italy.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>
<header>DEDICATION.</header>

...and things that cannot be changed must be endured; nevertheless, one must persist with constant prayers and studies in those things that concern the praises, honors, and glory of the Almighty. Among the hidden and most intimate secrets of nature, that admirable **Hermetic Phosphorus** <note>original: *Phosphorus Hermeticus*; a luminescent substance discovered by Christian Adolph Balduin in 1675, made from calcium nitrate.</note> seems to me to rightly stand out, [discovered by] the **Most Renowned Lord Christian Adolph Balduin, Fellow of the Academy of Nature-Curiosities of the Holy Roman Empire**, surnamed ***Hermes*** <note>Members of the Academy were given classical nicknames; Balduin was named after Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>, which he added as an appendix to his ***Hermetic Gold of the Breeze*** <note>original: *Auro Auræ Hermetico*; Balduin’s treatise on "atmospheric gold" or saltpeter.</note>. This little book is entirely gilded—indeed, it is golden—with **Hermetic Gold**. However, just as the golden bough, which the whole grove covers, was not revealed to **Aeneas** except by the flight of doves <note>A reference to Virgil’s *Aeneid*; the doves of Venus lead Aeneas to the golden bough required to enter the underworld.</note>: so this **Gold of the Breeze**, the **Bird of Hermes** <note>original: *Avicula Hermetis*; an alchemical symbol for volatile substances or the "spirit" of a material.</note>, will not become known to the noble Philosopher except by the guidance of the **Holy Spirit**. For such things are hidden within the secluded womb of nature, so that they are opened to only a few; but to the despisers of God and His marvelous works, they are eternally closed, lest pearls be thrown to swine, but rather the husks and the filth of the earth (the riches of the world), in which their snouts are deeply immersed. And although the **Hermetic Phosphorus**, like **Venus**, or the **common Phosphorus** <note>original: *Phosphori vulgaris*; here referring to the planet Venus as the "Light-Bringer" (Phosphoros in Greek), the morning star that appears before sunrise.</note>, which precedes Apollo <note>Apollo represents the sun.</note> with its clearest light as the herald of the day, now shines for all mortals and indicates that the day of the mystery (of the universal medicine) is at hand, yet the dregs of the common people, buried in the darkness of ignorance, are so far from blessedly enjoying this risen light that they are more likely to suffer harm from it, like moths <note>original: *hepiolorum*; likely referring to *hepialid* moths attracted to light.</note> burning themselves at night in the flame of a lamp.

This **Hermetic Phosphorus, Best and Most Faithful of Friends**, as soon as you yourself had handed it to me to read through within your house at **Schweinfurt** <note>original: *Svinfurti*; a city in Bavaria where the Academy was then centered.</note> a few weeks ago, I immediately saw and said that it agreed in many ways—by reason of its light-bearing quality—with the **Bologna stone** <note>original: *lapide Bononiensi*; a variety of the mineral barite found near Bologna, Italy, which became the first known persistent phosphor after being calcined.</note>, which is also illustrious and glowing in the dark, because that stone was thoroughly examined and known by me. At that moment, I promised that I would briefly publish something about it as soon as the opportunity arose. And although the **Most Renowned Gentlemen Poterius, Montalbanus, etc.** <note>Pierre Potier and Ovidio Montalbani, 17th-century physicians who wrote early accounts of the Bologna stone.</note> have written about this Bologna stone in the past, and especially **Fortunio Liceti of Genoa**, formerly my **Promoter** in the **Medical Faculty**—who is always to be revered by me even after his death—who published a singular and complete book about this stone in the year 1640; nevertheless, since I happened to have studied formerly at **Bologna**, and to have curiously devoted my labor for a year and more to Medicine as well as to understanding this stone, I will at least touch upon those things (as far as I am still able by memory in this course of my age, and can recall from things done many years ago) which seem to be established by my own experience rather than reprinting the writings of others.

<meta>The catchword "librum-" at the bottom of the page indicates that the next page begins with the word "book."</meta>

<summary>In this dedication, Christian Mentzel praises Balduin’s discovery of "Hermetic Phosphorus" and relates it to his own past studies of the phosphorescent Bologna Stone in Italy. He frames these scientific discoveries as divine secrets intended only for those who honor God and nature.</summary>
<keywords>Christian Mentzel, Christian Adolph Balduin, Phosphorus, Bologna stone, alchemy, Fortunio Liceti, Schweinfurt, history of chemistry, light-bearing minerals</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Christian Mentzel to Johann Michael Fehr. Mentzel is justifying his writing style and explaining the circumstances of his research. He transitions from discussing Balduin’s Phosphorus to his own observations of the "Bologna Stone" (a phosphorescent mineral), which he studied during his time in Italy.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>
<header>DEDICATION.</header>

...and to compile a book from other books (as is frequently loved to be done today). But as for those things which I have seen fit to bring forward from others as testimony, and to use their words, I shall not be ashamed to profess this openly and to express them in their own distinctive typefaces. As long as I lived in Bologna <note>original: *Bononia*; a major university city in Italy.</note>, I visited more than once the place where I was taught by the inhabitants the birthplace of the stone; and there I myself collected as many stones as possible, and saw to it that others were collected, so that I might send them partly to **Venice**, to the **Most Renowned Lord Dr. Tackenius** <note>Otto Tackenius (1610–1699), a famous German physician and chemist living in Venice, known for his theory that all things consist of acid and alkali.</note>, who also has a most exact knowledge of that stone, and partly so that I myself might calcine <term>calcine</term> <note>To heat a substance to high temperatures in order to change its physical or chemical state; in this context, to make the stone glow in the dark.</note> them at home and make its nature clear to me through much labor. Therefore, that I may keep my promises, I shall at least lightly touch upon (as I said) those things which are known to me concerning this stone, leaving more abundant details to the aforementioned **Renowned Men**, lest I bring (as they say) twice-cooked cabbage <note>original: *cramben bis coctam*; a Latin idiom meaning a boring repetition of something already known.</note>; instead, I shall set forth what more select things I have found in this "herb garden," and bring this light-bearing stone into the open, which is in many ways, though not in all, **to be compared** with the **Hermetic Phosphorus**.

There is also a primary reason why I am very brief in this matter: namely, the nature of my duty. Having followed the camps of the **Most Serene Elector of Brandenburg** <note>Frederick William (1620–1688), known as the "Great Elector."</note>, my **Most Clement** Lord, for seventeen years now, through various wandering journeys across **Germany**, every opportunity for writing has hitherto been cut off from me during my travels. This was true until at last, by order of the **Most Serene Elector**, it happened that I stayed for a little while here in his castle called **Sparenberg** <note>Sparrenberg Castle in Bielefeld.</note>, which sits on a high mountain overlooking the city of **Bielefeld**, with **His Most Serene Spouse** <note>Louise Henriette of Orange-Nassau or Dorothea of Schleswig-Holstein-Sonderburg-Glücksburg, depending on the exact year.</note>, who was forced to stop here because of an imminent birth, so that I might oversee her care. 

From there, having been restored to a bit more quiet for a few days, and thus having found an opportunity for writing, I have attempted to publish these few things concerning the light-bearing **Bologna** stone, written in a more Philosophical style, sprinkled with chemical and other technological terms, and not conforming in all things to the Roman classical authors. And although I would have preferred to remain hidden and to measure myself by my own small standard, rather than to hunt for some small glory among certain people from any kind of writing; you, however, **Most Excellent Man**, resisted this will of mine. By your overflowing kindness and friendship (to which I was perhaps found to be bound by a closer bond of kinship by marriage on my wife's side), you so changed my mind that, the barriers of my purpose having been broken, I do not hesitate to step out from the shadows into the light with my **Bologna** stone: especially as you added spurs to this by **showing me the Album of the Illustrious and Most Excellent Lord Fellows of the Academy of Nature-Curiosities of the Holy Roman Empire, in which you ordered me to insert my name and pledge my faith.**

->To whom<-

<summary>Mentzel explains that his duties as a military physician for the Elector of Brandenburg delayed his research, but he is now publishing his findings on the Bologna stone (a phosphorescent mineral) at the urging of Johann Michael Fehr. He describes his firsthand collection of the minerals in Italy and his chemical experiments with them.</summary>
<keywords>Bologna stone, phosphorus, Christian Mentzel, Otto Tackenius, Elector of Brandenburg, Bielefeld, Sparrenberg Castle, alchemy, mineralogy, Academy of Nature-Curiosities</keywords>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>D E D I C A T I O N.</header>

To whom then, rather than to *You*, *Most Illustrious of Men*, should I dedicate this modest little book, who has bound me to you with so many and such great bonds of friendship?

Receive therefore, *Greatest of Friends*, this offspring of my talent (such as it is) produced in a few days, the *light-bearing Bologna Stone* <note>original: *Lapidem Bononienfem luciferum*; refers to a type of baryte found near Bologna that glows after being heated.</note>, into the arms of your kindness and goodwill, so that under the shadow of your shield, having first conceived the light of Apollo <note>Apollo was the god of light; this refers to the stone being "charged" by exposure to the sun.</note>, it may shine more brightly, be protected, and always be cherished with sincere love. Farewell. Given at Sparenberg Castle / July 6th, 1675 <note>original: *Pridie Non. Jul.*, the day before the Nones of July.</note>.

# TO CHRISTIAN MENTZEL,
## Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine, Councilor and Physician to the Elector

->*Most Noble, Excellent, and Illustrious.*<-

->**When he was writing about the Phosphorus Stone on Mount Sparenberg.**<-

It is said that Prometheus, with a torch moved close,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Stole fire from the radiant wheel of the Sun.
The summits of Mount Caucasus aided him,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which were near to his own heaven and the stars.
Behold, Mentzel—a new son of Prometheus—recently
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Approached the high mountain under a lucky star:
There he forces the light of flaming Phoebus <note>A name for the Sun god.</note> into a Stone,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So that the *Phosphorus* <note>original: *Phosphorus*; from the Greek for "light-bringer."</note> may shine clearly from it in the darkness.
Now as a dweller of the mountain, continue to become famous in this way,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Mentzel! Fame sounds her eternal trumpets for you.

->*Freely and deservedly written for a most Honored Colleague and Friend at Berlin.*<-

->JOHANN SIGISMUND ELSHOLTZ.<-

---

The light-bearing stone, which famous Bologna displays,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The German land now sees shown in this book.
Here the glory of chief praise is owed to you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Mentzel, who blesses us with the novelty of this gift.

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of Christian Mentzel's dedication and a celebratory poem by Johann Sigismund Elsholtz, comparing Mentzel's work with the light-bearing stone to the myth of Prometheus stealing fire from the sun.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of commendatory poems and dedications following Christian Mentzel’s preface. The previous page featured a poem comparing Mentzel to a "new Prometheus" for his work on the light-bearing Bologna Stone at Sparenberg Castle. This page concludes a short prophetic verse by Johannes Manzius and begins a longer, more metaphorical tribute to Mentzel’s dual mastery of medicine and natural philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<sig>bb</sig>

Go on, Initiate <note>original: *Myſtes*; a term for a priest or one initiated into secret religious mysteries.</note> of nature, to reveal her mysteries,
Through this Stone, your fame shall be able to grow.
This *Stone* will not be ungrateful, but will return rewards
To the Author of light, and is itself a new light.
*The names of many shine on polished marbles,*
From whence your fame grows, *it will be a little book.*
In which you skillfully reveal the *Mysteries of the Stone*,
Art ensures that you can be capable of containing light.
*Time devours marble, but such is not the fate of books:*
For in learned books, *honor* stands immortal.

->Thus I auspiciously prophesied for this little book<-
->**Johannes Manzius, Rector of the Bielefeld School.**<-

---

O man of perceptive mind, who—
Not as a newcomer or recent arrival, but powerful
Through long experience—are known as a proven
*judge of the art* of *Chiron* <note>original: *Chironia*; referring to the centaur Chiron, the mythological founder of medicine.</note>,
Second to none in the charm of your character
And the glory of your courtesy. Just as through the heart of *Africa*,
Burning with the heat of the glowing sun,
The lust for battle or for a raw feast
Seizes the undaunted male offspring of a lioness—
Whether he stands among the lowing herds
And grips the struggling bull more fiercely with his bite,
Or, as a wanderer through all the sheepfolds,
Soon carries off the soft, bleating flock in his claws—
So into your more sagacious heart,
An immense ardor of curiosity and
Such a fervor for knowing, and a bold heat,
Has poured itself. *You are the glory of more than one Phoebus!* <note>Phoebus Apollo was the god of both the Sun and of Medicine; the poet implies Mentzel serves both the light of the stone and the art of healing.</note>
It is not enough, through herbs and the duties of healing,
To make the honors of *Machaon* <note>A legendary physician in the Iliad, son of the god of medicine, Asclepius.</note>
More celebrated, to halt death, and to grant a delay
To the boat <note>A reference to Charon’s skiff, which transports the souls of the dead across the River Styx.</note> which carries away the mortal race:
Unless you also seek, with a curious happiness,
The deep, sacred rites of Mother *Nature*,
And to have known the causes and ways
By which the LIGHT-BEARING STONE <note>original: *LUCIFER LAPIS*; literally "the light-bringing stone," referring to the phosphorescent properties of the Bologna stone (Baryte).</note> gives itself
To the College of BOLOGNA,

### Epilogue.

# PREFACE.

Wise antiquity seems to have established the analogy between heaven and earth, and of all things, upon no weak foundations, when it determined that the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>The "Great World" or the universe as a whole, which was thought to be reflected in the "Microcosm" or the human being.</note> and the highest things correspond to the lowest. Concerning this analogy—the Harmony of heaven and earth, and that <term>Golden Chain</term> <note>A classical concept, often attributed to Homer, representing the interconnectedness of all creation from the divine down to the material.</note> by which all natural things cling to one another—others have written elsewhere. For my part, striving for brevity, I here present a certain "analogical fire" within the *Bologna Stone*, properly prepared by fire; I bring forward by way of a summary at least that which still sticks in my memory after an interval of so many years. I will show how that *Stone*, by reason of the light it conceives from the air when illuminated by the sun's rays, partly agrees and partly differs with the *Hermetic Phosphorus of the most famous Christian Adolph Balduin*, surnamed *Hermes*. This *Hermetic Phosphorus* <note>A luminescent substance (calcium nitrate) discovered by Balduin in 1674; it was called "Hermetic" in honor of the mystical/chemical traditions of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>—which any curious observer of Nature views with me in the greatest admiration, and which in our age rises again like a sun, spreading and illuminating its light throughout the world not without the sun's help—provided the occasion for me to write here concerning this *Light-bearing Bologna Stone*. Hence it happened that, having abandoned the common method (whereby any subject is treated by its name, definition, genus, difference, causes, and similar <term>commonplaces</term>), I desire this *Hermetic Phosphorus* of the famous Balduin to be placed first, like a primary text which ought to be treated briefly by making a comparison with the other substance. For in this way, when I have dealt with the properties of our *Light-bearing Bologna Stone* which it shares with the *Hermetic Phosphorus*, the reading will become clearer to you, <term>Benevolent Reader</term>. Indeed, I hope that once a comparison is made between these two luminaries, the diligent student of nature may be able to inquire further and finally track down the truth—which ought to be the primary goal and end of every matter. Therefore, I shall immediately undertake the comparison of the *Light-bearing Bologna Stone* with the *Hermetic Phosphorus*. Having divided my little book, I will deal in the first section with the light-bearing properties in which our two Phosphors agree or differ, and what unique quality the *Hermetic Phosphorus* possesses that is different from the *Bologna Stone*, and vice versa. In the second section, we will deal with the *Bologna Stone* itself, both in general and in specific detail: namely, concerning its Inventor, its place of origin, and other matters...

<meta>catchword: ...matters,</meta>

<summary>In this preface, Mentzel introduces his study of the phosphorescent Bologna Stone, framing it within the Hermetic tradition of universal harmony and comparing it to Balduin's newly discovered "Hermetic Phosphorus." He explains his choice to eschew traditional academic structures in favor of a direct comparative analysis to better reveal the "truth" of natural light.</summary>

matters which are found in the said place, regarding the differences of the Stone, its causes, and whatever can be briefly discussed concerning the Bologna Stone in the manner of a handbook, leaving to the famous Liceto and others their own works, which have treated this Stone and its causes more extensively after the manner of the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, who often relied on complex systems of "qualities" and "essences" to explain natural phenomena.</note>.

# SECTION ONE.

## CHAPTER I.

### *On the Light-bearing Power of the Bologna Stone shared with the common Hermetic Phosphorus of the famous Balduin.*

The first observation of the most famous Balduin regarding his *Hermetic Phosphorus* is this: that during the day it attracts splendor from the light of the sun, as can be read at greater length in the preface to his *Phosphorus*.

This quality is entirely shared with our *Bologna Stone*. For after our stone has been properly <term>calcined</term> <note>A process of heating a substance to high temperatures in a furnace to change its physical or chemical properties, essential for making the Bologna Stone glow.</note> and exposed to the daylight—whether the air be dark with clouds or clear and open to the solar rays—in a brief moment of time it borrows light from the Sun, attracts it, and displays it in the dark. For this reason, our stone seems to deserve the name <term>Moonstone</term> <note>original: *Lunaria*</note> in a certain way, since just like the moon it derives its light from the Sun. However, our *Bologna Stone* performs this light-bearing feat only when the Sun is above the horizon, whether the day be clear or cloudy, just as I have personally experienced. But the most famous Poterius, in book 2 of his *Spagyric Pharmacy* <note>Alchemy or "Spagyric" medicine focused on separating and recombining the "essences" of plants and minerals.</note>, chapter 27, and following him, Fortunio Liceto, in *On Stones* <note>original: *Litho-phosphorus*</note>, chapter 5, etc., assert that this stone also receives its light from the Moon, which is why it is called the *Lunar Stone* <note>original: *Lapis Lunaris*</note> by some.

I, however, though I would not wish to contradict such great men—since much depends on the method of preparing or calcining the stone, and one stone may surpass another in its light-bearing power, so that in this way it might be possible for some Bologna stones to acquire light even from the borrowed light of the Moon and represent it in the darkness—nevertheless, I have very often conducted experiments with my own calcined stones. I exposed them at night, during very clear weather, to the borrowed light of the Moon when it was already entirely full, in order to investigate whether they would then also imbibe light from the Moon; but those stones, though excellently prepared and light-bearing, returned nothing of splendor or light in the dark at that time, even though I waited in vain through the whole night for the full—

<meta>catchword: moon</meta>

<summary>Mentzel begins his technical analysis by comparing the light-absorbing properties of the Bologna Stone to Balduin's phosphorus, noting that while some claim the stone can be "charged" by moonlight, his own experiments only show it reacting to the sun.</summary>
<keywords>Bologna Stone, Phosphorus, Christian Adolph Balduin, calcination, phosphorescence, Fortunio Liceti, moonlight vs sunlight, Moonstone</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the light-absorbing properties of the Bologna Stone and Christian Adolph Balduin’s "Hermetic Phosphorus," detailing experiments with various light sources like the sun, hearth fires, and candles.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>SECTION I. CHAPTER I.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

...I had performed experiments during a clear June. Indeed, in the very early morning, as soon as <note>Apollo: A poetic reference to the Sun god.</note> Apollo, having left the dawn behind, had touched the Horizon, those stones placed under the open sky began to shine again in the dark. They shone quite clearly, like glowing iron or a hot coal, with a vermillion-red color: a strong argument that the Sun is the fountainhead of fire and light, just as this is established by other proofs.

2. The second observation of the most famous Balduin is *that his Hermetic Phosphorus <note>A luminescent chemical discovered by Christian Adolph Balduin (1632–1682), made by dissolving chalk in nitric acid and then calcining it.</note> was found to shine even by the tiny flame of a candle.* Such a great "magnetism" of the Bologna Stone—to catch light so easily from a weak candle flame—was not observed by me, since I never moved it toward the flame of a lamp-wick. However, I discovered by experiment that when I placed it near a hearth fire, where there was much flame along with glowing coals, and then moved it after some time into a hat or a dark place, it gave back a small light, though barely perceptible. From this, it is possible that it too borrows light from a candle flame and can be "kindled," as it were. The <note>Liceti (1577–1657) was a prominent physician and philosopher who wrote a major treatise on the Bologna Stone.</note> famous Liceto in his *Lithophosphorus, chapter 4*, seems to approve of this, saying: *the Bologna stone catches light even from the flame of a burning torch, and for this reason it is called by some the "terrestrial moon."*

It is concluded from this that the Bologna stone, just like the Hermetic Phosphorus, can catch light from a hearth and earthly fire, and therefore these two "light-bearers" agree in this part to some extent; however, they differ in their power of capturing it: for the Bologna stone draws light from fire with more difficulty, while the Hermetic Phosphorus does so more easily.

3. The third observation of the famous Author is this: *that his Hermetic Phosphorus, even when enclosed in glass, nevertheless attracted light from the Sun’s rays and a lit candle.* I performed the same experiment with the Bologna stone; when placed by me into a glass vessel and thus closed and exposed to the solar rays, it scattered light from itself in a dark place. From this, our Author strives to prove the <term>porosity of glass</term> <note>At the time, many scientists believed that if light (which they often viewed as a physical substance) passed through glass, the glass must contain invisible "pores" or holes.</note>. Therefore, our two Phosphori agree in this matter as well.

4. The fourth observation of the aforementioned Author is *that his Phosphorus, when moved toward a white wall, not only shines in the darkness, but also illuminates the wall; and like the moon illuminating the earth, it gives back light from itself.* Although our Bologna stone does not greatly illuminate the dark surface on which it is placed when full of light—as the Hermetic Phosphorus illuminates a white wall—nevertheless, when placed back in a hat where I also put white paper, it poured out a light around itself (though weak and barely sensible), so that the surface, whether it was the hat or the paper, could be exactly distinguished. However, I did not perform the experiment on a white wall.

<meta>catchword: Con- (Continues to "Conclusion" or "Consequently")</meta>

<summary>Mentzel compares the Bologna Stone and Balduin’s Phosphorus, noting that both can absorb light from the sun or fire and can even shine through glass, though the Bologna Stone is less "magnetic" to weak light sources.</summary>
<keywords>Bologna Stone, Hermetic Phosphorus, Balduin, Liceto, phosphorescence, calcination, solar rays, hearth fire, porosity of glass</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Bologna Stone and Christian Adolph Balduin's "Hermetic Phosphorus," transitioning from their similarities to the unique ways Balduin's substance appears to surpass the natural stone in luminous intensity and resilience.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>On the Luminous Properties of the Bologna Stone compared with Hermetic Phosphorus</header>

Therefore, the Hermetic Phosphorus and the Bologna Stone <note>original: "lapis Bononiensis," a type of barium sulfate that glows after exposure to light.</note> agree in a certain way in this matter: namely, that both are able to pour forth light from themselves without contact, though the latter does so more feebly, while the former does so more strongly.

5. The fifth observation of the famous Balduin concerning his Hermetic Phosphorus <note>original: "Phosphorus Hermeticus," a luminous chemical discovered by Balduin, made from chalk and nitric acid.</note> is that it lays aside its borrowed light again after a brief interval of time. This same thing happens in the Bologna stone, whose light—conceived from the clear surrounding air—does not remain long in its subject, but again, after some delay, vanishes into thin air and returns to that place from whence it came: however, it repeats its light as often as it is exposed again to public light, though it does not retain it forever. Thus, both Phosphori <note>The term "phosphorus" was used generically in the 17th century for any substance that glowed in the dark.</note> agree in the five aforementioned ways in which they can be compared among themselves. Finally, there are other things in which they agree, which will be discussed in their proper place.

## CHAPTER II.
### *Regarding the superior light-bearing power of the Hermetic Phosphorus, by which it seems to surpass the Bologna Stone.*

But those things which the most famous Balduin records in his 6th, 7th, and 8th observations seem incomparable, sharing nothing in common between the Bologna Stone and the Hermetic Phosphorus by reason of their light-bearing power, namely: *that his Hermetic Phosphorus, pregnant with solar light and cast into a glass full of spirit of nitre, <note>original: "spiritu nitri," now known as nitric acid.</note> nonetheless scattered light from itself in the dark. Next, that having been extracted from that liquid and dried again in the dark so that it would lose its light, and being placed again in a glass full of cold water and exposed to the light of day, it nonetheless attracted a fiery splendor again even within the cold water itself. Finally, that having been removed again from the cold water and dried and deprived of its light in the dark, and placed upon a hot furnace, it also regained its light in the furnace itself, even though the chamber was dark and utterly deprived of light.* Perhaps that Phosphorus received its light-bearing force from the heat of the furnace, as it could not acquire it from the dark surrounding air of the room.

Such things are truly admirable: namely, that a most clear light inhering in some subject—and one which has retained both heat and hidden light not only through the greatest force of fire, or calcination <term>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical constitution</term>—but even more, that being kindled and exalted by external light (whether solar or terrestrial) and by heat as if anew, its heat and light are not extinguished by cold water or another liquid, but it retains its vigor, heat, and light unhurt, safe, and whole. *In all these things, I say, the Phosphorus*

<meta>catchword: Her-</meta>

<summary>This page compares the light-retention properties of the Bologna Stone and Balduin's Hermetic Phosphorus, noting that while both lose their glow over time, Balduin's discovery exhibits superior resilience by glowing even when submerged in acid or cold water.</summary>

<keywords>Bologna Stone, Hermetic Phosphorus, Christian Adolph Balduin, phosphorescence, spirit of nitre, nitric acid, calcination, light-bearing power</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical comparison between the Bologna Stone and Christian Adolph Balduin's "Hermetic Phosphorus." While the previous page listed similarities, this section discusses where Balduin’s substance outperforms the natural stone, specifically regarding its behavior in water, while introducing the unique color properties of the Bologna Stone.</meta>

# SECTION I. CHAPTER II.

The Hermetic Phosphorus <note>original: "Phosphorus Hermeticus," a luminous substance discovered by Balduin, typically made from calcium nitrate.</note> seems to surpass our Bologna Stone. Although I confess that I have never attempted such things for the sake of testing them, nor have I undertaken any thoughts of investigating them through experiment. I believe, however, that our stone does not shine when submerged in liquid or cold water because I know that once it has been calcined <note>Calcination is a process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical state, essential for making the Bologna stone glow.</note>, it easily drinks in water again, since before its preparation and calcination, it is ground into a fine powder and mixed with water. Whether the Hermetic Phosphorus, when submerged in water, also absorbs it into itself or not, our most famous Author [Balduin] will declare. Likewise, experience—the mistress of all things—will one day teach us whether our Bologna Stone can also perform such feats or not.

In the meantime, however, because I can affirm or proclaim nothing of this wonderful light-bearing quality regarding the Bologna Stone, which the famous Balduin has brought forth here concerning his Hermetic Phosphorus: therefore, let that Phosphorus now carry off the prize <note>original: "brabeum," a Greek-derived term for a prize or trophy in a contest.</note>, and let our Bologna Stone yield to it—a stone which was once so greatly admired, and to which the most famous Liceti <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a philosopher and scientist who wrote extensively on the "litholight" of the Bologna Stone.</note> throughout his work *On the Nature of Stones* (chapter 24 and elsewhere) denies that anything similar exists in nature regarding its light-bearing power.

But "day teaches day," <note>original: "dies diem docet," a proverb meaning that wisdom grows over time.</note> and just as the nourishing Sun produces, removes, and restores individual things each day: so too, in their appointed ages, the Sons of Apollo and the grandsons of Hermes <note>Poetic terms for chemists and alchemists.</note>—those for whom 
> the Titan fashioned hearts from a better clay <note>original: "de meliore luto finxit præcordia Titan," a quote from Juvenal's Satires referring to Prometheus creating a more refined class of human beings.</note> 
—bring forth the hidden arts of nature from the abyss of ignorance and restore those that were lost. 

As for the points which our most famous Author places in locations 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, and 14, these do not concern the universal magnetic light-bearing force, but rather its causes or other things to be discovered by the benefit of this Hermetic Phosphorus.

### CHAPTER III.

->*Concerning the light-bearing quality of the Bologna Stone regarding the colors proper to it, which the Hermetic Phosphorus does not seem to possess.*<-

Our Bologna Stone, therefore, shares several properties with the Hermetic Phosphorus of the most famous Balduin in terms of its light-bearing power, yet it does not share the most principal and excellent ones. However, by reason of the colors of the light, our stone seems to claim something peculiar and proper to itself. For although the ordinary color of the light in both luminaries is like glowing iron or a fiery coal—namely, red, like vermilion—I have nevertheless also observed light in it of a blue color, like verdigris, or a glow-worm, or burning sulfur, or a candle just now be-

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "ardere" (to burn) which is continued on the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author concedes that Balduin’s synthetic phosphorus currently holds more "prizes" for its durability, particularly in water, but argues that the Bologna Stone possesses unique color variations—specifically blues and greens—that the synthetic version lacks.</summary>
<keywords>Bologna Stone, Hermetic Phosphorus, Balduin, calcination, light-bearing power, alchemical philosophy, luminescence, Fortunio Liceti, color of light</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the light-emitting properties of the Bologna Stone, specifically focusing on the observations of Ovidio Montalbani regarding the different colors of light it produces compared to Balduin’s Hermetic Phosphorus.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>ON THE LIGHT-BEARING QUALITY OF THE BOLOGNA STONE, ETC.</header>

...beginning to glow. The *most famous Ovidio Montalbani, a Professor at Bologna* (whom I once heard lecturing from his Chair, having enjoyed a great friendship with that *most learned man*), also observed this same thing. Our *most famous Liceti* <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a philosopher and scientist who wrote extensively on the Bologna Stone.</note> shows this in his *Lithophosphorus*, chapter 27, saying: *The most famous Montalbani observed three differences regarding the colors: first, a vivid light, in deep red like a glowing coal or hot iron; then a bluish light, scarcely more than a light sulfur flame; and finally, very rare fiery sparks, as if they were embers.* However, that blue color—or according to *Montalbani*, "sub-blue"—is mixed with a certain greenness, such as is usually found in <term>verdigris</term> <note>original: "viridi ære," a green pigment formed on copper.</note>, <term>sulfur</term> <note>The text uses the alchemical symbol 🜍 for sulfur.</note>, and <term>Vitriol of Venus</term> <note>original: "vitriolo Veneris," an old term for copper sulfate.</note>, etc. Furthermore, it happened to me only once that I saw, in a certain calcined Bologna Stone, a light glowing with a dark red. I believe this is the one that *the famous Montalbani* described as resembling very rare fiery sparks. These two colors—namely the bluish-green and the glowing ember-like sparks—were not noted by the *most famous Balduin* in his *Hermetic Phosphorus*. From this, it is presumed that his Phosphorus lacks these colors. Yet I would not want to deny that the *famous Author* [Balduin] might eventually be able to tint his "Gold of the Air" <note>original: "Aurum Auræ," Balduin's poetic name for his phosphorus.</note> with these colors (if he wishes to devote study and labor to this matter), so that its light might yield such colors of itself in the dark.

# CHAPTER IV.
->On the Causes of the Colors of Light in the *Bologna Stone and the Hermetic Phosphorus.*<-

In order to inquire briefly into the immediate causes of the colors in the light of the *Bologna Stone*, we shall maintain the three-fold order of colors observed in our *Bologna Stone* by the *famous Montalbani*.

The *first*, ordinary, and principal color of light in our Stone, which it has in common with the <term>Hermetic Phosphorus</term>, is deep red, such as is usually seen in a coal or glowing iron. If you wished to name this color as a *substantial* one, according to the usual colors of painters with which they strive to represent common fire, coals, or glowing iron on a canvas, it would be necessary to call it <term>minium</term> <note>Red lead pigment.</note>, or a color similar to minium mixed from <term>cinnabar</term> <note>A bright red mineral (mercuric sulfide).</note> and <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: "auripigmentum," a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral.</note> or yellow <term>plumbagine</term> <note>A lead-based pigment.</note>. From where, then, does this red-golden color in common fire come? From the source, I answer, of all fire, heat, and color: namely, the nourishing Sun, the king of the stars, the golden-haired Apollo. He, penetrating and permeating all things with his
fostering heat and golden color, communicates the quality of his golden-red <term>Sulfur</term> to all things that are fit to receive such <term>Sulfur</term>...

...imparts [it] to suitable things: from which a flame, once again excited, results in such a color as had been previously generated and impressed upon them: in the flame, indeed, a shining golden hue, but in the coals and ashes, a <term>vermilion</term> <note>original: "miniaceus," referring to the color of minium or red lead.</note> and red. Whoever can provide a truer cause for this color in light and fire, I shall gladly follow in his footsteps.

The second color in the light of our stone appears to me as blue-green; however, to the *most famous Montalbani*, it is "sub-blue" <note>original: "subcæruleus," a bluish or pale blue color.</note>. I maintain that this color arises from <term>Sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the principle of combustibility and heat.</note> and <term>Vitriol of Venus</term> <note>original: "Vitriolo Veneris," an old term for copper sulfate.</note>. "Kind Venus," the <term>Phosphorus</term> <note>The "Light-Bringer" or Morning Star.</note> and <term>Hesperus</term> <note>The Evening Star.</note> of the ancients: now preceding <term>Apollo</term> <note>The Sun.</note>, now following him: the herald of both Day and Night. If the adornment of our "earthly Venus" <note>A reference to copper, which was associated with the planet Venus.</note> must flow from her, it is necessary that she be dressed and clothed in a blue-green garment, just as is found within her. She holds her own subjects, which she tints with such a color, sometimes more, sometimes less. In our "Venus" [copper] there is an abundance of <term>Sulfur</term> and blue <term>Vitriol</term>; and in blue Vitriol and Sulfur, there is also Venus. She is most fond of fire and clear air—so much so that almost no fire is kindled which does not, right at its start before the full flame arises, reflect a blue-green color in the manner of burning sulfur. Indeed, the base of any flame, whether it be from a <term>wick</term> <note>original: "ellychnium."</note> or from wood, displays this color at its sides.

Venus <note>represented by the symbol ♀.</note> tints all liquids with her <term>verdigris</term> <note>original: "æruginoso," the green color characteristic of oxidized copper.</note> taste and color; indeed, she fills the very air with her scent and color. Our "artificial <term>Sal ammoniac</term>" <note>original: "Sal armoniacum," ammonium chloride used in alchemical processes.</note>, when cast into a fire, produces a blue-green flame; it contracted this color from the copper vessel in which the Sal ammoniac had been prepared. Thus in blacksmiths' workshops or chemical laboratories, where even a tiny amount of Venus [copper], sulfur, or vitriol has been mixed with the coals, a bluish flame frequently emerges. Copper itself, in an open fire, enriches the flame with this color. And what is truly marvelous: if you blow a candle flame through a <term>blowpipe</term> <note>original: "siphunculum," a small tube used to direct a flame.</note>—the kind goldsmiths use to solder their silver-work with the help of <term>borax</term> or another medium—onto a copper object composed of sulfur or Vitriol of Venus, at the very moment the flame touches that copper object, the entire flame becomes tinted with a blue-green color, right down to the wick. This happens despite the force of the breath being blown, which one might assume would drive away the vitriolic-sulfurous or copper "effluvia" <note>Vapors or microscopic particles given off by a substance.</note> rather than allowing them to resist the flame’s force or spread throughout it. The flame absorbs the copper particles so rapidly that no force of air or breath can hinder it. From this, it is probable that our Venus consists entirely of fiery and sulfurous parts,

...and that the air is filled with such outpourings and "effluvia" <note>Invisible particles or vapors thought to flow from all physical bodies.</note> of all things. # CHAPTER V.

### *On the Causes of the White Color in the light of the Bologna Stone and of other things.*

The third and final color of light observed in our Bologna Stone by the most famous <term>Montalbani</term> <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), a Bolognese philosopher and scientist who wrote extensively on the stone.</note> is that the light in it sometimes resembles very rare, fiery sparks <note>original: "favillas," small glowing embers or sparks.</note>. Since every spark above the ashes—under which the hiding fire still glows red—shines with a white color, the color of the light in the Bologna Stone will therefore sometimes be white: a thing which I happened to see only once, and then only dimly. To those seeking the causes of this, the mixture of <term>white sulfur</term>, <term>silver sulfur</term>, and <term>tin sulfur</term>, as well as <term>saline-nitrous</term> particles, seem to be the reason to me.

Our <term>Diana</term> <note>In alchemy, Diana represents the Moon and the metal silver.</note> shines white with her swans, and with <term>Jove</term> <note>Jupiter, the alchemical representative of tin.</note> she imparts much whiteness to the things she falls upon: she illuminates and surpasses <term>Venus</term> <note>Copper.</note> herself in whiteness, especially when she has acquired moisture. Go out into the deep sea, and on a night when the sky is clear (while your ship, with a calmer movement of the air, furrows the deepest sea, such as the Atlantic), throw even the lightest things into the water—even the smallest droplets of your spit, scattered from your mouth like dew—and you will see silvery little sparks shining back in the sea itself, as many as the droplets of your spit that fall into the water like dew. <term>Rotten wood</term> <note>A reference to "foxfire," the bioluminescence produced by certain fungi on decaying wood.</note> does not exhibit its silver splendor at night if it is deprived of its moisture: for when it is robbed of its parts of red sulfur, the white sulfur remains with the nitrous particles...

...but it is necessary that in moisture, wood reflects a bright white color at night. <note>The author refers to "foxfire," the bioluminescence produced by certain fungi on decaying wood.</note>

Once, when I was a student, I was calcining <note>Heating a substance to high temperatures to induce chemical change.</note> antimony with nitre <note>Saltpeter (potassium nitrate).</note> in the common manner within a small pot, so that I might produce what is commonly called a "diaphoretic" <term>diaphoretic</term> <note>A medicine used to induce sweating, thought to purge the body of "ill humors."</note>. When I had stirred the material in the crucible with a spatula, and had hidden the fire beneath with bricks so it would not be visible, it was a dark place and evening time; the stirred material appeared to me—especially at the sides of the pot—entirely silvery, with a very bright white light, though with a tiny bit of green mixed in.

I attributed the cause to some white sulfur which the antimony contained when mixed with the nitre, although this phenomenon never shone for me again, even when I sought it out several times afterward. *Very white sugar*, which is considered the best and is quite hard, will exhibit a white light like sparks if you scrape some of it off with a knife in the dark. This is due to the salt-nitrous parts mixed with white sulfur. <note>This phenomenon is now known as triboluminescence, where light is generated through the breaking of chemical bonds when a material is scratched, crushed, or rubbed.</note>

I believe these same particles also exit from the hair and body of a **cat** in the manner of a shining outpouring <note>original: "effluvii," referring to invisible particles thought to flow from bodies.</note>, when the skin of the cat is frequently rubbed with the hand in the dark against the grain—that is, from the tail toward the head. On this subject, *Juan Nieremberg* <note>Spanish Jesuit and natural historian (1595–1658).</note> in Book 1, Chapter 7 of his *History of Exotic Nature*, and from him our most celebrated *Baldwin* <note>Christian Adolph Balduin, discoverer of "Baldwin's Phosphorus" (calcium nitrate).</note> in his *Phosphorus* prefixed here, reports a story of a noble girl who, with a comb, shook flames from her hair into her lap like burning stars in the sky. I can solemnly affirm that this very thing happened to me when I was still a young man. In my father's house, when I rose one day very early in the morning to attend the sacred service at church, I went from my bedroom into the heated room <term>hypocaustum</term> <note>original: "hypocaustum," a room heated by a stove or underfloor system, common in Central and Northern Europe.</note> (it was near the end of February). Sitting there by the stove and dressing myself, I began to comb my hair with a brush made of pig bristles. And behold! As soon as I pushed the comb downward from my head and hair, innumerable tiny sparks shone brightly—all of a white color—and were shaken out from the hair. Terrified at first by this sight, I looked at the comb, the stove, and whatever might be in front of or behind my head. But when nothing else presented itself to my sight, I combed my hair again; and as many times as I repeated the act of combing, so many times did the tiny sparks fall from my hair. Immediately I went to the window (there was at least one through which the morning dawn could fall into the chamber, which was 50 feet long but no more than 12 wide) so that I could see the spectacle by the light of the rising day as well; however, it did not present itself as clearly to the eyes there as it did in the darker place behind the stove. On this morning, more time was devoted to combing than usually to more serious matters, because of this spectacle. On another morning and...

After that, I experienced nothing of the sort myself. However, the distinguished Johann Manzius, Rector of the Bielefeld School, while I was overseeing the printing of these pages in Bielefeld, told me that once, as a young man recovering from a serious illness, he was going to bed in the dark and unfastening the buttons of his tunic when many sparks of fire suddenly fell from his chest. This same man, in the flower of his youth, several times observed little sparks while combing his hair in the dark.

Although I could attribute the cause of this phenomenon to certain effluvia <note>Invisible emissions or "vapors" thought by early scientists to flow from bodies.</note> of the head and hair—which contain white <term>Sulfur</term><note>The original uses the alchemical symbol 🜍</note> mixed with a nitrous salt—I fear there may have been another hidden cause at work, since those sparks from his head occurred only during one particular early morning. From these examples, it is clear that the cause of white light in the dark consists chiefly of white Sulfur (in which <term>Saturn</term><note>Alchemical symbol ♄, representing lead</note> and Jupiter <note>Representing tin</note> abound) mixed with saline-nitrous particles, often joined with a certain moisture. Thus, when our "Stone of Apollo" <note>A poetic reference to the Bologna Stone, associated here with the sun/gold</note> has acquired a salty mixture of gold and the silver of the Moon by the will of Jupiter, it produces light in the dark. According to the most celebrated Montalbanus, <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), an Italian scientist who wrote extensively on the Bologna Stone.</note> this light exhibits very rare, fire-like embers.

# CHAPTER VI.
## On the Property of Sulfur and the Stench in the Bologna Stone
### *As it differs from the Hermetic Phosphorus of the distinguished Mr. Christian Adolph Balduin.*

Although I discussed the triple sulfur found in the Bologna Stone in relation to its light in the preceding chapter, our Stone is distinguished from "Hermetic Phosphorus" <note>A phosphorescent substance (calcium nitrate) discovered by the alchemist Christian Adolph Balduin in 1674.</note> by the massive stench of the sulfur within it. I presume Hermetic Phosphorus to be a most pure entity without any stench; therefore, I wish to discourse briefly here on the sulfur and the stench of the Bologna Stone. In those Bologna "Light-Bearers" <note>Lapis Luciferis; stones prepared to glow in the dark.</note>—especially those that lacked the quality of glowing—I detected a most foul-smelling sulfur. For while I attempted to calcine <note>To heat a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical structure.</note> them in a fierce fire, I saw a certain yellow-colored sulfur, like our common sulfur, being cooked out of the cracks in the stones in the very heat of the fire. This sulfur was not consumed by the fire but persisted within it, merely giving rise to little bubbles. I perceived no flame emerging from the stones from the start of the calcination to the end, even though the stench from the sulfur filled the air. Therefore, the extracted stones (which I had placed in the fire whole, not crushed) spread a putrid stench, similar to the Sulfur of Antimony <note>Stibium, or antimony sulfide.</note> when it is mixed with salt...

<meta>This page continues the author’s chemical analysis of the "Bologna Stone" (a type of barite that glows in the dark), moving from a general discussion of its composition to specific observations about the sulfurous impurities that give the stone its characteristic smell and its ability to tarnish metals.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>SECTION I. CHAPTER VI.</header>

The stone is <term>calcined</term><note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a fine powder or change its properties.</note> with tartar or with the ashes of burnt beech wood, and its <term>Sulfur</term><note>In this context, sulfur refers both to the element and to the alchemical principle of combustibility and "soul."</note> is extracted using heat. Iron sometimes gives off such a stench when processed with Sulfur; so too does Arsenic. I had accidentally stored these calcined Stones near some silver and copper coins, which the Stones had so infected with their Sulfurous <term>effluvia</term><note>Invisible vapors or particles thought to flow from bodies and affect their surroundings.</note> and blackness that I could barely recognize my silver *Julios* or my copper *bezones* <note>original: "Julios meos argenteos, cupreosque... bezones". The *Giulio* was a silver coin minted in the Papal States, and the *bezzone* (or *bezzo*) was a low-value Venetian copper coin.</note> as the Italians call them. Nor could I easily remove the blackness from them again, even with a wash. This is proof that the Sulfur in the Bologna Stone is coarse and <term>fixed</term><note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that remains stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to intense heat.</note> in some way, as it does not burst into flame in the fire, but persists in it for quite a long time. Under the force of the fire, it only gives off bubbles, as if it were mixed with some fixed salt that would likewise render the Sulfur stable. 

Hence, since after strong heating and calcination the Stone still breathes out Sulfurous vapors that tarnish metals, it must be that the Sulfur in it is not entirely fixed, but only in part. For that which is entirely fixed endures the torture of the fire and remains untouched within it, emitting no vapors—just as gold does. However, the fact that the Sulfur in it is coarse and earthy is proven by its stench after heating. If the Sulfur in it were pure and devoid of all earthiness, it would emit no stench, but in its purity would also remain fixed in the fire. 

From this I conclude that the famous *Hermetic Phosphorus* of Baldwin <note>Christian Adolph Balduin (1632–1682) was a rival chemist who discovered a different phosphorescent substance made from chalk and nitric acid.</note> consists of a purer and more fixed Sulfur. Because that "Gold" (or solar Sulfur) comes to it from a simpler Atmosphere, it is undoubtedly also free of all stench. For this reason, our Bologna Stones are more transparent, purer, and better suited for capturing light when they contain less Sulfur, provided they have endured the fire of calcination. Poterius also noted long ago in his *Spagyric Pharmacy* (Book 2, Chapter 27) that a foul-smelling Sulfur is present in our stones.

He adds further that they possess a very sharp, biting, and truly arsenical salt. This is found to be true, as they possess a caustic power after heating, and <term>Psilothra</term><note>From the Greek *psilothron*, a substance used to remove hair; a depilatory.</note> are easily prepared from them. Furthermore, Poterius says in the place cited that its Sulfur is not as fleeting as that of arsenic, but rather remains fixed in the fire and endures strong calcination. It is necessary, therefore, that the Sulfur in our stones be mixed with a certain fixed salt, which preserves it unharmed in the fire and does not allow it to be consumed so quickly.

# SECTION TWO.
## CHAPTER I.
### Concerning the Birthplace of the Light-Bearing Stone and whether it is found anywhere except in the territory of Bologna.

Before I proceed to the natural causes of the **Bologna Stone**, I have seen fit to first discuss its **Birthplace**, and what other natural fossil things may be found in the said location, so that with these things known, we may better inquire into the nature and cause of our Stone. Since this *stone* is called *Bolognese* by us, its *birthplace* is indicated by this epithet: namely **Bologna** of the Italians, the *nourishing mother of studies* <note>original: "alma studiorum mater," a traditional title for the University of Bologna, the oldest university in the Western world.</note> as she is absolutely called, commendable both in name and in fact. This Bolognese soil produced this light-bearing stone approximately seventy years before I wrote these words. For in the year 1604, a certain citizen of Bologna (*as the illustrious Licetus relates in "On the Light-Bearing Stone," chapter 2* <note>Fortunio Liceto (1577–1657), an Italian physician and philosopher who wrote *Lithheosphoros* (1640) about the Bologna Stone.</note>) named **Vincenzo Casciarolo**, a man devoted to Chemistry <term>transferred from the cobbler's trade to gold-making</term> <note>original: "ad Chrysopœiam à sutrina translatus." *Chrysopoeia* is the alchemical term for the transmutation of base metals into gold.</note>, when he found this stone heavy and pregnant with Solar Sulfur, he called it "Solar," and judged it fit for **gold-making**. This man prepared this Stone—*having been subjected to much labor beforehand—not so that it might produce gold from itself, but so that it might swallow into itself the golden light of the Sun, and steal the celestial treasure from heaven like a new Prometheus*, (*to use the very words of the illustrious Licetus.*) More concerning the occasion of the inventor's discovery may be read in Poterius, *Book 2 of Spagyric Pharmacy, chapter 27.* <note>Pierre Potier (Poterius), a French physician and chemist active in the early 17th century.</note> The inventor was, therefore, one devoted to Chemistry.

O divine art! which has opened such divine things to us mortals. For what is more sublime than that art, which is bitten by so many teeth of envy? For just as Sacrosanct Theology is infested in infinite ways by Satan: so too this art, which searches out the hidden recesses of **NATURE**, the daughter of God, is attacked by the members of Satan. But let the most illustrious **Ole Borch** <note>original: "Olaus Borrichius," a Danish polymath known for his defense of alchemy.</note> and others treat the true praises of Chemistry and vindicate it against its adversaries. It is not my place to digress further here, but rather to adorn the runaway from the cobbler’s shop, **Casciarolo**, with his own tribute: that being desirous of this divine art, he brought forth this light-bearing Stone of **Bologna** through his Chemical labors; even if, while seeking gold, he uselessly squandered gold upon himself and his master (*as Poterius testifies*). This is something that most certainly happens to those who seek only gold through this art and gape greedily after it. Furthermore, the specific birthplace

<meta>This page transitions from the general history of the Bologna Stone to its specific geographic origin on Mount Paternus. It discusses reports of similar stones found elsewhere and begins a geological description of the surrounding Apennine region.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
<header>SECTION II. CHAPTER I.</header>

There is a mountain near *Bologna* called *Mount Paternus* (commonly *Monte Paderno*), which I visited quite often while I was studying Medicine at Bologna, so that I might seek out our Stone in its native soil. This birthplace, and the road by which one travels to it from the city, was described most exquisitely by *Potier* <note>Pierre Potier (c. 1581–1640), also known as Petrus Poterius, was a French physician and chemist who lived in Bologna and wrote extensively on the stone's preparation.</note> in the place I have frequently cited; indeed, even *Apelles* <note>A famous painter of ancient Greece, often used as a shorthand for the pinnacle of artistic skill.</note>, the glory of painters, could not have presented it better to our sight with his skillful brush. In the said Mount Paternus, after heavy rains that run down like a torrent, the **Bologna Stone** is washed out and found in the open cracks and fissures of the mountain.

However, although this light-bearing stone has so far only been found in the territory of Bologna, I would not dare to assert that it exists nowhere else but in the Bolognese region. For if *De Thou’s History, Book 6* <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1533–1617) was a French historian; the author is referencing his monumental history of his own times.</note> does not exceed belief, it mentions a certain wonderful light-bearing stone brought from the Indies in the year 1550 by a certain man who was in *Boulogne* <note>original: "Bononia Belgarum," referring to Boulogne-sur-Mer in modern France, to distinguish it from "Bononia Italorum," the Italian Bologna.</note>. Thus, not only will Italian Bologna be fertile in light-bearing stones, but some place in the Indies as well. So too, *Athanasius Kircher* <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a Jesuit polymath who studied everything from volcanology to microbes.</note> writes in *The Great Art of Light and Shadow, Book 3, part 4, question 2*, that the ore of this light-bearing stone is found near Toulouse. Likewise, that light-bearing flint of *Jan Baptist van Helmont* <note>A Flemish chemist and physician (1580–1644) who recognized that there were different types of "airs" (gases).</note>, which he discusses in his treatise *The Great Necessity*, must have originated from a different fatherland than Bologna, since our Bologna Stone is not of the flint variety. Indeed, our stone recognizes as its birthplace not only Mount Paternus near Bologna, but also the fields called *Roncharia* and *Pradalbino*, as the most illustrious *Potier* adds his support to my assertions.

## CHAPTER II.

->*Concerning other things that are found in the birthplace of the Bologna Stone.*<-

The most illustrious city of Bologna is situated at the foot of the Apennine mountains, which divide nearly all of Italy down the middle. As soon as you set foot or move outside the city gate called *San Mammolo*, desiring to take yourself to Mount Paternus, you are forced to climb the Apennines. In this stretch, there are huge mountains consisting of pure **gypsum stone**, under which the inhabitants build fires <note>original: "ignem substruunt," referring to the process of calcining gypsum.</note> so that they may burn the gypsum into lime for constructing and whitening buildings. **Gypsum**, or **gypsum stone**, is of the limestone family: it is transparent, sometimes striated, sometimes scaly and flaky like *talc* or *specular stone* <note>original: "lapis specularis," a transparent mineral like selenite or mica that was split into thin sheets to be used as window panes before glass was common.</note>. However, gypsum differs from specular stone and talc, because these cannot as easily [be burned by]

->### OF OTHER THINGS WHICH ARE IN THE PLACE, etc.<-

...to be overcome by fire and burned into lime [more easily] than gypsum or common limestone. For there is a certain *kind of talc* that can hardly be subdued by fire, such as *Muscovite* <note>original: *Moscoviticum*; a form of mica widely used in Russia for windows</note> and a certain kind of *specular stone* <term>Lapis specularis: a transparent mineral, often mica or selenite, that could be split into thin sheets to be used like window glass</term>, especially that which serves in place of glass in lanterns and is flexible; it is not easily conquered by fire. Gypsum, however, even at the flame of a lamp-wick, loses its transparency and consistency, and easily turns into white lime. Bolognese Gypsum <note>The author is likely referring to the Bologna Stone itself, which is chemically a form of barium sulfate (barite), though he calls it "gypsum" due to its similar appearance.</note> strongly resembles specular stone, since it is entirely transparent and can also be separated into very thin sheets, just like specular stone: but this Gypsum is not of such great size as specular stone is, nor, as I hinted, does it resist fire, but it is very easily calcined. However, I did not find types of Talc and specular stone in the said place or around Bologna, even though the *famous Licetus* <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), an Italian physician and philosopher who wrote a major work on the Bologna Stone in 1640.</note> asserts in *chapter 1 of his Lithologia* that they are found there. The same author also writes in the cited place that *Agate was dug up in the stone's birthplace; [and] that Diamond, similar to the Bohemian kind, Jaspers, marcasites of every kind, Sal ammoniac, Vitriol, Antimony, "Jew Stone" and rarer plants exist there.*

I did not encounter Agate and Jaspers, although I would not wish to deny everything that I have not seen. However, I did collect transparent flints there, from which European "diamonds" <note>In this context, usually referring to "Bohemian diamonds," which are actually clear, high-quality quartz crystals.</note> are polished. For these had once become known to me when I was living in *Prussia* <note>original: *Borussia*</note> as a student of medicine. It happened to me once that I had dissected a stork, taken from its nest, with an anatomical knife, in whose stomach among other pebbles I also removed a "diamond" flint of the best quality; which, once polished, far surpassed the Bohemian kind in excellence. Such flints, from which today crystalline vessels of the best kind are forged, are found almost everywhere, especially where streams and lively springs gush from the earth, and the purer sand is washed out by nature: as is well known in the County of *Cleves* <note>A historical territory on the lower Rhine, now divided between Germany and the Netherlands.</note>. Nor did it happen that I saw Antimony and "Jew Stone" <term>Lapis Judaicus: fossilized sea urchin spines, historically used to treat kidney stones</term> there; although perhaps they are present. For from the antimonial and arsenical stench which our calcined stones exhale (as will be discussed below), it is easily established that an ore of Antimony also lies hidden there. *Jew Stone* was once found by me in the fields of *Prussia*, but otherwise nowhere else. On Mount Paternus near Bologna, it also happened to me to see a very large stone, weighing more than a hundred pounds. This stone so exactly resembled the figure of a half-man, like a statue, that it seemed to have been carved from the stone into such a form by art. For in appearance it recalled the head, neck, arms, and genitals. So that I might have it explored whether [it was made] by art, or truly by the effort of nature, in

SECTION II. CHAPTER I.

*Near Bologna is a mountain called Mount Paternus (commonly known as Monte Paderno), to which I frequently betook myself when I was a student of medicine at Bologna, so that I might seek out our Stone in its native soil. This birthplace, and the path by which one goes to it from the city, has been described most exquisitely by Poterius <note>Pierre Potier (1581–1640), a French physician who lived in Bologna and wrote about the stone’s medicinal and chemical properties.</note> in the place often cited, so much so that even Apelles, the glory of painters, could not better set it before our sight with his skillful brush. In the said Mount Paternus, after huge rains running down from it like a torrent, the Bolognese Stone is washed out into the open cracks and fissures of the mountain and found.*

Although this light-bearing stone <note>original: *Lapis lucifer*; referring to its phosphorescent properties</note> has so far only been found in the territory of Bologna: nevertheless, I would not dare to assert that it exists nowhere else but in the territory of Bologna. For if the *History of Thuanus* <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a famous French historian.</note>, book 6, does not exceed belief, concerning a certain wonderful light-bearing Stone, which in the year 1550 was brought from the *Indies* by some man who was from *Boulogne-sur-Mer* <note>original: *Bononia Belgarum*; the author is distinguishing between the French and Italian cities that share a Latin name</note>, then not only will the *Bologna of the Italians* be fertile in light-bearing stones, but some place in the *Indies* as well. Thus *Athanasius Kircher* <note>A famous Jesuit polymath (1602–1680) known for his study of light and magnetism.</note> *in The Great Art of Light and Shadow, book 3, part 4, question 2* writes that the ore of this light-bearing stone is found near *Toulouse*. Likewise, that light-bearing flint of *Jan Baptist van Helmont* <note>A pioneering Flemish chemist (1580–1644).</note>, of which he speaks in his treatise *The Great Necessity* <note>original: *Magnum Oportet*</note>, will have its origin from another homeland than *Bologna*, since our *Bolognese Stone* is not of the genus of flints. Indeed, our stone itself recognizes as its birthplace not only Mount Paternus near Bologna, but also the fields called *Roncharia* and *Pradalbino*, as the most famous *Poterius* adds his support <note>original: *calculum addit*; literally "adds a pebble," an ancient way of casting a vote of approval</note> to my assertions.

## CHAPTER II.
### *Of other things which are found in the birthplace of the Bolognese Stone.*

At the foot of the *Apennine Mountains*, which cut through almost the middle of all *Italy*, the most famous *City of Bologna* is situated. For as soon as you set foot outside the *city gate* called *San Mamolo*, or move so as to desire to take yourself to *Mount Paternus*, you are forced to ascend the *Apennines*. In this stretch, there are huge mountains consisting of pure gypsous stone, under which the inhabitants place fire so that they may burn the gypsum into lime <note>The process of heating minerals to create a powdery substance used in plaster and mortar.</note>, for constructing and whitewashing buildings. Gypsum, or gypsous stone, is of the genus of calcareous stone: transparent, sometimes striated, sometimes scaly and can be split <note>original: *scissile*</note> like talc or specular stone <term>Lapis specularis: a transparent mineral, often mica or selenite, used in antiquity for windows.</term>. However, gypsum differs from specular stone and talc in that these cannot be so easily...

### CONCERNING OTHER THINGS WHICH ARE IN THE PLACE, ETC.

[be overcome] by fire and burned into lime, than gypsum or common limestone. For there is a certain kind of talc that can hardly be conquered by fire, such as *Muscovite glass* <note>original: *Moscoviticum*; a type of mica known as Muscovite, often used for windows in Russia</note> and a certain *mirror stone* <note>original: *Lapis specularis*; usually refers to selenite or large sheets of mica</note>, especially that which takes the place of glass in lamps and is flexible; it is not easily overcome by fire. Gypsum, however, even at the flame of a lamp's wick, loses its transparency and consistency, and easily turns into white lime. Bolognese Gypsum greatly mimics the mirror stone, since it is entirely transparent and can also be separated into very thin sheets, just like the mirror stone. But this Gypsum is not of such great size as the mirror stone is, nor, as I have hinted, does it resist fire, but it is very easily calcined. However, I did not find types of talc and the mirror stone in the said place or around *Bologna*, although the famous *Licetus* <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), an Italian physician and philosopher who wrote a famous treatise on the Bolognese stone.</note> in book 1 of his work on stones affirms that they are found in that same place. He also writes in the cited passage that *Agate was dug up in the native place of the stone; and that Diamonds, similar to the Bohemian ones, Jaspers, all kinds of marcasites <note>A term historically used for various sulfide minerals, often iron pyrite</note>, Sal ammoniac, Vitriol, Antimony, Jew’s stone <note>original: *lapis Judaicus*; these are actually fossilized spines of sea urchins, which were used in medicine for treating kidney stones</note>, and rarer plants exist in that same place.*

I did not encounter Agate and Jaspers, although I do not wish to deny everything that I myself have not seen. Truly, I did collect there transparent flints, from which European diamonds are polished. For those had formerly become known to me when I was living in *Prussia* <note>original: *Borussia*</note> as a student of medicine. It had happened to me once that I dissected a stork, taken from its nest, with an anatomical knife; in its stomach, among other pebbles, I removed a diamond-like flint of the best quality. When it was later polished, it far surpassed the *Bohemian* diamond in excellence. Such flints, from which the best kind of crystal vessels are forged today, are found almost everywhere, especially where lively streams and springs gush out of the earth and the purer sand is washed out by nature, as is well known in the County of *Cleves* <note>A historical territory in the Rhineland</note>. Nor did it happen that I saw Antimony and Jew’s stone there, although perhaps they are present. For from the antimonial and arsenical stench which our calcined stones exhale (as will be said below), it is easily established that a vein of Antimony also lies hidden there. The *Jew’s stone* was once found by me in the fields of *Prussia*, but nowhere else besides. On Mount *Paternus* near *Bologna*, it also happened that I saw a very large stone, weighing more than a hundred pounds. This stone, like a statue, so exactly mimicked the figure of a half-man that it seemed to have been carved into such a form from the stone by art. For it represented in its appearance the head, neck, arms, and belly. Therefore, so that I might have it investigated whether it was from art or truly by the industry of nature, in

But I shall speak of Marcasites more extensively in the following chapter.

# CHAPTER III.

->*Concerning Marcasites and other things which are found in the native place of the Bolognese Stone.*<-

Marcasites are of two kinds: some are called *golden*, and others *silver*. These latter approach the nature of metal more closely. For in **Mount Bructerus** <note>The Brocken, the highest peak in the Harz Mountains of Germany, famous for its mining history.</note> and other places, it is dug out just as other metals are accustomed to being found, clinging to their rocks and clumps of earth. The ore of this mineral around the said mountain consists of flint stone, rarely Sulfur, but is most especially mixed with a leaden and silvery luster. From this crushed ore, silver Marcasite is melted down, destined for various uses. But that which is called golden Marcasite seems to me to be of an entirely different nature and distinct from the silver Marcasite. The common people call it "golden" because it consists of a golden color, though for the most part it is copper or brassy. In these Marcasites, a great deal of Sulfur <note>The author uses the chemical symbol Δ for sulfur.</note> is detected, and Vitriol <note>The author uses the symbol ♀ (the sign for Venus/Copper) to indicate copper vitriol or blue vitriol.</note> is washed out of them. For after all the Sulfur <note>Δ</note> has been consumed by the force and flame of fire, a red earth remains from which Copper <note>♀</note> can be prepared by the art of Vulcan <note>The "art of Vulcan" refers to metallurgy or smelting.</note>. This Marcasite is found in the native place of our Stone. These Bolognese Marcasites are *pyrite stones* <note>original: *Pyritae lapides*; literally "fire-stones," so named because they strike sparks.</note>, of that kind which our German Knights have hitherto been accustomed to adapt for their wheel-lock pistols, such as are also found around *Bologna*. But more correctly, in my opinion, I could name these Marcasites a *sulfur ore* that contains copper or iron; just as various sulfur ores of this kind are dug out of the earth and the metal-bearing cliffs, like clustered crystals with a golden luster...
