# CONCERNING MARCASITES AND OTHER THINGS FOUND IN THE NATIVE PLACE OF THE BOLOGNESE STONE

[...of a golden] color, glittering beautifully, they are embedded; from these, sulfur, chalcanthum <note>original: *chalchantum*; also known as blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note> or vitriol, and finally copper <note>represented by the symbol ♀</note> or iron <note>referred to as Mars</note>, or both, are prepared by metallurgical arts. The size of these Bolognese Marcasites <note>A historical term for iron pyrite or "fool's gold," often found in globular or crystalline forms.</note> is not great: for they are collected in small pieces here and there *on the mountain called Paternus*, and they do not exceed three ounces in weight. Their shape is irregular: but they very often have the appearance of a cluster consisting of pure copper-like crystals: although I have not infrequently collected them in the shape of round globules, the diameter of which does not exceed an inch. Among these, some represent so exactly the buttons <note>original: *globulos*; here referring to decorative buttons for clothing</note> which we are accustomed to fasten to our coats, along with their stems <note>original: *pediculis*; literally "little feet," referring to the shank or attachment point of a button</note>, that I was doubtful whether these Marcasites had such a metallic beginning by chance, or were indeed produced into such a form by the work of nature alone. Therefore, this golden Marcasite is quite different from the other, which is commonly called silver; because the latter consists of much Mercury <note>the liquid metal, considered a primary element in alchemy</note> that is somewhat more fixed, and a little Sulfur: the former, however, consists of much fire <note>represented by the symbol △</note> and Vitriol, but no Mercury—unless you wish to call the metallic bodies that lie hidden in them "slightly coagulated Mercury," since Mercury is present in all metals. This seems also to be hinted at by **OUR GEBER THE ARAB**, *a man industrious and ingenious in Chemical labors, in Book 2, part 1, chapter 60 of his "Sum of Perfection" concerning the Nature of Marcasite, when he says: Marcasite has a double substance in its creation. That is, of mortified quicksilver approaching fixation, and of burning Sulfur.* <note>Geber is the latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan; the "Summa Perfectionis" is a foundational medieval alchemical text.</note>

It is as if the *Author* wishes to say here: *Marcasite is twofold: One, which consists of mortified quicksilver, etc.; the other, however, which consists of burning Sulfur.* Furthermore, our *Famous Author Liceti* <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a scholar who wrote extensively on the "Bolognese Stone" or lithophosphorus.</note> says that sal ammoniac <note>A naturally occurring mineral, ammonium chloride, often found near volcanoes.</note> is also found in the same place. Truly, concerning those things which have not been seen by me, I would not dare to pronounce absolutely. However, I found a certain kind of the light-bearing Bolognese Stone, representing very closely the appearance of what we commonly call Armenian salt <note>original: *salis armeniaci*; likely a reference to a type of salt or evaporite mineral used in dyes and medicine</note>, in the *Bolognese* territory (in the place commonly called *la Roncheria*). I intend to treat this in the following sections, where I shall deal specifically with the form, differences, and characteristics of our Stone. It pleases me to interpose here a few words on what I have seen regarding sea shells on high mountains, both around Bologna and elsewhere, and what my opinion of them is.

Not far from this mountain called *Paternus*, the *Homeland* of our stone, perhaps only a single Italian mile distant (the name of the place has slipped from memory), a huge, steep Mountain looms, eroded by the violence of torrential waters, which frequent rains flowing from neighboring mountains create, and they throw down and cast off significant masses of earth from that mountain. ## SECTION II. CHAPTER III.

In the collapse of the mountain, in its upper part, many ruins or layers are seen, consisting of the shells of marine shellfish of every kind, with a great deal of sand cast between them like "layer upon layer" <note>original: *strati superstrati*; a term used in early chemistry and geology to describe horizontal layers</note>, (as the common crowd of Chemists speaks). For between these layers or series of shellfish shells, sand is interposed to the thickness of an ell <note>A historical unit of length, roughly equivalent to the length of a man’s arm</note> and beyond. Moreover, the shells of the various shellfish were all distinct from each other, not embedded in any stone, so that everything could be handled and identified separately by hand. This was caused by the pure sand, mixed with no mud or clay, which had preserved the shells of the shellfish whole for many centuries.

Meanwhile, however, through the long passage of time, all those shells were easily reducible into a very white lime <note>original: *calcem*; calcium oxide or a similar powdery residue</note>, as if they had been calcined <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile substances</note> by the violence of fire. Having contemplated such things with wonder, I could find no other cause for why such offspring of the ocean were carried to the peaks of mountains, except for the universal flood and cataclysm of the lands. The shores of this flood were finally the peaks of the highest mountains, into which the tide of the universal ocean then drove and deposited such marine things, so that they might be a testimony of divine vengeance for us and our descendants until the final judgment: just as many of the learned confess with me. 

For it is absurd to establish that the earth, from itself and its own nature, fashions and produces such marine things without any end or use, since nothing has been produced in vain or superfluously by nature and its supreme Creator. Indeed, the harder parts of animals, such as bones and all shell-like things, can endure for many centuries without any corruption. Finally, the length of time—which is like a devouring fire for all things—turns such harder "spermatic" <note>Here used in the archaic sense of "formative" or "seed-like" parts that give structure</note> parts into lime. From this they even acquire medicinal power, and can be used within the human body against diseases, as if they had been prepared and calcined by the Chemical art through fire. 

Spectacles of such a universal inundation are seen in many parts of our earthly world. On the rock of Sparemberg Castle <note>Sparrenberg Castle in Bielefeld, Germany</note>, and in the vicinity, huge rocks are cut out, composed entirely of the shells of every kind of shellfish. They stick to the rock—indeed they adhere to each other so closely—that you would say the stones are homogeneous <note>Of the same kind; uniform in structure</note>. The mud had so occupied the shells from the beginning that it now seems like a single mass of Stone. And where the mud had been red, it has now mixed and hardened with the white shells, providing the elegance of marble. Around Colmar <note>original: *Colmariam*; a city in Alsace, France</note>, the market town of Upper Alsace, I saw the potters of that place make excellent crucibles <note>original: *tigilla*; heat-resistant containers used for melting metals</note> for melting metals from such shell-bearing stones. They crushed them into powder (for they are of a less hard consistency) and mixed them with a little very tenacious clay. In these very...

### CHAPTER IV.

->*On the Shape and Size of the Light-bearing Bolognese Stone.*<-

The distinguished Licetus <note>Fortunio Liceto (1577–1657), an Italian physician and philosopher who wrote extensively on the Bolognese Stone</note> established in Chapter 19 of his *Lithosphorus* <note>original: *Lith.*; referring to Liceto's 1640 work 'Lithosphorus, sive de Lapide Bononiensi'</note> that no certain and regular external shape is given for our Stone, since most stones in general rarely obtain a determined shape. Regarding this matter, however, a doubt once arose for me in Italy, as I found this stone, for the most part, to be of an elliptical shape. I collected some that so exactly mimicked the form and appearance of a dried plum <note>original: *pruni exsiccati*</note> that, if they were placed among real dried plums, they could not be distinguished from the rest by their external form. Concerning this singular shape of the stone, as well as on *Marcasites* <note>Iron pyrite or similar metallic-looking minerals</note>, I presented a specific little dissertation to the *most distinguished* **FORTUNIO LICETO** while he was still living, as I said above at the end of Chapter 2. It is pleasing, therefore, to briefly touch upon some of the external shapes of the Stone, and its somewhat determined *size*, which most often equals a fist or a hen's egg, more or less. *Its shape is oblong, rounded, flat, circular, or depressed: sometimes thicker, sometimes of a mixed shape. See the attached diagram.* Such shapes have also been recognized along with me by the *distinguished gentlemen Poterius, Montalbanus, Licetus, etc.* <note>Pierre Potier (Poterius) and Ovidio Montalbani were prominent 17th-century scholars interested in the stone's properties</note> It happened, however, that once in the Bolognese countryside, in the place commonly called *la Roncheria*, as I was returning to the city from Mount Paternus, I came upon a stone of immense mass. It was a white stone, weighing several hundred pounds. When I had struck off a piece from it using another harder stone and examined that piece at home by means of fire, I discovered that this stone, being of the best quality, also shone quite clearly in the dark. This stone, however, had a different internal structure than the other smaller ones. For it possessed internal oblong streaks—straight, parallel, and not converging toward any center—just as the internal substance of sal ammoniac <note term="sal armeniacus">Ammonium chloride; used here as a comparison for the stone's crystalline, fibrous internal texture</note> is accustomed to appear, to which this Stone was very similar, as its image, sketched as well as possible in *Number 1, Figure 2*, shows. The distinguished Poterius also once saw these stones with me. For he says in the place so often mentioned: *To its*

(namely, to the right of the path that leads to Mount Paternus), in the field commonly called *Roncheria*, a great quantity of a certain white stone is found, *at first glance resembling sal ammoniac* <note>Ammonium chloride; a salt that was historically highly valued in alchemy and medicine</note>. Here a doubt arises, whether the *Famous Liceto* <note>Fortunio Liceto (1577–1657), a scholar who wrote on the Bolognese Stone</note> or other authors viewed this stone as sal ammoniac, since I have not found this salt in the territory of Bologna <note>original: *Bonon.*</note>. I have seen stones of this kind used by our German gold-beaters <note>original: *Auricuforibus*</note> (which they call *Spaat* <note>original: *Spaat*; a term used by miners for various crystalline minerals like spar or feldspar</note>), for beaten gold leaves; they sprinkle the powder of this stone between them, so that the gold leaves do not stick together, but can be easily separated from one another. The nature of sal ammoniac is volatile, nor does it resist fire so easily that it can be "fixed" <note>An alchemical term meaning to make a substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate</note>. 

Here occurs a passage by the *Famous BOCCONE* <note>Paolo Boccone (1633–1704), a renowned Sicilian naturalist and botanist</note>, a Sicilian nobleman and botanist to the *Grand Duke of Tuscany*, in the book of his letters to friends, *Letter 7, page 47*, concerning Armenian salt <note>original: *sale Armeniaco*; often used interchangeably with sal ammoniac in early modern texts</note>, found upon the iron-like slag <note>original: *scorias*</note> ejected after the recent horrible eruption of Mount Etna in Sicily by the force of an almost infernal fire. This salt, as the author reports, had all the characteristics of Armenian salt, especially regarding the colors: lemon-yellow, saffron, rusty-green <note>original: *æruginosum*; the color of copper rust or verdigris</note>, and others; but it was a little more "fixed" <note>stable under heat</note>. A matter truly worthy of wonder, that Armenian salt—formerly called *Ammoniac*—which today for us Europeans is not produced by nature alone but is sold as a purely manufactured product, is of an extremely volatile nature—so much so, indeed, that by its benefit, those skilled in *Chemistry* <note>original: *Chemiæ*; refers to early modern chemical and alchemical practices</note> make volatile things fixed—yet it could withstand such an incredible fire (in which even the earth itself, and whatever was vomited out by the fire, as the *Famous Author* reports, flowed like liquid lead) and remain intact upon the left-behind slag. From this, the aforementioned *Famous Author* will explain the nature of this salt to us a little more fully, and will further illuminate our thinking regarding this salt—whether it was mixed with some gypsous stone or something of another, more fixed nature. 

Moreover, although in the year 1650 I saw by ship while sailing past Mount *Etna* in Sicily, covered and obscured by snow, clouds, and smoke, as well as the island of *Stromboli* <note>original: *Strongylen*</note>, flashing with a very high flame at the peak of the mountain, and likewise the *Volcanic Islands* and *Lipari Islands*, belching flames and smoke at their bases as if from the sea itself, and afterward I climbed *Vesuvius* (called *Monte di Somma* by the Italians), yet I happened to see nothing of any sal ammoniac. Indeed, on the theater-shaped summit of *Vesuvius*, on the part that looks toward the sea, I saw upon the ashes

### CHAPTER V.

->*On the Differences and Marks of the best light-bearing*<-
->*Bolognese Stone, and of the baser or non-shining kind.*<-

After we have touched upon the specific differences of our Stone, let us proceed in a few words to the marks of the best stone, and of those less proven to give back light in the dark. The illustrious *Licetus* <note>Fortunio Liceto (1577–1657), an Italian scholar who wrote "Lithophosphorus" in 1640, one of the first major works on the stone.</note> reviews the differences of the stone in chapter 21 of his work on stones, according to the mind of the most illustrious *Montalbani* <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), a scientist from Bologna who specialized in the local minerals.</note> and *Potier* <note>Pierre Potier (1581–1643), a French physician and chemist who lived in Bologna.</note>. Of these classifications, the latter lists three; the former, however, has four. I, following the opinion of the illustrious Montalbani, shall embrace his four differences and add a fifth regarding the glowing stone.

The first difference is: that the first stone be oblong, round, flattened, lens-shaped in figure, able to be split into fairly wide thin plates, not striated <note>Without parallel grooves or lines.</note>, with a pale crust, hard, heavy, and transparent, shining with a dark-blue clarity, in which there is a great deal of the purest gypseous <note>Resembling gypsum, though we now know the stone is actually barite.</note> substance: I add, that it should have in the middle of its "back" a certain small pit or dimple, such as is depicted in Number 1, Figure 1. This stone is judged by everyone to be the best of all. 2. The second Stone is not unlike the former, partly able to be split into thin leaves, and partly striated like *Antimony* <note>original: "Stibii". Antimony was a common reference point for metallic, needle-like mineral structures.</note>, also endowed with a smooth, pale crust. This too is light-bearing, but yields in quality to the superior one. 3. The third stone is oblong and thick like an egg, with a grey crust, sometimes smooth, sometimes rough: broken through the middle, it shows striae and rays tending from the surface of the circumference to the middle, as if toward some center. And this center is elongated like an axis from one end of the stone to the other, and (which is well worth noting) it contains not only coarse, less fixed sulfur, but also something metallic. And since the whole stone is of a gypseous nature, those striae appear like little crystals of gypsum, upon

...metallic center attached to it. This Stone, the purer and more transparent it is, the more luminous and <unclear>clearer</unclear> it becomes in the dark. 4. *The Fourth* type agrees with the preceding third in its shape and size. For this reason, it is also more impure and less recommended, because although it may be calcined <note>The process of heating a mineral to high temperatures to change its chemical structure.</note> for a very long time, it nevertheless gives back no light in the dark. See Figure 2, number 1. Concerning this stone and, by the way, concerning Armenian salt, I have treated more extensively above in the preceding chapter. Among these five varieties of stones, only that one mentioned in the 4th place is less fit for receiving light; and indeed on account of the impure, stinking sulfur. This mineral matter <note>original: "♁" (the alchemical symbol for Antimony or Earth/Stone)</note> is of a martial <note>Containing iron (associated with the planet Mars).</note> and arsenical nature, and abounds with a very sharp salt, which makes that sulfur somewhat fixed, so that it cannot be consumed by fire: about which see chapter 4, section 1, where the stinking sulfur of our stone was discussed, insofar as it differs from Hermetic Phosphorus. The remaining four varieties of stone—namely the first, second, third, and fifth—show the best marks of the stone by reason of its light-bearing quality, which the stone draws from the surrounding air impregnated with light, and gives it back in the dark. Now, the method by which these stones may be properly prepared by fire, so that they acquire this quality, must be spoken of briefly.

# ON THE PREPARATION OF THE BOLOGNA STONE, ETC.

...place, it seemed to me the best of all. I am pleased to set down his words here:

> *And indeed, he says, for the light-bearing power most desired in it, it is calcined <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical properties.</note> in two ways. First, the stone is reduced to the finest dust and calcined in a crucible with a very strong fire. Second, the same stone is reduced to dust and compressed into little cakes the size of a thaler <note>A large silver coin common in Europe at the time; here used to describe the shape and size.</note>, using either plain water or egg white. These, once dried on their own, are layered with charcoal in a wind furnace and, with a very strong fire, are calcined for 4 or 5 hours. When the furnace has cooled on its own, remove the cakes. But if they are not sufficiently "cooked" the first time—which is known if they receive little light—then repeat the calcination in the same way, layering the said cakes just as before. Sometimes this calcination is repeated three times. The best is that which is made from the most select stones: shiny, pure, and transparent. Thus, the stone, very finely ground in a reverberatory furnace <note>A furnace that reflects heat back onto the material.</note>, is baked until it easily receives light. From that powder, various small animals are shaped in little boxes, which glow wonderfully in the dark.*

The famous Licetus says in his <term>Lithologia <note>original: "Lith."; The study of rocks or a specific treatise on the subject.</note></term> chapter 33 that this stone can be calcined on its own to acquire its light-bearing power. The famous Montalbanus once showed me in Bologna the method of calcining that he had printed and published. Since I do not have it at hand, I cannot include it in this booklet. I myself, although I once burned and consumed much charcoal in Bologna while calcining this stone, could not prepare luminous stones from whole stones; rather, I found it necessary, following the mind of Poterius, to reduce them to dust. 

Therefore, I selected stones of the highest quality—namely, shiny, transparent, untainted by any impurities, heavy, and weighty, which when broken show internal flakes or thin layers, or rectangular crystalline squares. Or perhaps a piece of that kind of gypsum stone, which was of great mass and contained straight, parallel lines or grooves not directed toward any center. I crushed these stones in a clean mortar, and once reduced to dust, I poured pure water over them, washing the powder several times of all filth. From the moistened powder, I formed large or small cakes and dried them on blotting paper. 

I then placed the cakes dried in this way into a small furnace made of well-baked bricks, outdoors while the weather was clear. This done, I successfully completed the work using well-burnt charcoal of medium size, <term>layer upon layer <note>original: "strato (ut dicitur chemicè) super strato"; a standard alchemical term for alternating layers of material.</note></term> with the said cakes, in the manner of Poterius described above. When the furnace had cooled naturally, I removed the cakes; those that had been turned into <term>Phosphors <note>original: "Phosphoræ"; substances that emit light without heat.</note></term> by this first calcination I set aside for use. The rest I submitted to calcination once more,

SECTION II. CHAPTER VII.

It must be well noted that your little furnace, which you build for this work, as well as the coals, the stones, and everything else required for this business, must be pure and free from any foreign matter; otherwise, you will labor in vain and to no purpose. For just as these stones delight in the clearest and purest light of the Air <note>original: "Aurae"; the author refers to the "Aura," suggesting the stones have a natural affinity for refined atmospheric or celestial light</note>: so they are very easily fouled by the depravity of some other impure admixture, and are not rendered bright and clear. Therefore, I advise you to be most diligent concerning purity in this matter, and no less elsewhere.

## CHAPTER VII.

### *On the essence and proximate causes of the light-bearing Bologna Stone.*

Just as the essence of a hidden disease is not always drawn from a single diagnostic sign, but from many, and those sometimes diverse: so the <term>quiddity <note>original: "quidditas"; the fundamental nature or "what-ness" of a thing in scholastic philosophy.</note></term> of the Bologna stone is not undeservedly sought from the many things found around it, as if they were ingredients entering into its mixture. For it is so arranged in the mineral kingdom that not everything is seen to be born from itself: but rather to be produced from various natural things found near its creation.

Thus our Bologna Stone, since it is a mixed body composed of its elements and natural principles, as well as other foreign bodies—like other baser stones and most imperfect mixed natural bodies—it was therefore necessary to consider its birthplace, and what is chiefly found there that might enter into its substance and have something in common with it; from which our stone might acquire its composition, its mixture, and its immediate material. Indeed, because the material of the Stone is almost entirely of a gypsum-like nature, and gypsum-stone grows in the greatest quantity in its birthplace near Bologna: therefore the immediate and predominant material of our Stone will be gypsum-stone, which was discussed briefly above in Section II, Chapter 2. Furthermore, since in the Bologna stone there is a foul-smelling <term>sulfur <note>original symbol: 🜍; the alchemical principle of soul and flammability. In modern chemistry, barite is barium sulfate.</note></term>, which is to some extent fixed, and its birthplace supplies many sulfurous stones and <term>marcasites <note>A term then used for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrite or "fool's gold."</note></term> full of sulfur and <term>vitriol <note>A sulfate mineral, usually referring here to the acidic or salty crusts found on weathered minerals.</note></term>: therefore it is necessary that sulfur and vitriol also concur in the composition of our stone. Finally, a caustic power that stings the tongue indicates that the nature of a certain sharp and <term>arsenical <note>Likely referring to a corrosive or toxic mineral salt rather than pure arsenic.</note></term> salt is present in the stone. Even if this salt is not found pure by itself, without the admixture of some other thing: it will nevertheless be mixed with the clods and the earth, in the manner of <term>niter <note>original: "nitri"; saltpeter or potassium nitrate, often associated with "fiery" properties.</note></term> and every other salt. For niter

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>ON THE ESSENCE AND CAUSES OF THE BOLOGNA STONE, etc.</header>

...for sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>, arsenic, sandarac <note>original: "sandaracha"; a red arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, etc.—according to the illustrious Liceto <note>Fortunio Liceti (1577–1657), a famous Italian physician and philosopher who wrote extensively on the "litheosphorus" or Bologna Stone</note>—are found therein. Therefore, the <term>quiddity <note>original: "quidditas"; a scholastic term referring to the fundamental nature or "what-ness" of a thing</note></term> and definition of the *light-bearing Bologna Stone* shall be that it is a natural, fossilized <note>In the 17th century, "fossil" meant anything dug out of the earth, not necessarily organic remains</note>, mixed, and imperfect body. It consists of a proximate material that is mostly gypsum-like, with a smaller portion of sulfurous, arsenical, and sometimes vitriolic <note>sulfate-based</note> or sharp saline substances. It acquires its light-bearing power from the surrounding daylight air especially, and from fire—as well as other uses in medicine—after its own powerful <term>calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical properties</note></term>. Having thus established the definition of our stone, which we drew from its proximate material cause, we must briefly examine its efficient cause and its form. Setting aside the remote efficient cause, I shall touch upon at least the proximate one. And since the proximate efficient cause of any stone—which bestows the <term>substantial form <note>the essential nature that makes a thing what it is</note></term> upon the prepared matter—seems to be an older stone or a part of one falling into that matter and producing a similar form within it through its own substantial form: therefore, the *proximate efficient cause of our stone* appears to be an older stone, or a fragment of it, bestowing a <term>temperament <note>original: "temperamentum"; a specific balance or mixture of qualities</note></term> similar to its own upon the nearby matter (which already possesses the common dispositions from other external causes), and producing within that temperament the essential form of the same species. For just as any vegetable, when it reaches the peak of its growth and begins to diminish and die, deposits its seminal power into earth suited for its regeneration: so too the seminal power of stones seems to reside in older stones that are already aging. This power places a similar temperament, like a kind of seminal force, into suitable nearby matter, so that the essential form of the same species results from it.

*The substantial form, therefore, shall be the specific essential temperament of a perfect mixture, with a predominance of purer earth.*

Finally, the purpose of our stone, although it consists of the gathering of light to be returned in the darkness, another can also be given: namely, its *use in medical matters, in Chemistry, etc.*

# CHAPTER VIII.
## On the causes of light in the Bologna Stone, and on its medical use.

It is truly a difficult matter to determine the causes of the light-bearing virtue in our Bologna Stone and in other things that shine at night without any flame, since fire, light, and radiance seem to be something divine...

## SECTION II. CHAPTER VIII.

...to taste of something divine. For it pleased even the Creator of heaven and earth to clothe Himself in fire and to speak through it, and for His Angels to be represented at times by flames. I think, therefore, that I should speak briefly about such an exalted matter, so that, following the example of Daedalus, we may fly on the wings of our reasoning and remain in the middle of the air. Let us not, like Icarus, be raised too close to the **Gold of the Universe** <note>original: "Universi Aurum"; a reference to the Sun or the ultimate source of light and heat</note> and, being consumed by this **Sulfurous fire**, be cast down from there in a most fatal fall into the Ocean of ignorance. For this was the cause, as Ovid sings in the fourth Elegy of his third book of *Tristia*:

> Namely, because this one flew high, and that one lower:
> ->For neither of them possessed their own wings.<- <note>original: "Nempe quod hic altè, demissiùs ille volabat: Nam pennas ambo non habuere suas." Ovid uses this myth to warn against ambition and high-reaching curiosity.</note>

The illustrious Fortunio Liceti <note>A famous Italian physician and philosopher (1577–1657) who wrote a major work on the Bologna Stone</note>, from chapters 41 to 43 of his work on the *Lithophosphorus*, encounters the opposing opinions of the distinguished gentlemen Lagalla <note>Giulio Cesare Lagalla (1576–1624), a professor of philosophy in Rome</note> and Montalbani <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), a scientist from Bologna</note> regarding this matter, and strives to refute them. Of these men, the former [Lagalla] established that the light in our stone is a quality of a celestial and fiery substance, which is shared by all things that shine in the dark. The latter [Montalbani], however, argued that the **calcined stone** <term>calcination <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its properties</note></term> is ignited by the surrounding light, according to its own sulfurous and fiery effluvia <note>invisible particles or "breaths" flowing out of a substance</note> which emerge from the calcined stone; because these have a nature akin to fire and light, they easily catch fire and light from the surrounding light. Finally, the illustrious Liceti brings forward his own opinion: namely, that the first physical cause of light in our stone is vaporous air, illuminated within the pores of the calcined stone. I, as a seeker of truth, will neither subscribe to any one opinion, nor will I swear by the words of any single Master. Yet I will not oppose anyone for the sake of it, since very often disputes arise not about the things themselves, but only about words. I say briefly, however: *That Light, without a fiery subject and air, can never be perceived by the eyes.*

Therefore, three things must necessarily come together for light to occur, as causes to an effect. The first of these is a **fiery subject**, or heat lurking within the subject, as the essential cause of light inherent in that subject. The second is the **aura** <note>the surrounding air or atmosphere</note> proportioned to the heat in the subject, acting as a concurring cause that excites the light within the subject. The third is the **eye**, which is conformed to the light already existing, acting as an instrumental cause, and without which light cannot be perceived. Now, heat—depending on the quality of the subject—is either moist or dry. The latter flows out from its subject in fiery and sulfurous parts; the former flows out in **saline-nitrous** <note>containing salt-like and nitrate-like particles</note> particles. When heat, through a kind of existing decay, evaporates from a moist subject in these saline-nitrous particles, it is ignited by the vaporous air and the saline-nitrous particles within it, and it becomes a white light in the dark, just as is seen in rotting wood...

<summary>The author reviews 17th-century theories on why the Bologna Stone glows, citing scholars like Liceti and Montalbani, before proposing that light requires three components: internal heat, the surrounding air, and a functioning eye.</summary>

<page-num>219</page-num>
<header>ON THE CAUSES OF LIGHT IN THE BOLOGNA STONE AND ITS MEDICAL USE</header>

...appears in the fluids and sweats of animals. However, when dry heat exhales from its dry, sulfurous, and fiery subject, it is ignited by the dry air and the sulfurous particles existing in the air; thus a colored light is created, which adheres to its sulfurous subject, just as it shines back in our Bologna stones. Regarding the causes of the colors of light, one may look above at Section I, chapters 4 and 5.

According to the celebrated Balduin <note>Christian Adolph Balduin (1632–1682), a German alchemist who discovered a type of phosphorescent calcium nitrate</note>, there is Gold in the Breeze <note>original: "Aurum in Aura"; a phrase Balduin used to describe a "universal spirit" or his specific phosphorescent discovery</note>, and Sulfur in the Salt <note>original: ⊖, the alchemical symbol for salt</note>; there is light in fire, and light in air. This light in the fire is generated from the air, but in the air itself, it appears to be primordial and seminal. This air is like the purest crystal or a mirror, receiving all the forms of things as objects. Nor does this seem to oppose the Holy Scriptures. For since the Divine Breeze <note>original: "Divina Aura"; referring to the Spirit of God in Genesis</note> brooded upon the waters from the beginning of the world and cherished them, by His word, He first produced light and separated it from the darkness. This primordial light was not in the sun, as the sun did not yet exist; therefore it was necessary for it to have been in the breeze, or ether, without which no light exists, and indeed it still exists there now. From this primordial light, as if from some salt or a most pure, clear glass, the Almighty Creator by His word produced the Gold of the Breeze—our sun—which cherishes and fertilizes the earth with its heat. Therefore, the sun does not exist without the ether, nor does light exist without both. Thus, for light to shine upon us, it is necessary for the sun and the ether, fire and air, to come together. These are my brief thoughts on light and its causes in the Bologna stone.

Now, briefly concerning its use in medicine: the celebrated Liceti <note>Fortunio Liceti, author of a major 1640 work on the stone</note>, in chapter 23 of *The Light-Bearing Stone*, recounts that the calcined Bologna stone is a <term>psilothrum</term> <note>a depilatory or hair-remover</note> by itself, as is the lye made from it. Hence, it is caustic. Secondly, its smoke excites vomiting by whatever means it is applied. Thirdly, by its saltiness, it helps those suffering from <term>podagricos</term> <note>gout in the feet</note>. He mentions the same in chapter 22. Further medicinal uses for the Bologna Stone are not recounted by the aforementioned or other authors.

---

# EPILOGUE.

And so, we have compared our Bologna stone with the Hermetic Phosphorus, and we have proved that it agrees with it in the following ways:

1. That both attract light from the sun's light and the daylight.
2. That both similarly borrow splendor from a hearth fire and the flame of a torch, and cause that splendor to shine in the darkness.
3. That both, when enclosed in glass, acquire the same light-bearing quality.
4. That both scatter a small amount of light from themselves over a short distance, and to some extent illuminate their own substance and the adjacent body.
5. That both, when enclosed, lose their acquired light again after a short delay, and they regain it as often as they are moved again either to the public light of day, or private light within buildings, or to our fire.
6. That for the most part, like glowing iron or coal, in co-

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of light within the Bologna Stone. While the previous section discussed the "Hermetic Phosphorus" and the "Gold in the Breeze," this chapter transitions into the physical and philosophical debates between contemporary scholars like Licetus and Montalbani regarding the stone's inner fire.</meta>

### SECTION II. CHAPTER VIII.

...savors of something divine. For it pleased the Creator of heaven and earth Himself at times to clothe Himself in fire and to speak through it, and to have His Angels represented by flames. I believe, therefore, that a few words must be said about such a sublime matter so that, following the example of Daedalus, flying on the wings of our reasoning, we may remain in the middle air; lest like Icarus we be lifted too high toward the <term>Gold of the Universe</term> <note>Likely referring to the Sun or the alchemical "Universal Spirit"</note> and, scorched by this <term>Sulfurous fire</term>, be cast down by a most mournful fall into the Ocean of Ignorance. For this was the cause, as <note>Ovid (43 BC – 17 AD), the Roman poet</note> *Ovid* sings in Book 3 of the *Tristia*, Elegy 4:

> *Truly, because the one flew high, and the other lower;*
> *For neither of them possessed their own wings.* <note>original: "Nempe quod hic altè, demissiùs ille volabat: Nam pennas ambo non habuere suas."</note>

The famous *Fortunius Licetus* <note>An Italian philosopher and scientist (1577–1657) who wrote extensively on phosphorescence</note> in chapters 41 through 43 of his work *On Lithophosphorus*, strives to oppose and refute the opinions of the distinguished men *V. V. Lagella* and *Montalbani* <note>Ovidio Montalbani (1601–1671), a Bolognese scientist who studied the stone</note> regarding this matter. One of them [Lagella] posits that the light in our stone is a quality of celestial and fiery substance shared by all things that shine in the dark. The other [Montalbani] suggests that the <term>calcined stone</term> <note>Stone that has been heated to a high temperature to change its properties</note> is ignited by the surrounding light, following its own sulfurous and fiery effluvia which exit the calcined stone; because of their nature being akin to fire and light, they easily catch fire and light from the surrounding illumination. Finally, the distinguished *Licetus* offers his own opinion: namely, that the primary physical cause of light in our stone is <term>vaporous air</term>, illuminated within the pores of the calcined stone. 

I, as a seeker of truth, will neither subscribe to any one opinion nor swear by the words of any master. Yet I will not oppose anyone, since disputes often arise not about the things themselves, but only about words. I say briefly: *That Light can never be perceived by the eyes without a fiery subject and air.*

Three things, therefore, must necessarily come together for light, like causes for an effect. The first of these is the **fiery subject**, or the heat lying hidden in the subject, as the essential cause of light inherent in the subject. The second is the **breeze** <note>original: "aura"</note> proportioned to the heat in the subject, as a concurring cause that excites the light in the subject. The third is the **eye**, which is conformable to the light already existing, as an instrumental cause, and without which light cannot be perceived. Now, heat—depending on the quality of the subject—is either wet or dry. The latter flows from its subject through fiery and sulfurous parts; the former through <term>saline-nitrous</term> <note>A term referring to salts or niter (saltpeter), thought to be essential for phosphorescence and life</note> particles. When heat, existing through a certain putrefaction in saline-nitrous particles, evaporates from a wet subject, it is ignited by the vaporous air and the saline-nitrous particles within it, and it becomes a white light in the dark, just as in putrid wood

<meta>catchword: dis</meta>

<summary>This page explores the theoretical causes of the Bologna Stone's light, contrasting the views of Fortunius Licetus and Ovidio Montalbani while asserting that light requires a fiery subject, air, and a perceiving eye.</summary>

# EPILOGUE.

...shine with a golden-red or cinnabar-like color. 7. That the **Bologna Stone**, regarding its internal form—as figure 2, number 2 shows—does not differ much in its external appearance from the *Universal Magnet* of the famous <note>Christian Adolph Balduin (1632–1682), an alchemist known for discovering a phosphorescent substance</note> Balduin on page 41, from which the *Hermetic Phosphorus* <note>The "light-bringer" of Hermes; a self-luminous alchemical preparation</note> emerged. 8. That our **Bologna Stone** can by no means shine in the dark if it is contaminated with much impure sulfur; but that it is rendered all the more brilliant and clear the more it is of a purer nature, and that it easily acquires a stain from any adjacent impure material, causing it to shine less. In the same way, the *Hermetic Phosphorus*, having emerged from the pure breeze, must necessarily be extremely pure in its nature to be such a potent "Light-Bearer" <term>Phosphorus</term> <note>From the Greek 'phosphoros', meaning 'bringing light'</note>.

We have also held a brief discourse on the Bologna stone specifically, and we have brought forward what seemed most necessary regarding its generation, nature, and causes. Its **medicinal use** is meager; its **moral use**, however, is ample. For this stone seems to warn the true sons of <term>Hermes</term> <note>The mythical founder of alchemy</note> and <term>Aesculapius</term> <note>The Greek god of medicine</note> that they should not be overly lavish with their "weapons" <note>original: *armorum*; meaning their secret tools and knowledge</note> before the common folk, yet they should remain without envy toward one another. Instead, they should be *Inquirers of Nature* <note>original: *Curiosi Naturae*; a reference to the 'Academia Naturae Curiosorum', one of the oldest scientific societies</note> and strive daily to investigate its secrets rather than profaning them, "lest they hand swords to the mad" <note>A metaphorical warning against giving powerful knowledge to those who would misuse it</note>. In this way, they will daily instruct themselves with medical wisdom—by which they surpass the pretenders in the art—and they will protect both the honor of God the Most High (which ought to be the primary goal of human life) and their own authority.

But that pure, sincere, white, glowing stone of ours—transparent, illustrious, and clear, without any foul and impure sulfur—and likewise that almost incomparable *Hermetic Phosphorus*, present to us mortals most brilliant faces with their clearest light, and offer a remarkable use: namely, if we wish to conceive within ourselves the divine fire, by which we might shine in these darknesses with virtues and good works toward one another. To do so, we must necessarily be made pure, sincere, and white, polluted by no foul sulfur of vices. Indeed, if we imitate the light-bearing qualities of our glowing stone and the *Hermetic Phosphorus* so that we light the way for our neighbor with all love, we shall see light in the Divine light, and we shall radiate like stars among pious minds and pure souls.

->I have spoken sincerely and candidly.<-
